Katalog Berita (Halaman 24/57)

#6690. The Midi Bob Is the Sleek, High-Shine Haircut That Flatters 50-Plus Hair

This elegant style will be everywhere this fall.

Finding a hairstyle more sought-after than a well-cut midi bob (a long bob that brushes the shoulders or collarbones but delivers a blunt, full effect) is no small feat. Favored by celebrities such as Pamela Anderson, Halle Berry, Hailey Bieber, Kaia Gerber, and Julianne Moore, this shoulder-grazing style has become something of a signature among A-listers in recent years. Coincidentally, it is also one of the most flattering cuts for anyone over 50.

Most recently, Claudia Winkleman has brought the midi bob back into the spotlight, as her bold signature hairstyle continues to make headlines as the Celebrity Traitors hits screens.

As the name suggests, the lob offers a middle ground between long hair and a bob. It falls anywhere between the chin and the collarbone and is often seen as a transitional style for those looking to go shorter gradually.

“Hitting just at the collarbone, the lob offers the best of both worlds: the ease of a bob with the styling flexibility of longer hair,” says Elliot Bute, hairstylist at Hershesons Fitzrovia. “It’s incredibly versatile, particularly for anyone who wants the ease of a shorter haircut but still wants to be able to tie their hair back.”

He adds that the cut works across most face shapes because you can adjust where the length sits and where you introduce layers or movement. Take Julianne Moore, for example. Her blunt cut creates glossy fullness in her natural hair, while fellow actor Winona Ryder has adopted the style in a more undone, choppy way.

Remember, depending on your lob style, your styling routine will look different, as Tim Scott-Wright, owner of Tim Scott-Wright at The Hair Surgery, explains here.

“The lob is a strong, on-trend look that is great with a wave,” he says. “It’s kind of an in-between look and is best worn wavy with texture. It’s a way of modernizing lacklustre long hair without losing all your length. Cate Blanchett and Reese Witherspoon have both recently sported this look.”

Consider investing in a texture spray or air-dry cream if your hair has natural shape and movement, or use a curling tong or straightener to create soft, natural bevels.

#6686. Earthy Neutral Jeans Make Cold-Weather Outfits Feel Instantly Warmer

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

A richer alternative to classic blue denim, from cocoa wide-legs to sage straight-legs.

Listen, we love classic blue denim, but as fashion obsessives, we also believe in changing things up. If you want to drift away from black and indigo this season, consider jeans in earthy neutrals to bring a fresh outlook to your fall and winter wardrobe. The palette runs wide: chocolaty browns, soft sands, light stone blues, olive, rust, sage, and even dusty pink.

In addition to their warmth, what we love about these shades is how easily they work. Think of them as neutrals with more personality: a pair of cocoa wide-legs looks sharp with a cream turtleneck and loafers, while sage straight-legs soften an oversize charcoal blazer. Look for a rich, slightly variable dye and a sturdy cotton twill that will hold its shape and fade gracefully, then style them as you would your favorite blues, with a little more intention and a lot more warmth.

Below, we’ve rounded up some of the best earth-toned jeans to pair with your favorite fall/winter pieces.

These jeans in a cool-toned brown called “Washed Canteen Clean” pair a highly versatile earth-toned shade with a flared hem. Wear them with your favorite purple-toned knit and black ankle boots for a look that feels moody, polished, and effortlessly autumnal.

This relaxed straight-leg silhouette has a vintage-inspired shape that feels thoroughly modern, done in a light, marbled khaki wash.

Along with a wide-leg silhouette, this pair in the shade Great Basin, a hue reminiscent of muddy waters, brings a rich, moody depth to your denim rotation. Style them with a cobalt cable-knit and tan suede boots for a tonal look that feels on-trend and a little rugged.

Drawstring toggles at both hems add a subtle, unexpected sporty detail (hence the name) to a relaxed jean. Team them with your favorite court sneakers for an elevated athletic-leaning look.

In the shade “Tobacco,” these jeans are built to last a lifetime. The wide-leg fit and timeless color will earn a spot in your rotation all year long.

Barrel-leg jeans continue to be popular this season, yet this soft sepia shade trades the trend’s usual denim blue for a softer, warmer, more grounded take. Pair them with a fitted black bodysuit and pointed-toe boots to balance the roomy shape with something sleek.

Something about this shape and earthy shade brings a sense of calm and ease to our hectic everyday lives. It’s the perfect jean for slipping into a relaxed, weekend mindset.

Made of soft-stretch denim, this brown jean features a high-waisted, straight-leg shape that flatters without feeling restrictive. Style it with a tucked-in white tee, a shearling coat, and loafers for a polished, easy outfit that works from the office to the weekend.

A barrel leg in a vintage-inspired brown wash gives these jeans a lived-in, worn-in feel from the very first wear. Style them with a slim ribbed tank, a cropped leather jacket, and sling-back flats to keep the roomy shape looking sharp.

When you think of an everyday jean, perhaps the color army green doesn’t immediately come to mind. But once you see how versatile this shade can be, it’s determined to win a permanent spot in your rotation.

This slim palazzo jean brings ’70s vibes and softness to a fall color palette. Wear it with a rust-colored turtleneck, a tan suede jacket, and platform boots for a retro-leaning outfit that feels warm, confident, and distinctly seasonal.

#6682. The 11 Best Fragrances Released This October

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The new smoky, spicy scents you need to know about.

I love fragrance. I love fall. Put the two together, and I’m in olfactory heaven. I’ve been excited to put this roundup together since the first new spooky scents started hitting shelves early last month—as we get into November, it’s evident that October will be a tough one to follow up.

There’s plenty of spice and smoke in the air, plus rich gourmands that feel tailor-made for colder weather. There’s even a new fragrance collection in partnership with Robert Eggers’s latest gothic horror film, for anyone who’s ever wanted to smell like they could turn into a werewolf at any moment—or, well, just want to smell like they’ve been at a bonfire. Ready to find your next fall scent? Read on for 11 exciting new fragrances you can get now.

If you’re looking for a new go-to fragrance for fall and winter, let it be Commodity Spice. Like a hot cup of chai, this expressive fragrance is warm and smooth with a rich cocktail of spices including saffron, nutmeg, clove, cardamom, and cinnamon. If I close my eyes, I feel like I just stepped into a cozy little tea and spices shop, and I want to wear that feeling like a sweater.

When Fulton & Roark released Cathedral of Ruins, it quickly became one of our favorite cold-weather smoky scents. Not only does it have a super unique aroma of smoldering frankincense, black pepper, rose, and woods, but it has a heartbreakingly beautiful origin story—the brand’s founders were inspired to create the scent after visiting the abandoned City Methodist Church in Gary, Indiana. The fragrance is an homage to the religious rituals that once took place in that church.

Think of a buttery croissant; a butterscotch candy; a cinnamon roll with sweet, creamy frosting. Those are the vibes of Gap’s newest fragrance. A late addition to the newly (re)launched collection of EDPs, it joins the group as the first gourmand—just in time for fall. Oh, and if you like how Bliss smells, you can complete the collection with the scented hand cream and body lotion.

I was always under the impression that fruity fragrances and woody ones had very little to do with each other and that, for the most part, they should stay separate. That’s until I smelled Maison d’Etto’s Pinehurst. Here, fruity notes of rambutan, blood orange, and ripe pineapple provide a beautiful overlay to base notes of leather, oud, and other woods. It’s a radiant scent that works year-round, day and night.

David Moltz (the “D.S.” of D.S. & Durga) was inspired to create this Resurrected Corpse Flower fragrance after visiting the Brooklyn Botanical Garden during the height of a rare blooming of a corpse flower. What he smelled was, in his words, “like a baby diaper pail in a rotten fish tank full of rubber lily bulbs & olives.” But then he got to thinking, what if that scent could be cleaned of all its rotten odors and reborn into something beautiful? Thus, Resurrected Corpse Flower was born. With green olive, lily, tuberose, freesia, and deep bog cedar, it’s a truly special scent that’s at once floral and swampy.

Because this fragrance is so unlike anything I’ve ever smelled before, it might be a bit polarizing; I’m sure some of you will love it, and others might prefer one of D.S. & Durga’s other unique fragrances. But after leaving this scent on my skin for a bit, I really started to appreciate it.

In contrast to some of the deep, rich gourmands on this list, Lumira’s Soléor offers a lighter, sun-soaked gourmand option. Instead of creamy sweetness, it’s more of a soft, sensual sweetness that isn’t too heavy or overpowering. Smooth amber and candied fruit up top give it a bit of a citrusy kick, which fades into caramel, vanilla, and woods. It works for fall and winter just as well as it does for spring and summer.

If you do want something rich and creamy, Phlur’s Heavy Cream is the way to go. It’s an ambery gourmand with notes of vanilla mousse, white orchid, and creamy musk, which are balanced out by hints of sparkling lemon and orange flower, adding a little bit of lightness to the otherwise heavy fragrance. And, of course, with a name like Heavy Cream, there has to be a coordinating whipped body butter for the full experience.

How do you make decadence refined? You create a rich, vanilla gourmand and add the sophisticated warmth of Peru balsam. It’s definitely a sweet scent, and the combination of Cristal vanilla, bourbon vanilla, tonka bean, and black sugar creates the aroma of a classic crème brûlée. But once you get past the creamy sweetness of it all, you’re left with a sensual, balsamic scent that lingers.

I didn’t know a fragrance could smell so much like Halloween, but Heretic teamed up with Robert Eggers’ new movie Werwulf and did just that. At first, you get spice and smoke from cardamom, smoke, and leather; it then moves into something woody, earthy, and animalic with fir balsam, labdanum, patchouli, moss, and damp earth. The “suggestion of wet fur” is what really makes this scent stand out—and what gives it that spooky edge. Wear with your scariest Halloween costume.

In a departure from the mostly spicy, rich, gourmand scents that hit shelves this October, Drake’s Better World Fragrance House partnered with October’s Very Own for a fresh, citrusy fragrance that gives the impression of a refreshing white wine sangria with notes of passion fruit, rose, white cedarwood, and roasted tonka bean. But in its spherical burgundy glass bottle, it’ll blend in perfectly with your other fall and winter scents.

Roll-on fragrance oils are all the rage right now, and new fragrance house Gamine has already come out with its own collection of fragrance oils called Microdose. With six parfum oils, you can wear each individually or combine two or three for your own unique scent. My favorite of the bunch is DMT, a metallic fragrance built on notes of burnt latex, aerated foam, and hot steel. I wasn’t sure I would like it based on the description—and the fact that I’d never heard of a metallic scent before—but I’m actually obsessed with it.

#6680. Dia Art Foundation and Chanel Celebrate Steve McQueen and Roni Horn

Sofia Coppola, Julio Torres, and Marion Cotillard also attended the gala.

As co-chairs of Dia Fall Night 2026, Carrie Mae Weems, Rosemarie Trockel, and Matthieu Blazy hosted an amazing roster of guests to celebrate the Dia Art Foundation’s initiatives. Sofia Coppola, Marion Cotillard, Julio Torres, and artists Chase Hall, Marina Abramović, Garrett Bradley, and Cecily Brown were among those who turned out for the Chanel-sponsored event, which honored artists Steve McQueen and Roni Horn. Both McQueen and Horn have created installations shown at Dia Beacon, Dia’s most well-known exhibition space. It also served as a goodbye to Dia director Jessica Morgan, who will leave the organization after over a decade to serve as director of the Tate.

The evening began at Dia’s Chelsea galleries, where the show “David Lamelas: The Machine” was on display. Guests then made their way north a few blocks for cocktails and dinner. During the meal, artist Matthew Barney introduced Horn, his longtime friend, and reflected on a memory of explaining her work to his young daughter. In tribute to McQueen, musician and poet Meshell Ndegeocello made her way around the room performing a hymn and a striking spoken-word piece. (She collaborated with McQueen on the sound component of his 2024 Dia Beacon work “Bass.”) She was accompanied by singers Jade Hicks and Marsha Deboe and guitarist Chris Bruce.

Chanel and Blazy’s partnership with Dia is part of the brand’s continued support of arts organizations, which includes working with museums like the National Portrait Gallery in London and the Centre Pompidou in Paris.

Check out some of the starry art world attendees below.

Adrienne Gaffney is a features editor at ELLE and previously worked at WSJ Magazine and Vanity Fair.

#6670. Skyler Gisondo on His New Fashion Campaign—and Dressing Like Harrison Ford in the ’90s

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Primetime star is gearing up in Bonobos and getting ready for a very big 2027.

Skyler Gisondo is remarkably easy to talk to. If you’ve seen him on one of your various screens—whether as Decoy Dan from Primetime, Jimmy Olsen from Superman, or maybe just that meme of him from a decade-old red-carpet photo—you probably assumed as much. It’s the big smile, the friendly eyes, the freckles, the general sense of wholesomeness. But I can confirm it, because even though I know we’ve got a relatively brief window of time to discuss his new campaign with men’s fashion brand Bonobos over Zoom, we spend the first few minutes of our chat just … shooting the shit. It feels like catching up with a buddy you haven’t seen in a while. Relaxed. Natural.

Thankfully, the vibe continues as we start digging into matters of style. Gisondo tells me he’s always been interested in dressing well but hasn’t always known where to start. “I would see a cool dude walking down the street or something, and think, ‘Man, I want to look like that guy.’ But I never knew how. I always felt lost,” he says. He describes his 20s as a decade of “desperately trying on shoes that didn’t fit or something.”

He turned 30 recently, though, and “I really feel like over the last couple of years, I’ve homed in on my sense of style, what I feel good and confident in.” A very good time, then, to star in a fashion campaign—especially considering this seems like a breakout moment for the actor. He’s been working consistently since he was a kid, but his performance in Primetime as a young actor who gradually realizes just how toxic the entertainment industry can be has clearly launched him to another level. And he still has his upcoming role in Focker-in-Law this November and a couple more appearances as Jimmy Olsen (in Man of Tomorrow on the big screen and The People v. Gorilla Grodd on television) on the docket for 2027.

Over the course of our conversation, we touch on everything from dressing like a cowboy (a mistake) to using Harrison Ford in the ’90s as your style North Star (a very good idea). We also chat about the stuff he’s stolen from sets for his own wardrobe, the psychological validation of joining the Shrek franchise, and why, after Primetime, we can expect more creative big swings in the future. Read on for a few highlights.

This interview has been condensed and edited.

“It’s ultimately about your own confidence. I feel like the sweet spot for fashion is when what you look like reflects how you feel inside—when you get to this equilibrium and it allows you to be a more fully realized version of yourself. We all grow and our sense of fashion evolves with us but ultimately like I feel like there should be some current that runs through all of it. The stuff I’m drawn to is the more timeless, classic stuff. I look at Harrison Ford in the ’90s and I’m just like, it doesn’t get cooler, you know?”

“For a while, for whatever reason, me and every actor I know were like trying to look like a cowboy. And it just hit me one day: ‘None of us have ever worked a day in our lives on a cattle ranch, man.’ I don’t even know how to use a hammer! Like, what are we doing? Can we just be ourselves?”

“I’m pretty active. I surf and hike a lot when I’m at home. And I love walking; if I have an hour or two between meetings, I’ll just walk a few miles and take calls. I want something comfortable and functional, and I find a lot of vintage workwear just hits that and fits me well. And the story is so compelling to me. The idea of ‘Who’s worn this? What were they getting up to?’ ”

“What drew me to the campaign and to the clothes is that there’s a kind of timelessness to that style. My mom thought I looked amazing in the photos. My girlfriend too. My grandma and my sisters thought I looked great. It’s this very accessible and sort of classically cool thing they have going on. And I felt like me. I felt confident—so much so that I stole several items from the set.”

“With shows and movies, the fear is that it’s a one-of-one item that you might need back for another season. But that’s when you hit them with the ‘pretty, pretty please?’ You say, ‘I swear to God, I’ll guard it with my life.’ And they say, ‘Okay, but you can’t tell anybody.’ With a campaign, it’s probably easier because they’re excited for you to have it and represent. But probably 80 percent of my closet is basically just clothes stolen from characters and sets. It’s fun because you get to try on a bunch of versions of yourself and learn what you like.

“For instance, with Jimmy Olsen in the first Superman movie, they did a fantastic job with this retro-future thing. And then in The People v. Gorilla Grodd, they went a little more vintage-y in a way that felt more like me, Skyler, personally. I tried to steal almost the entire closet.”

“Well, right now I’m doing press for Primetime and Focker-in-Law. I’m doing another movie for A24 called Goblin that I’m not sure how much I can say about; I really don’t want to get fired. But I’m so excited. It’s the most insane script I think I have ever read, and it’s definitely a big swing. We have Man of Tomorrow coming out in the summer, and then Shrek 5. The number of texts I got when I got Shrek … ‘You made it, man! Oh my God! I had no idea you made it!’ I was like, ‘Thank you. Yeah, baby! Shrek 5!’

“But ultimately, it’s a very exciting moment because it’s the first time I have people asking, ‘What do you want to do?’ And I’m like, ‘Damn! I never really thought about that!’ I feel like Primetime lit a fire under me in a real way. And even though I’ve kind of been doing this my whole life, I feel a sense of drive that I’ve never felt really in this way before. So, yeah, man, I’m excited.”

#6669. Meet Melat Kiros, the 29-Year-Old Bringing Gen Z Politics to Congress

After losing her law-firm job over her stance on Gaza protests, she turned generational frustration into a historic win.

In 2023, Melat Kiros was fired. She was 26 and worked at a major New York law firm, but she refused to condemn an open letter addressed to dozens of law firms that had urged law school deans to crack down on antisemitism on university campuses, at a time when students were protesting the slaughter in Gaza. Kiros condemned antisemitism, but warned law firms, including the one she worked for, against confusing “bigotry with the geopolitical question of Israel’s legitimacy.” This cost her her job.

She, however, was not discouraged, as she belongs to a generation that is no longer willing to set aside its values for the sake of a career. “The right to question not only the legitimacy of a government but also its actions is the bedrock principle of a true democracy,” she said of her decision. Since then, Kiros has devoted herself more intensely to politics, and yesterday, in the midterm elections, she won as Colorado’s representative to the House of Representatives. Before her, only one other Gen Z representative, Democrat Maxwell Frost of Florida, had made it to Parliament in 2022, but she became the first Gen Z woman in Congress.

Melat Kiros was born in Addis Ababa, Ethiopia, but moved to Denver with her family when she was still a baby and grew up there. She graduated from Washington College in Maryland in 2018, where she studied political science and economics, before earning a law degree from the University of Notre Dame in 2022. Today she is a lawyer and is pursuing a doctorate in public policy. In the meantime, however, she also works as a barista. It is not easy to balance a political career with her studies, but it is worth it.

After being fired, Kiros returned to Colorado and, in 2024, joined the election campaign of Democratic congressional candidate John Padora as communications director. It was excellent training for her, so much so that, two years later, she decided to run for Congress herself. On her side, she has strong political positions—she supports universal health care and child care, but also more politically controversial issues, such as abolishing Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) and ending U.S. military aid to Israel. Her ideals openly align with her actions, and in fact during the election campaign she refused funding from corporate PACs and pro-Israel groups, while also sharply criticizing her Democratic primary opponent Diana DeGette, who had instead accepted donations from defense contractors as well as energy and pharmaceutical companies.

It is clear that Kiros's mark is different: She shows us what Gen Z can and will be able to do when it is given space. In this sense, her election also fits well into the climate of student protests, in Europe and around the world, by a generation that wants to make its voice heard. Yet it is still not easy: According to research by Future Caucus, more than 100 Generation Z candidates ran in the 2026 midterm elections for Congress, but the study found that young candidates were less likely to make it to the general election than other age groups, with only 24 of them appearing on the November ballots.

Kiros also had to face paternalism and dismissive attitudes from those who considered her unsuitable and inexperienced. “Now is not the time to gamble and send somebody with no experience to Washington,” DeGette had said during the primaries. Kiros, however, turned it into a strength, centering her congressional candidacy precisely on the theme of generational change. She emphasized socialist issues dear to younger people, such as affordability, social policies, climate change, countering the influence of multinational corporations, and support for students, families, and workers.

“People, I think, were really eager to see someone who is younger, who has lived experience with the economy that we're living through right now, the climate crisis that we are going to have to deal with the consequences of,” she said in an interview. For her, “socialism is about making it clear that we have basic needs for every single human being,” and she has promised to fight for this.

She also knows she has a lot to learn, but that does not stop her from walking through the doors of Congress with her head held high, without being overly intimidated by traditions and alliances. “Young people understand that we are in the fight of our lives,” she said. “We need to have leaders that are going to be fighting for the kind of bold policies that actually get something done, and they’re refusing to settle or wait for our turn or ask for permission.”

#6655. Why El Clasico Is the Best Grudge Match in All of Sports

Last Saturday, before the biggest grudge match in international soccer, Spanish police officers decked out in riot gear surrounded my Barcelona hotel. Blue lights bounced from the sun-stained stone of the alley as kids scrambled away in the distance. In the skirmish, one had lost a single ASICS sneaker that sat idly in the road until, sometime between night and the light of day, someone snatched it up for themselves. You often hear of the way heated football rivalries and cup finals bring out a kind of mob mentality among fans. The match, however, wasn’t set to kick off for another 24 hours.

Three days prior I’d landed in Barcelona to attend FC Barcelona versus Real Madrid, better known as El Clásico, for which Tyler the Creator’s GOLF brand had partnered with Spotify on Barcelona’s gameday kits. If you care about football in the slightest you know about El Clásico, though it’s worth knowing about even if you don’t.

Since 1902—the year of Real Madrid’s founding, and three years after that of FC Barcelona—the two biggest football clubs in Spain have clashed in a rivalry match dubbed El Clásico (the face-off now happens twice a season, plus various tournament play that varies in schedule year to year). Before last week’s installment, the match had been played 264 times over 122 years. In most high-stakes football rivalries, one club holds a historical edge: Arsenal has a handy 20 to 30 wins over Tottenham Hotspur in the North London Derby, depending on when you start keeping track, while Manchester United edges out Liverpool in total wins. But heading into the first El Clásico of the 2026-27 La Liga season, Barcelona and Real Madrid stood deadlocked with 106 wins each.

American sports rivalries are often the result of proximity, with most pitting divisional neighbors and crosstown rivals against one another, with the exception of teams that share a bitter history of championship bouts (like the Lakers and the Celtics). But if we’re being honest, not even Yankees-Red Sox holds a candle to El Clásico. Apart from both competing in the upper echelon of La Liga’s clubs, FC Barcelona and Real Madrid’s rivalry has stewed in over a century of political turmoil between the Catalan capital and the city that houses the Spanish throne. During the 20th century as Spain looked to enforce a uniform identity across the nation, Catalonia sought to maintain its independence from the crown and, crucially, its right to speak Catalan rather than Spanish. Francisco Franco’s dictatorship brought more enmity, as Madrid became the proxy club for a centralized Spain while Barcelona came to represent Catalonia’s Republican Left. During the Franco years, when both the language and the flag of Catalan were banned, Barcelona’s Camp Nou stadium became a safe haven for the free expression of Catalan identity. Meanwhile, Franco’s foreign minister Fernando Maria de Castiella declared Real Madrid “the best embassy we’ve ever had.” The wounds of that era have hardly subsided: while the war may be over, as recently as 2017 the Catalan people voted on whether or not they’d remain a part of Spain (the vote was declared illegal by the Spanish government, and there was police intervention at polling stations).

Today, the clubs are recognized as polar opposites in every sense, from politics to style of play: Real Madrid stands for conservatism and a unified Spain, while Barcelona remains the club of the rebellious Left. Madrid takes an Evil Empire approach to roster construction, buying up the most exciting free agents on the market for record-breaking fees. (This century alone they’ve rostered Cristiano Ronaldo, David Beckham, and Zinedine Zidane, and the club currently boasts a striker line that includes Jude Bellingham, Kylian Mbappé, and Vinicius Jr.) Barcelona, on the other hand, has the most acclaimed and successful youth academy in all of football. The school, La Masia, has promoted a singular theory of football for decades, prioritizing position play and a dominant possession game perfected by FC Barcelona veterans like Lionel Messi, Xavi, Pep Guardiola, and Andrés Iniesta. When Spain won the World Cup this summer, they did so with eight former students of La Masia on the pitch (one of whom, Marc Cucarella, currently plays for Real Madrid).

To this day, the rivalry is characterized by an unmistakable hostility. In my four days in Barcelona, I didn’t see one person wearing Real Madrid’s crest, though the souvenir shops lining La Rambla were well-stocked with the club’s bootleg gear. At one point, I walked past a wall scrawled with graffiti: “SPANISH GO HOME.” When we finally got to the match, most Madrid fans were sequestered to a supporter section in the upper deck surrounded by protective plexiglass.

“This is the biggest match in club football,” explained Joel Jaggar, a British transplant who’s lived in Barcelona for a decade. Jaggar runs Real Vintage Football, a rare jersey boutique in El Raval (just outside of the city’s Gothic Quarter) that was unsurprisingly overwhelmed with visitors all weekend. He’d stocked up on at least a hundred Messi jerseys, all of which were gone by the time they closed on Friday. “A lot of people come to Barcelona specifically for this game,” he shared. “Big fans, sure, but also people who want to experience the game for the sake of the experience.” Globally, he argued, the word “Barcelona” is as closely associated with the club as it is the city. “You can’t say the same for Madrid,” he quipped.

Jaggar and I met the morning after a swell of student-organized protests regarding the city’s cost-of-living crisis boiled over into vandalism and looting near the neighborhood that houses his store. There was even suspicion El Clásico would have to be postponed or cancelled altogether. Come morning though, those anxieties had faded–as is so often the case, football continued uninterrupted.

Even with capacity capped at around 62,000 during ongoing renovations, Camp Nou is something to behold. Ahead of kickoff, supporters lifted color-coordinated pieces of poster nestled into each seat,forming a massive, stadium-wide tifo in the colors of the club and, in our section, the Catalonian flag. Fireworks exploded, and El Clásico began.

But much of the match’s first half was mired in tension, as Barcelona dominated possession but failed to score. The crowd finally began to pop when Madrid nabbed their first goal, then properly exploded when Barca tied it up in the second half. When Mbappé scored his second goal of the evening at ‘87 minutes after a beautiful assist from Bellingham, he vaulted over to the gate of the Barcelona supporters’ section, taunting them as trash thrown from the crowd rained over him. Madrid held on, gaining the tiniest of advantages in the El Clásico win count: 107 to their rivals’ 106.

As we walked out of Camp Nou, which will hold a whopping 105,000 spectators when its renovation is complete, I thought about the sort of sporting events that tend to grace bucket lists: Super Bowls and World Cup Finals and World Series games. So often the point is the spectacle of a season or tournament ending—the big trophy, the championship ring, the confetti and fireworks. El Clásico is different. There’s no trophy at the end, no ultimate resolution. Like the ideological divide for which it’s become a proxy, El Clásico is in a constant state of ebb and flow. Each time Barcelona and Real Madrid take the pitch, there’s no decisive victory so much as another brick laid in the foundation of football’s greatest rivalry. These clubs have come to blows 265 times. But as long as there are football stadiums in Barcelona and Madrid, El Clásico will endure as a conduit for the enduring rancor between the country's two biggest cities.

#6650. Reggie Dinkins Is Still the Funniest Show on TV

Tracy Morgan and Daniel Radcliffe return for season 2 of their hilarious NBC sitcom somehow even wittier than before.

It’s a gross exaggeration to say that television is a lost art, but we can maybe agree that it has changed. Big-time. But sadly, not all of it is for the better. The binge model pigeonholes every show into a novel, scriptwriters tragically factor in second-screen doomscrolling, and the half-hour situational comedy has been driven to near extinction. Which is why I thank the television gods for The Fall and Rise of Reggie Dinkins and then thank them again that it’s back so soon.

Still sharp, still absurd, and still just plain funny, The Fall and Rise of Reggie Dinkins proves sophomores aren’t always in a slump, with a new season that picks up from season 1’s high point. “More of the same” isn’t bad news here, and if the series can keep pace, we might have a proper TV MVP when other sitcoms can’t make the cut.

Season 2 of Reggie Dinkins, which premiered November 2 on NBC, catches up with Daniel Radcliffe’s anguished auteur, Arthur Tobin, as he documents the show’s hero, Reggie Dinkins (Tracy Morgan). Dinkins is a disgraced football superstar, and he’s hell-bent on rehabilitating his public profile. Arthur still lives in the Dinkins’ crowded New Jersey mansion that also houses Reggie’s levelheaded ex-wife and manager Monica (Erika Alexander), their son Carmelo (Jalyn Hall), Reggie’s younger fiancée Brina (Precious Way), and Bobby Moynihan as Rusty, Reggie’s freeloading BFF who is too integral to the family’s cohesion to kick to the curb.

This season, Arthur grows ever closer to the Dinkins clan, despite his own hyperawareness of his documentary ethics violations. (Episode 2 of this season at last interrogates how compromised the in-progress project has been since the start.) Most of the ensemble, Radcliffe and Morgan included, exists in a sort of stasis—this is a network sitcom, after all—but it’s Precious Way who shines as the much-improved standout of the bunch.

Formerly “just” the younger woman in Reggie’s life last season, Brina has grown into the soul of the show. Along with Way’s buoyant charisma, there’s a newfound depth to Brina that wasn’t present in her before. I’ll admit, I thought she was grating at first blush, and I was ready to write her off as a shallow antagonist who didn’t seem involved with Reggie or the family in earnest. But in season 2, she’s evolved into a phenomenal supporting player who instantly brightens the room and contrasts beautifully with the others, most of all Radcliffe’s flummoxed British artiste. (A throwaway gag involving her “Voice Bucket Challenge” had me cracking up at my desk.) It’s a mere single component upgrade we’re talking about here, but it elevates Reggie Dinkins to a much higher degree this time around.

Jalyn Hall, too, grows in his once-thankless role as Reggie and Monica’s teenage son, Carmelo. Through a new overarching storyline involving a forbidden romance, which becomes a linchpin for most of season 2’s first half, Carmelo crystallizes in his specific variant of “Blerd” that is more refreshing than derivative. He’s not just “The Geek,” as you’d find in most sitcoms. He’s fluid enough to perform the function without killing the buzz.

The first four episodes of the season, provided to the press, keep up season 1’s winning formula—22-minute-sitcom hijinks that unfold against an airy atmosphere with well-timed punchlines. Daniel Radcliffe and Tracy Morgan further assert their powers as TV’s unlikeliest duo, and I can’t get enough of them. I pray there are yet more improbable excuses to ensure multiple seasons of a single documentary’s production. Sure, the rhythms are familiar now, but it’s welcome fun. Reggie Dinkins is a potent reminder of what television used to be and what it still can be if we’re brave enough to let it.

#6649. Demi Moore Takes Denim-on-Denim Dressing to the Next Level in a GapStudio Dress at the 2026 CFDA Fashion Awards

A very American look for an American fashion ceremony

From hot-pink Matières Fécales ballgowns to iridescent Gucci dresses bursting with feathers and completely see-through chainmail Dolce & Gabbana slips, Demi Moore has never been afraid to take risks on the red carpet. So, when the Council of Fashion Designers of America (CFDA) hosted the 2026 Fashion Awards in New York City on Monday night (colloquially referred to as “the Oscars of American fashion”), it was no surprise to see Moore turning heads yet again.

With her look, the Oscar nominee took denim dressing to the next level, in a layered GapStudio dress designed by one of the night’s honorees: Zac Posen.

Since February 2024, Posen has acted as the creative director and executive vice president of Gap Inc., and the American brand for which he serves at the helm was recognized with the Innovation Award last night.

Posen’s experimental approach to the classic American brand was on full display when Moore arrived in a dress that looked like it was made out of nearly ten different pairs of blue jeans.

Composed of a dark-wash denim, the halter-style design featured a split-hem skirt that pooled on the ground, while the form-fitting bodice was made of stacks and stacks of material that emulated the waistbands of jeans. A line of buttons dotted the center, while up top, a final layer folded over to create a subtle sweetheart neckline.

For another denim touch, Moore carried a flap bag that even came equipped with a jean-fabric handle, designed by the brand’s executive director of accessories, Reed Krakoff. Finally, she completed the fit with tan suede pumps that peeked out from under the skirt of her dress, and a set of diamond earrings from Hassanzadeh.

Over the years, we’ve seen plenty of stars pull off denim-on-denim fits, but never with something quite like this.

#6648. 31 Best Gifts for Mom in 2026, According to GQ Editors

There's an art to giving a good gift to someone who's been a mother to you, whether that's your actual mom, grandmother, or the person who's always looked out for you. And it has precious little to do with how much you spend. They say what goes around comes around, and when the occasion comes to buy her something special, there's not better time to return a little of the favor. To that end, we've rounded up the best gifts for mom, from modest indulgences to proper splurges, all chosen with an eye toward good taste, a little surprise, and the small triumph of getting it just right.

Still stumped? We'd suggest perusing the rest of our gift guides and signing up for the GQ Recommends newsletter for more gift ideas worth your money.

I have an AYR sweater in this very fabric, and I've been wearing the hell out of it lately. It's extraordinarily soft, with a bouncy, bouclé-like texture that you really have to feel to appreciate. The High has all that going for it, plus a cropped fit and a prim, high-set collar.

We're firm believers that a good tuxedo is one you'd wear any old day. Which is why we're digging this casual cotton tuxedo shirt from Gap's collaboration with Victoria Beckham. It's awfully chic, and a reason for her to get "dressed up" with nowhere special to go.

Breda's Jane Time Charm is a clever little thing. Made from gold-plated stainless steel, the miniature timepiece can be worn as a necklace or clipped to a keychain, but I think it makes for a pretty decoration on a lapel.

Timex's Cavatina Mini looks like something you'd stumble upon at an estate sale, with its petite 17mm case and old-school script logo. The design nods to the brand's '60s-era women's watches, though this one's fresh out of the box.

Thistles is an IYKYK eyewear label whose fashionable milieu stretches from Em Rata to Kim Gordon and any mom worth her vintage Prada. There are plenty of excellent frames to choose from, but the square-shaped Suede is a safe bet.

Coach has a bona fide can't-miss gift on its hands with the Brooklyn, a slouchy, do-it-all carryall. It comes in a range of sizes, but we're partial to the 34, a generously proportioned everyday bag with room for just about everything.

Cashmere is the safest bet you can make at Quince, so it's nice to see the brand putting it to work as the lining of these leather gloves. And at that price, there isn't much to complain about.

If she's made it this far in life without a pair of UGGs, the Zora ballet flat might be the one that finally wins her over. And if she's already living that UGG life, well, she's gonna need these.

A fresher, more spirited take on the original Coco, Coco Mademoiselle pairs sparkling orange and jasmine with rose and patchouli. For a mom with a taste for the classics, but an appreciation for modern flair, this one'll hit.

We recommended Therabody's Depuffing Wand last year, and we're running it back because it's just so dang good at what it does. Hot and cold treatments in one wonderfully unfussy little device make it a welcome addition to her skincare routine.

I like giving silk pillowcases because they're a beauty treatment and a bedroom upgrade in one. Clementine's smooth silk helps reduce friction against hair, minimizing tangles and breakage, while leaving fewer sleep creases.

Dyson's new travel-size Supersonic brings the brand's high-powered drying technology to a more portable package. It's compact enough to tuck into a carry-on without sacrificing the elite airflow of the original, and it's compatible with all the same attachments.

Laneige's Lip Sleeping Mask is a cult favorite, and chances are she already knows it, maybe even swears by it. But the new gingerbread flavor for the season? A stroke of seasonal madness, and a nice little stocking stuffer to boot.

Aesop is about as close to a sure thing as gifting gets. Its new Solias Hand Serum has a light, floral, citrusy, and herbaceous aroma, with a nourishing formula to help keep Mom's hands in fighting shape through winter.

No. 04 Bois de Balincourt has long drawn comparisons to Le Labo's Santal 33, only at a considerably friendlier price and without the burdern of its own ubiquity. Warm sandalwood and cedar, with earthy vetiver and a little spice—a perfect year-round candle.

I'm a coffee drinker through and through, but I'd consider switching allegiances to tea if it meant getting to use this teapot from Balmuda. Just looking at the thing feels therapeutic.

For farmers' market queens, this tote really does tick all the boxes: heavy-duty cotton canvas, six exterior pockets, a clever strap for securing, say, a bouquet of dahlihas, and a structured design that stands upright on its own.

This insulated Dutch oven tote from Staub and Clare V. is just the thing for the potluck monarch arriving with a cassoulet in tow. A highly specific gifting proposition, admittedly, but an excellent one nonetheless.

Inspired by the pleated designs of Issey Miyake, Wooj's Kinoko lamp has a pretty mushroom silhouette and a softly ribbed shade. A charming little thing for that neglected corner that could use some illumination.

There are plenty of newfangled brands making cute, colorful homewares these days, but long live Marimekko. These towels caught my eye for their classic brick pattern, rendered in a perfect shade of red, with all the pedigree of Finnish design behind them.

Apple's new entry-level laptop might be marketed to students, but it's also a very good laptop for anyone who just needs to do regular laptop things. Browsing, emailing, streaming, the occasional spreadsheet—the Neo handles the everyday stuff without demanding a small fortune for the privilege of owning a fruit-embossed gadget.

If you really want to round out the gift, throw in this laptop sleeve from Cuyana. Made from Italian leather, it's chic enough to be carried around on its own, which is more than you can say for most laptop accessories.

Sony's XM6 headphones are already among the best noise-canceling cans you can buy, but now they come in this very, very good matte olive gray colorway. As if we needed another reason to recommend them.

Brooklyn-based designer Virginia Sin made a name for herself with porcelain versions of disposable paper plates, and her talent for making everyday objects feel a little more special extends beyond the dinner table. Case in point: this little number, which would look mighty fine on mom's desk.

If mom's recently been promoted to grandmother, I know you've been thinking about getting her a digital picture frame, and yes, you absolutely should. But not just any digital picture frame: the Aura is the one to beat, and only the best will do when it comes to showing off the new bundle.

Twelve South's Valet gives her phone a proper place to recharge, with a handy catchall for keys, jewelry, and whatever else tends to accumulate around the house.

Prettier humidifiers? There aren't many. Canopy's 2.0 is a welcome antidote to the moisture-sapping misery of winter, and will look great wherever she puts it.

And just like that, aesthetically pleasing pill organizers are a thing. Fine by us. Where else is she supposed to stash her daily rotation of vitamins, supplements, and what have you?

Never underestimate the restorative powers of a little targeted heat. Ostrichpillow's new Embrace is a clever upgrade to the humble heating pad, with a wearable design, three heat settings, and up to three hours of cordless warmth.

Again, never underestimate the restorative powers of a little targeted heat. Or, in this case, heat from head to toe. HigherDose's sauna blanket is easy to set up and use, and delivers one hell of a sweat session without requiring a trip to an actual hot box.

Some peace and quiet might be the greatest gift of all. Loop's Switch 2 earplugs offer three levels of noise reduction, adjustable with a simple dial, for everything from quick naps to concerts to moments when she'd like you, specifically, to pipe down.

For the mom who likes to get her miles in, these open-ear headphones let her soundtrack a morning run without losing track of the world around her. They're lightweight, stay put even when she's picking up the pace, and pack up to 12 hours of battery life.

#6646. The 2026 Esquire Cologne Awards

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Whether you’re looking to make a big impression on a special occasion or just searching for a great everyday scent, these winners have you covered.

There’s never been a better time to be a guy who wants to smell good. The men’s fragrance market is booming, the scents are bolder than ever, and you can shop for pretty much anything you could possibly imagine—from long-lasting extracts for a big night out to easy-to-wear options that’ll fit right in at the office—with ease.

The only problem with all that abundance is the project of narrowing things down to a manageable number of scents that might work for you. Which is where we come in. This year, the team here at Esquire decided to go big for our annual Cologne Awards. Our fragrance experts called in hundreds of new releases over the course of the year, narrowing them down through extensive testing and internal debate before each creating their own short list of winners.

That’s when things got really interesting. Round by round, the Esquire Cologne Awards committee met to discuss potential winners, spritzing and testing again and again before finally producing the list you see here. These are the 40 best scents of the year. You might not connect with every one of them—but if you can’t find something you love here, well, good luck out there.

This has become one of our daily wears—and for good reason. It’s going to please pretty much anyone. Spicy and aromatic, this cologne is vanilla and amber forward with discernible notes of Iris germanica and lavender giving it a more floral edge. The result is sensual and soft but never unmasculine, thanks to the aromatic freshness. Plus, it comes packaged in a giant flask. There’s nothing manlier than having a giant flask (literally) of cologne behind your bathroom mirror.

Leather lovers, this one is for you. Tom Ford’s newest Ombré Leather Reserve blends notes of earthy, black leather with silver juniper and sandalwood, creating a balanced, rooted, yet refreshing take on Ford’s classic Ombré Leather eau de parfum. Inspired by the great American West and the mountains and deserts that constitute it, this scent is a perfect balance between refinement and rugged masculinity.

If you were around in the ’90s and spent some time at the mall, you probably remember Gap Dream. It was the most iconic scent of the original lineup, which was discontinued in the early aughts. But Gap has given the people what they want: a relaunch of its beloved fragrance line. Dream remains the hero of the collection, fresh and clean like a brand-new white T-shirt, with bright citrus, soft florals, and botanical musk.

Noble but knows how to fuck. A raw oud with fresh sensuality. The peach is jam-like, adding a softness to the suede accord. Velvet Mood is a leather fragrance with added warmth, texture, and juicy sweetness. (It recalls the intimate, sexual encounter in Call Me by Your Name. The one with the peach. You know it.) It’s captivating and aura-maxxing, not a blind buy for most. But we’re still on the side of biting the peach.

This cologne has one job, and that job is beach. It’s salty, woody, and as fresh as anything else we smelled this year. From the blond driftwood to the beach rose, coconut water, and sea salt, all the notes are reminiscent of tropical paradise. It may not be summer anymore, but the perfect summer scent has landed. Now that it’s in your possession, you can turn any day into a beach day.

Though it is totally within the Ranger Station wheelhouse of western-inspired cowpoke smells, it’s a new earthy floral. The easy choice would have been to mix sandalwood and ambergris with whiskey and call it another saloon smell. Instead the Ranger Station team gave it an iris root heart—soft and floral without losing its edge. It’s not betting the farm and drinking away the loss. It’s betting the farm, losing it, and stepping out those double swing doors to see a sunrise and smell a new dewy and green morning. You lost it all, but that was yesterday. Keep moving forward and the next win can’t be too far down the road.

It took four years to get right and was, according to the company, the last thing on which Giorgio Armani put his personal stamp of approval. And it’s not what you might expect if you’re thinking of the aquatic, mineral freshness of something like Acqua di Giò. I Will is a different animal—all wood and vanilla with bergamot to keep things from getting too dark and dense. It’s rich and modern and exactly what people will be talking about 20 years from now when they’re discussing the Italian fashion house’s other iconic scents.

Soft and sensual, Musc Pallida lingers like a whisper on your skin. Powdery iris and warm musk are the main components here, and they work together seamlessly. It’s light and airy but doesn’t fade quickly, and it smells good on pretty much anyone. Don’t go into Musc Pallida expecting your typical manly musk fragrance—it’s much more elegant and intimate.

Earthy sweetness, earthly delights. This fragrance from Byredo is sweet without ever venturing into gourmand territory. Even with its Madagascar vanilla and vanilla liquor accord at the base, it is punctuated with fougères and florals that give it complexity without steering it into being a spice bomb or a cognac cocktail. Instead it has a bright greenness and soft musk that can see the light of day and what’s to come. Simply fresh, refined with some sweetness—for a beautiful day.

A sophisticated blend of cinnamon, sage, and bergamot gives this fragrance a warm, spicy character. Cinnamon fragrances tend to be overpowering and reserved for nighttime, but this scent feels sophisticated and is a great daytime spice since the spicy top notes are balanced by a vanilla and praline base. It’s an elegant take on a spice-forward scent and a Nordstrom exclusive, which makes sense since Parfums de Marly is one of the retailer’s top-selling brands.

Creed’s Iris Debonair—a totally new offering, not a new expression—walks a wonderful line. It’s very gentlemanly with its cool and classic fougère heart. The opening and its transition into the middle is a lot of bergamot, absinthe, and lavender, making it seem like an expected (albeit exceptional) barbershop scent. But as it settles, iris starts to come to the forefront. It’s not the old-fashioned iris of Green Irish Tweed but a softer one that pairs well with a soft ambrette dry down. It has a true masculine and a true feminine side, and neither controls your lasting memory of the scent. Totally captivating.

Citrus and amber are not natural bedfellows, but this scent—a more highly concentrated version of the former Le Gemme Tygar—makes a very strong case for their marriage. It’s bright and grapefruit-y in one instant and then rich and woody in another. The more you smell it, the more the moniker starts to make sense. It is, after all, named after tigereye, a gemstone revered not for its sameness but for the striking impression that its contrasting bands of color create.

Fig fragrances are having a moment, but this new Granado EDP blows all other fig scents out of the water. Candied green fig reveals rich, jammy notes that are sweet but not in the typical syrupy way; it’s a lush, even fresh kind of sweet. Once you get hints of sweet dates, florals, and woods, it all combines to create a beautifully creamy gourmand.

It opens fruity and floral. A burst of raspberry gives way to peony, geranium, jasmine, magnolia. That might sound a little tough for some guys—but remember it’s just the beginning. Those notes soon give way to a base of deep, powdery musks. It’s not your typical masculine fragrance; it’s something a little more delicate and, though the scent is rooted in centuries of Chinese tradition, modern. We’d encourage you to give it a try. You might just find a new daily driver.

Before you spritz this woody scent, you have to admire the artistry of the bottle. Mind Games is known for its chess-inspired bottles, and Knight’s Tour is a great example of that. The scent inside is a sophisticated blend of lavender and saffron with spicy accents up top and smooth woods at the base. It’s a great go-to option for every day.

One Esquire editor made Ripe her scent of the summer after it launched in the spring. The reason? Its perfect balance of warm florals, fresh fruity notes, soft woods, and musks is lush and enticing. Have you ever thought about how notes of lychee, watermelon sorbet, green leaves, rose water, and creamy sandalwood work together? Spoiler alert: It’s pretty awesome.

Ojai is like waking up in a luxury hotel in California, sunlight peeking through the shades as fruity and floral notes dance around the room. Juicy, ripe mango and honey-like saffron are at the heart of this fragrance, giving it a sweet yet elevated quality. But it also has an earthy, amber-y depth to it, thanks to patchouli and frankincense at the base.

Put rhubarb in a fragrance and we’re there. Demon Dancer takes the tartness of rhubarb and blackcurrant and adds bright citrus, fresh peppermint, and radiant orange blossom for an exciting opening before it settles into a woody base of amber, musk, and tonka bean. When the cool, fresh top and heart notes reveal the warm base, it really is something to take in.

Imagine that feeling when you’re walking around a smog-choked city and find an unexpected oasis. It’s fresh, green, but with just that tiniest hint of something darker and deeper. That’s this scent. It’s wood and pepper and greenery and a reminder that even in the midst of urban sprawl, we can always find a way back to nature.

The Dolce & Gabbana classic is now available as an alcohol-free perfume gel, making it a travel-friendly option for sensitive skin. The curved metal applicator makes it easy to apply the fragrance directly to pulse points, while the pocket-sized packaging is perfect for on-the-go touch-ups. The gel formula also works as a great base for layering if you already own the original fragrance and want to extend the wear time of the cult-favorite citrus scent.

#6641. What Writing a Book About JFK Jr. Taught Me About Personal Style

John F. Kennedy Jr. wasn’t the first guy to ride a bike wearing a suit. Likewise, the presidential scion and People’s 1988 Sexiest Man Alive didn’t invent the backward Kangol cap, the pinstriped suit, or wearing a waistcoat with jeans—he just did it better than pretty much anybody else. That’s a big part of why he was one of the most photographed men on the planet in the 1990s, and why he remains a menswear moodboard staple today, nearly 30 years after his untimely death. Still, no matter how hard you try, and no matter how closely you hew to the source material, all attempts to replicate JFK Jr.’s style are destined to leave you looking like a runner-up in a Washington Square Park lookalike contest.

Ask anyone who encountered John-John in his prime—which, in my experience, is virtually everyone who lived in Manhattan in the 1990s—and the first thing they’ll mention is his magnetism, his charm, and the fact that, against all odds, he was actually better-looking in person. I know this because, having recently written a book about JFK Jr.’s style, I’ve spent a good part of the last year poring over photos, reading biographies, speaking to people who knew him, and otherwise immersing myself in his world. Among the conclusions I’ve come to is that you probably won’t look as cool as JFK Jr. did wearing rollerblades, but there are a few style moves you can borrow from his playbook without resorting to full Kennedy cosplay.

JFK Jr.’s wardrobe was built on four pillars: tailoring, preppy basics, workwear, and sportswear. But it was the idiosyncratic way he combined them (i.e., a backward baseball cap with a suit, a polo neck sweater under a tweed blazer, hiking boots with tailored trousers) that resulted in some of his most memorable looks. Surprisingly, while the combination of straight-leg jeans, a button-up dress shirt, and a blazer has become shorthand for JFK Jr.’s style, I’ve never seen any evidence of him wearing it. In fact, he seldom wore jeans at all (some speculate his mother thought they looked shabby, while others told me that jeans didn’t complement his active lifestyle). That said, plenty of other ’90s style icons, from Ralph Lauren to Jerry Seinfeld, rocked this particular look, and it’s a pretty easy one to pull off. As with everything about JFK Jr.’s style, it’s not about lifting these combinations wholesale, but understanding the precise balance of contrast that makes them work.

One of the most consistent elements of JFK Jr.’s style was his love of discordant accessories, from his collection of flat caps to his ever-present wallet chain to his Oakley Eye Jacket shades. Some of these were practical—he was so prone to misplacing his wallet and keys that he needed to chain them to his pants—but others reflected his love of color (funky knit caps), his enthusiasm for skiing (fleece headbands), and his passion for travel (various hats picked up on trips to India and Thailand). Some accessories will suit you better than others, but if you have a personal connection to it, all the better. And most importantly, limit yourself to one standout add-on per outfit.

Speaking of accessories: JFK Jr. was a dedicated scarf guy, and he’d pair one with everything from joggers and sneakers to his best navy blue suit. Rather than draping a scarf loosely around his neck, he tended to tie it in either a dressy overhand or classic Parisian knot, both of which lend a more formal look. He also loved wearing a thick scarf as outerwear, throwing it over a sweatshirt on a chilly day instead of a coat, often paired with a knit cap. For best results, don’t pick a scarf that matches the rest of your outfit too closely.

Among the most surprising things I discovered about JFK Jr.’s wardrobe was his watch collection. I wasn’t able to uncover any evidence of him wearing the long-lost Rolex Daytona given to him by Aristotle Onassis (a watch that, if ever found, would give Paul Newman a run for his money), but I did identify an unusually wide range of other pieces in his collection, from a Victorinox Swiss Army field watch to a Swatch chronograph to a vintage Rolex Oyster Perpetual. This is of a piece with everything else we know about JFK Jr.: he wasn’t bound by any single style, brand, or price point, and got just as much enjoyment out of a cheap quartz watch as a luxury mechanical one.

As a rich kid raised on the Upper East Side, JFK Jr.’s closet included plenty of blazers, chinos, and polos. He was a gym rat who loved playing sports, so he also had a lot of sweats, tees, and sneakers. He was a downtown socialite, so he owned a lot of nice suits and tuxes, too. He also had an enviable collection of graphic tees from NYC institutions like the Russian & Turkish Baths in the East Village and the New York Athletic Club, as well as some commemorating events like the 1984 NYC Fresh Festival, a seminal moment in hip-hop history. What stands out to me—and what I believe makes his style so distinctive—is that JFK Jr.’s wardrobe was an authentic reflection of who he was, where he came from, and what he loved. This might be the most important style lesson any of us can learn from him: Great personal style is less about clothes than wearing them in a way that expresses your individuality.

American Style: The Look and Legacy of JFK Jr. is now available for pre-order.

#6639. Pamela Anderson’s ‘Winter Cherry’ Nails Are the Holiday Manicure to Bookmark

An elevated take on the classic seasonal manicure.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Slightly deviating from her usual clean, minimalist, barely-there manicure, Pamela Anderson just made a case for 2026’s update to classic red nails. The actor and model was spotted at the 2026 CFDA Awards sporting a freshly lacquered set of “winter cherry nails,” a rich update on the classic red manicure.

Perfectly suited to the upcoming holiday season, winter cherry comes as a slightly more nuanced alternative to the beloved seasonal favorite “cherry red.” Think classic red, but with greater depth and dimension. With their slightly deeper tone, winter cherry nails are more at home next to a roaring fire than on a picnic blanket. It serves as proof of just how impactful a statement manicure can be, and Anderson serves as the ultimate muse, contrasting the manicure against a sleek black gown and crisp black-and-white clutch.

“We’re seeing reds become much deeper and richer,” Susan Carroll, founder of Notting Hill beauty destination Young LDN, previously told ELLE. Beyond selecting the perfect shade, Carroll notes that meticulous prep is paramount. After all, a striking winter cherry red leaves little room for errors. “Start with neatly shaped nails and well-prepped cuticles, then make sure the nail plate is completely clean before applying your base coat.”

To finish off, Carroll recommends adding a glossy top coat for a high-shine effect, similar to Anderson’s. Additionally, for those looking to explore further, we have compiled the best winter cherry manicure colors to bookmark ahead of your next appointment or DIY at-home manicure.

#6637. Patrick Stewart Was a Serious Watch Guy. Here Are 5 of the Late Hollywood Legend's Best.

From a treasured Rolex to his beloved IWC, the late actor had a lifelong appreciation for the art of watchmaking.

“Someone once told me that time was a predator that stalked us all our lives," begins Captain Jean-Luc Picard, played by Sir Patrick Stewart in Star Trek Generations, in one of his most memorable lines. "I rather believe that time is a companion who goes with us on a journey, reminds us to cherish every moment, because it will never come again.”

Those musings on time feel particularly poignant now, following the British actor's death. There’s something rather comforting about the idea of time as a companion, particularly when you consider Stewart’s lifelong fascination with the very objects we use to measure it.

Because, as it turns out, Patrick Stewart was quite the watch guy. Not simply someone who happened to wear an expensive timepiece on the red carpet, but a genuine enthusiast with an appreciation for the craftsmanship, history, and ingenuity behind them. He bought his first watch at 18 and, by his own admission, never threw one away.

“The watch industry never ceases to amaze me,” he told the Fondation de la Haute Horlogerie in 2019. “Because a watch is more than just an object that gives the time. Behind every watch there is an idea, a design, a concept, an innovation, and the watchmaker's desire to create something that is greater than anything that has ever existed before.”

It's an enthusiasm that stayed with him throughout his life, from the Rolex he reportedly spent almost all his savings on after arriving in Hollywood to his enduring affection for IWC Schaffhausen. And, naturally, there were some rather lovely watches along the way.

So, in celebration of an extraordinary career and a life well lived, we've taken a look back at some of Patrick Stewart's finest watch moments.

Perhaps the most meaningful watch in Stewart's story is one for which we don't have an exact reference. Long before he became one of Hollywood's most recognizable actors, Stewart was given a rather unexpected piece of career advice by the actress Eva Marie Saint. She advised him that appearances mattered, and that an expensive watch might help convince producers he was already a successful actor. So, she took him to a Beverly Hills jeweller, where he picked out a Rolex. The only problem? It cost him his entire savings. “I was eating spaghetti and boiled potatoes for months after that!” he recalled in a 2019 interview with the Fondation de la Haute Horlogerie.

It's touching anecdote given Stewart's modest upbringing. Having grown up in poverty, he admitted that it took him years to feel comfortable spending money on beautiful things. But eventually, he came to realize there was nothing wrong with enjoying the rewards of his success.

Tissot Le Locle Powermatic 80 to the 2017 Canadian Grand Prix

One of Stewart's most entertaining watch moments came at the 2017 Canadian Grand Prix, when he joined Daniel Ricciardo on the podium and celebrated the Australian driver's third-place finish by drinking champagne from his racing boot. Yes, really.

And while most people were understandably distracted by the sight of Captain Picard downing champagne from a sweaty shoe, there was a rather lovely watch on his wrist, a Tissot Le Locle Powermatic 80. The reasonably priced Swiss automatic features a silver guilloché dial, Roman numerals, a black leather strap, and an impressive 80-hour power reserve. Proof that even a relatively affordable dress watch can look the part.

Long before his appearances at watchmaking galas, Stewart was already making a convincing case for the understated dress watch. In this archival portrait, the actor wears a small, gold-tone, rectangular timepiece, seemingly a Cartier Tank, on a dark leather strap. Paired with a pale linen-style suit and a knitted white top, it's a particularly handsome combination.

First introduced in 1917, the Cartier Tank has become one of watchmaking's most enduring designs, worn by everyone from Andy Warhol to Muhammad Ali. Its appeal lies in its simplicity: a rectangular case, elegant proportions, and a design that has barely needed changing in more than a century. And judging by this photograph, Stewart understood the appeal of a good dress watch long before his later affection for IWC became public knowledge.

IWC Portofino Automatic to the 2018 Academy Awards

When it came to dressing for the red carpet, Stewart understood the appeal of keeping things classic. At the 90th Academy Awards in 2018, he opted for an IWC Portofino Automatic, reference IW356504, a rather beautiful dress watch that proved you don't need an enormous, diamond-covered timepiece to make an impression. Housed in a 40mm case of 18-carat rose gold, the watch features a silver-plated dial, slim gold hour markers, and a discreet date window at three o'clock. It's finished with a dark brown alligator leather strap and powered by an automatic movement. There's nothing particularly flashy about it, which is precisely what makes it so appealing.

IWC Portugieser Chronograph Classic at the 2017 SIHH Gala

By 2017, Stewart's appreciation for fine watchmaking had brought him into the company of some of the industry's most celebrated names. That January, he attended IWC Schaffhausen's Decoding the Beauty of Time gala in Geneva alongside his wife, Sunny Ozell, sporting a rather handsome IWC Portugieser Chronograph Classic.

First introduced in 2013, the reference 3904 is a particularly elegant piece of watchmaking. Featuring a 42mm case, distinctive Arabic numerals, and a railway-track minute scale, it's a slightly sportier proposition than the Portofino. Inside sits IWC's automatic calibre 89361, complete with a flyback chronograph function and an impressive 68-hour power reserve. The evening celebrated the Swiss manufacturer's renewed Da Vinci collection, but Stewart's choice of wristwear showcased another of IWC's most recognizable designs. And given that he once named IWC as his favorite watchmaker, it's hardly surprising that he had such good taste.

#6636. 7 Peter Millar Staples That Work Wherever the Day Takes You

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Good clothes, fewer decisions. What’s not to like?

Getting dressed for just about anything is a whole lot easier when you have the right essentials in your closet. Whether you’re heading into the office, catching a flight, or packing for a weekend away, a few well-chosen pieces can take you from one plan to the next without having to give your outfit much thought.

That’s the appeal of Peter Millar’s Crown Collection, which brings together classic menswear with thoughtful details, quality fabrics, and a relaxed approach to dressing well. There’s an emphasis on comfort and practicality, but never at the expense of looking polished. From tailored trousers and half-zips to quilted layers and other everyday favorites, these are pieces you'll find yourself reaching for again and again.

Ahead, seven picks from the collection, available at Bloomingdale’s, that deserve a spot in your closet. Because looking good shouldn’t require a whole lot of effort.

A chore coat is a great casual Friday-to-weekend staple when you want to look put together without feeling too dressed up. This navy style has a relaxed, cardigan-like feel, with a classic collar and patch pockets that work just as well with a T-shirt and jeans as they do with a button-down and chinos.

The Essex Vest is an iconic piece within Peter Millar’s Crown Collection and is designed as a versatile layering option for fall that works across a range of occasions. This black diamond-quilted style offers an extra layer of warmth without the bulk of a jacket, making it just as useful for your morning commute as it is for a round of golf or an afternoon apple picking.

Few pieces strike the balance between comfort and looking sharp quite like a good half-zip. This navy pullover is comfortable enough for a long flight but polished enough to wear over a collared shirt with tailored trousers. Prediction: You’ll want it in multiple colors.

A quilted coat is timeless, especially in a rich, dark olive shade. This one has a classic countryside feel, but its clean shape looks just as at home on city streets with jeans and boots or layered over a button-down and trousers. A bottle opener tucked inside the left interior pocket adds a handy signature touch. It’s a smart choice for chilly days that don’t call for a heavy overcoat.

Utilitarian pants are a wardrobe icon, and this five-pocket design offers a slightly more refined take on the classic. The smooth sateen fabric and rich blue color give them a polished look that works with everything from a polo at the office to a crisp shirt and loafers for dinner.

Every man’s shoe collection should include a pair of driving loafers, particularly when comfort and looking elegant are equally important. These walnut-brown suede drivers feature classic penny-loafer detailing and flexible, rubber-studded soles, making them a natural choice with chinos, tailored trousers, or jeans.

A checked button-down is a classic for a reason, but how about one with a little more personality? This soft twill style features a small-scale pattern and subtle grapeseed color that look particularly great with navy, gray, or khaki. Designed to be tucked in, it pairs easily with tailored trousers for work or sateen five pocket pants for a more relaxed look.
