#6632. 14 Best Work Jackets for Men 2026, According to GQ Editors

For better or worse, the best work jackets for men are no longer associated with garages, factory floors, and loading docks. Instead, as the likes of Paul Mescal and Austin Butler (who told GQ he wore his for a year straight) continue to fly their flag, a great work jacket has become every guy's fastest route to semi-rugged heartthrob style.

Honestly, they make looking good almost too easy. First, they inject your fits with casual, weekend tough-guy energy. (This, to be clear, is a plus.) Second, they flatter you in all the right places, framing your shoulders and neatening up your waistline whether you’re pairing them with jeans or the kinds of wide, puddling trousers we've long been yapping about.

Better still, you don’t have to spend a fortune. The classics from Dickies and Carhartt are as dependable as ever, and even the independent designer options are within reach of your budget—this is a cotton-forward option, after all. Here are 14 of our favorites, all of which will only look better after a few years (and a few scuffs) of real wear.

The Carhartt Detroit work jacket is the classic for a reason. It’s been around for more than 70 years—not quite as old as blue jeans or cowboy boots, but easily in the upper echelon of American fashion’s great exports. So much so that today, luxury brands are riffing on it, even selling pre-distressed, washed-down versions at triple the price. (And yes, this is also the work jacket that Austin Butler told us he wears.)

Cut to the waist, and built from thick, stiff canvas with three perfectly placed front pockets, the Detroit is an Eisenhower-style silhouette (during WWII, the then-Army General overhauled U.S. military dress and introduced the cropped, boxy jacket to American forces). Its insulated blanket lining and corduroy collar also make it one of the few true year-round, toasty work jackets out there. Simply put, this is a real piece of clothing for real life. (Though if it's not fancy enough for you, there’s always the street-ready, slimmer, unlined Carhartt WIP version.)

Just like Dickies' iconic 874 work pants, the brand's insulated Eisenhower jacket (there he is again) is a hard-wearing, warm, wallet-friendly piece of gear that fits incredibly well. Nearly as old as the Detroit Jacket above, it doesn’t always get the same respect. But maybe that's just because it flies a little more under the radar, with its logo patch tucked discreetly near the hem. Decades in, it remains a low-key, low-cost icon—less shouty than its Carhartt cousin, but every bit as authentic.

Wallace & Barnes, J.Crew's workwear line, is back with tough fabrics, heritage details, and a big hit of #menswear-era nostalgia—and this wool-lined work jacket shows exactly why folks missed it so much. It has all of the heartthrob-coded details you probably had in mind when you decided to look for a jacket like this: Corduroy collar, zippered chest pocket, slightly cropped fit. But it isn't just good-looking. It also has a wool-blend blanket lining, for superior warmth to help you enjoy it even if the depths of winter. And that, if cost-per-wear logic still applies, makes it more than worth its slightly higher price point.

Welcome to the world of truly warm fabrics, and actually glove-friendly pockets. Filson’s been mastering cold-weather gear since your great-great-grandfather was in short pants, and this work jacket continues that legacy. Based on the brand’s original 1914 Cruiser, it’s cut from 24-ounce Mackinaw wool — a tightly woven twill that truly blocks wind, sheds rain, and stays warm even when wet. Without being mean, it’s built to outlast even you. Pricey? Sure. But it’s the kind of thing your kids will remember you by (and fight for) someday.

Buck Mason’s archive is full to bursting with vintage pieces from decades past, which explains why its collections are filled with absolute bangers these days. Here the brand has taken a mid-century work jacket and remade it, tweaking the fit to make it a touch more relaxed, and using a soft Italian doeskin cotton fabric that’s more plush than anything a worksite would have issued back in the day. There's even more to love in the details: Shoulder pleats for easy movement, real horn buttons on the chest pockets, and a dressy point collar.

For Lo-heads, Polo Ralph Lauren's hard-wearing zipper jackets are the stuff of legend. And while you might have grown up with an inherited cotton example from your dad, in the last decade of so, Polo has been pushing its ‘Bi-Swing’ polyester and/or nylon takes instead. That's fine, but some of us aren't so willing to trade in the soft, rugged comfort of a full-cotton jacket. That's where the rarer Bayport Poplin jacket comes in. A work jacket with no delusions, it’s not stealing the working man’s valor — it knows exactly what it is. It’s Ralph Lauren, and it knows you’re working hard at hardly working. But it's also strong enough to handle whatever activities you throw at it, leisure or otherwise.

Sure, Copenhagen's Mfpen has its silhouettes dialed, but don't overlook its fabrications. Here, it’s a dense wool-blend with a multi-tonal yarn construction. Textured, substantive, perfect.

While most waxed cotton jackets lean more country estate than work site, Tom Beckbe's high-quality range include a few closer, more modern fits. Perfect for those looking for a work jacket that's also waterproof.

A designer-adjacent example for the fashion crowd, this piece from South Korea's FrizmWORKS doesn't just look good, it feels good too, thanks to that soft leather collar that's just buttery once it's flipped up.

Stan Ray is part of what you might call the big three of American workwear, alongside Dickies and Carhartt). The Texas-born brand’s aptly named Roughneck Jacket delivers exactly what you’d expect. It’s tough, practical, and built for wear. Ribbed cuffs keep the cold out, while the washed-out hue delivers a perfectly broken-in look from day one.

This jacket from New York's Corridor—made from a hefty cotton-blend twill—is a more colorful take on the work jacket, and bound to give any fall fit a lift.

Just as the Polo jacket above is an RL icon, so is this faithfully-recreated nautical deck jacket from Ralph's RRL line. It's restocked every fall, it sells out every fall.

A key feature of all work jackets is their shorter length, but for those of us who want to make sure ours hits right at the belt, look no further than Abercrombie’s entry.

A leading Parisian purveyor of vintage garms, BRUT has recently moved into making its own stuff, all rooted in the archival vintage styles they obsess over. The results are, how you say, on point.

These days, work jackets seem to have an undeniable stranglehold on the imagination of guys who spend all day staring at their keyboards. (There’s a political element to this, of course. Whether or not you act in solidarity with your fellow laborers under capitalism, a work jacket allows you to look the part of the everyman.) But also, they’re just a great jacket for busy lives! There’s a generally accommodating cut that doesn’t restrict your movement, plenty of pockets, and hardy materials that can easily handle a snag, or sudden shower.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)

And then the folks on the Morning Joe show mournfully deplored Mr. Paine’s divisive rhetoric.

(And that is not even to mention that Representative Jamie Raskin will be chairing the House Judiciary Committee.)

#6627. The Real-Life Diet of John King, Who Hides Mini Protein Bars Behind the Magic Wall on Election Night

Nobody white knuckles Election Day the way John King does. Since 2008, CNN’s chief national correspondent has spent his midterm and presidential elections in wizard-like command of the Magic Wall—the omnipresent interactive map that drills into granular data from county to county as votes trickle in. (This year it will also include stats that show whether the cost of gas, rent, or groceries have increased or decreased in specific precincts.) The so-called charthrob has also completed some especially long shifts—working back-to-back 12- to 16-hour days during the 2020 presidential election, when results took about a week to tabulate. This year, to prepare for the midterms, he’s been traveling nonstop between battleground states, interviewing sources to stay on top of the national mood. “The way to be at my best on election night is to actually do the homework by visiting those places in the year and a half or so leading up to the election,” he says.

That is no easy task, given the 63-year-old dad of three also lives with multiple sclerosis. To keep his symptoms in check and his head cool as results pour in, he’s created a disciplined regime that includes hitting the gym no matter how little sleep he gets, keeping a mental map of healthy places to eat in flyover country, and occasionally giving himself time to sightsee—or stare at a wall. GQ spoke with King a week ahead of Tuesday’s races to learn how he keeps steady amid the chaos.

John King: All this year, I’ve been on the road, on average, about two weeks a month for the midterm cycle. And we started obviously late last year. You have to do that. This last week is particularly grueling. Normally we go out and we do one state and we travel into say, North Carolina. We were there for three days. We went to Texas and we were there for four days, three nights. The schedule’s pretty busy on those days, but you have a little bit of breathing room and you can make time for a hotel gym and you can search a little bit to find a better restaurant that might have a few actual salads or fresh fish on the menu. This week, we’re doing five states in five days.

That included working until about 10 o’clock on Monday night and then having to catch a 6 a.m. flight Tuesday morning. It included last night wrapping at about 8 o’clock in Detroit and having to drive four, four and a half hours into central Ohio to be here today. On those days, you’re not really in control of your schedule. Your schedule is in charge of you. You just have to be somewhere at a certain point the next day. So everything is driven by that.

So can you fly there? If not, you have to drive there. And in those situations, if you’re talking about trying to keep a healthy diet, that becomes a pretty tough challenge. You’re driving past the McDonald’s and the Burger Kings and the Culvers and you’re thinking, Oh, wow, that looks great. And you shouldn’t go anywhere near it.

Sometimes you do. Sometimes you do because you decided you deserve a treat. Sometimes you do because you have no choice. My philosophy about that is, like anything in life, if you indulge, then you just have to pay the price on the other end. So that means having yogurt and granola for breakfast and some berries, and that means making sure you get to the gym.

I’m psychotic about the gym. In part because I have multiple sclerosis and I have some mobility challenges sometimes. At the end of just about every day, I get what the doctors call MS fatigue. You get headaches and your legs are very heavy. And so I have found in dealing with this that if I exercise in the morning, it really helps me. And so the other day, when we had that 6 a.m. flight, even though I didn’t get to bed until 11 at night, I got up at 2 in the morning and went to the hotel gym.

I’m trading sleep for exercise, which if you do that over 10 days, two weeks, you’re going to pay a price for it. But we’re doing five states in five days. It’s going to be pretty grueling anyway. We had an early start this morning. We had to be to a farm pretty early, and so I was at the hotel gym at 5:30 in the morning. That’s just part of my routine.

That depends on how much time I have. If I can, I work out for about 90 minutes in the morning. Every gym is a little different. Some hotels are great, some hotels are in-between. The hotel we stayed in last night was a bit of a time warp. The equipment was pretty old. If there’s a recumbent bike, I obviously have either emails from overnight to read or news articles to read or things to write. So I’ll spend 30 minutes or so on a recumbent bike, where I’m pedaling to get my body warmed up and I’m also doing some work, reading and communicating about the day ahead.

And then I get on the treadmill for a little bit. I’m not a runner, but I can jog a little bit, or do incline treadmill just to get my heart rate up a little bit, and then try to do a little bit of light weights if I have the time.

You do what you can. I’m pretty set in that I bike because I have to do some administrative stuff and that’s easiest on the bike. And then I get on the treadmill where I just try to listen to music and zone out a little bit. And if I can, I’ll do a quick circuit of some light weights because if you just do cardio, that one beer you have at the end of the night stays with you. So you got to do the weight.

I don’t usually write scripts or anything, but I will write to people back in Washington who are asking me questions. Or if I’m trying to find somebody on trips like this, a lot of stuff we do on the fly. I’ll occasionally write graphics. If I know I need to write a graphic or in the middle of a process, if we’re editing a piece, the editor might send me a graphic that they’ve done, What do you think? I’m not trying to write my actual scripts for pieces. Sometimes I tweak them, but if I tried to write them from scratch on a bike, I would lose my mind.

You have no choice. If I’m going to get my exercise in, at least part of that, it’s going to have to include some work time.

Yes. When I travel. I started this when I covered the White House—I covered the White House for 10 years—especially on international trips where you’re living on local time wherever you are in the world, but our CNN programming is on US time. I would have what I call the 20-minute rule where no matter how tired I was, if I was in a cool place around the world or even in the country, I just tried to get outside of the hotel for 20 minutes of me time. And so if you’re in Paris, you take a walk and stare at the Eiffel Tower.

I remember the Bush-Putin trip to Estonia where Bush said he looked into Putin’s soul. That became a big controversy. I remember after that day—we were on television all day and it was a big day—just walking around and looking at the architecture. The Berlin Wall had come down. We were a decade past that or more. But in a lot of those countries, you’d have this fabulous European architecture, like you’re in Paris or like you were in Prague, and then across the street would be this horrible concrete structure that the communists built.

And so that’s how I learned. I’m a kid who grew up in Dorchester, Massachusetts. My family had no money. We never traveled. So I have this enormous gift of this job that sends me everywhere. And so I really think it’s important both for the curiosity part of it, but also I have a lot of work to do, but I’ll actually be more efficient at my job if I take 20 minutes to just say, “Wow, this is a cool place. Let’s enjoy it.”

I don’t think of myself as someone who meditates, but do I sit still sometimes and listen to music. I try to keep up with the books my son in high school is reading. Sorry, CNN, sorry, politics, sorry, campaigns. I’m filing you away for 15 or 20 minutes. That’s absolutely critical to mental health.

I try to pack a carry-on on this trip because we’re out for five days. Plus, on the tail end of it, I have to go to New York City because my daughter lives in New York City and she just got engaged and we’re having a celebration for her on Saturday night.

Thank you. It’s a really amazing time. And of course, it all comes in a tornado, but that’s fine. But I have to bring a suit and I have to bring some other clothes. And as I said, my exercise is important, so I have to pack gym stuff. So usually I think of myself as a very efficient, tight packer so that I can have a carry-on bag, just so, number one, they can’t lose it. And number two, you can keep moving faster. But on this trip, I actually have to check my bag because I’m on the road for six days and then I’m going to New York for two more. I don’t like checking a bag, but so be it. I have to do it on this trip.

I operate on East Coast time all the time. I get tired. I wouldn’t really even call it jet lag because I try to keep my body on East Coast time and my brain on East Coast time because that’s who I report to. After international trips, I’ll get jet-lagged, but exercise is important, number one. I drink too much coffee, and so on a long trip, that can get to you. Your body gets a little jittery if you have a little bit of jet lag. You try to sleep when you can. One thing I was never good at until campaigns is napping. Now I can take a 20-minute nap. There was a time in my life I could not nap. I can take a 20-minute nap now, which helps you sometimes when you need to. But I’m just trying to think of the last time I had really profound jet lag. I can’t have jet lag, I guess.

Yeah. I’m not allowed. My work doesn’t allow me normally, so I just bull through it. That doesn’t mean I don’t get tired. This trip is going to be grueling. I’ll get to New York on Saturday and I’ll have three or four hours before my daughter’s dinner and I’ll need to get to the gym, but I’m also betting I’ll stare at a wall for a few minutes and just be like, Whoa. But I don’t dwell on it because I can’t.

It gets hard on the hotel menu. Most hotels now at least will have a yogurt parfait or at least yogurt. There’s a very wide—what do we want to call it?— quality control or quality between the different kind of hotels you stay at.

We’ve been to Wisconsin several times. There’s a hotel called The Trade in Wisconsin we always stay at and they have a spectacular yogurt granola parfait bowl. I know in the morning I’m going to have a nice, healthy breakfast. Other places, it’s a challenge and you can’t find it or you just have no choice. So you grab the bacon, egg and cheese sandwich and that means I’m having a salad for lunch.

Then when you’re on the road, like last night, we were driving here, we had a four-and-a-half-hour drive, so you stop at a random gas station in small town America and you’re a little hungry. I try to grab the almonds, not the potato chips. Luckily, I’ve never been a sugar person, so I’m not going to drink soda that has sugar in it. I don’t even really drink diet soda. But on the road, it’s mostly water and coffee. And then, I’d be lying if I didn’t say I’m very happy if I could find a cold beer at the end of the day.

Yesterday, we had fantastic Middle Eastern food in Dearborn, Michigan. Just a chicken salad, falafel. And we’ve been back there a couple times. There are a couple other places where we know of these small businesses, just some local person has started, just a place where they just have salads and healthy smoothies. When we’re in those towns, we go back to those places because we’re grateful they’re there and they help us reset the bad-road-food meter.

If I’m in Kansas City, I’m going to Arthur Bryant’s barbecue, and that is full of sodium and it’s not good for you, but it’s amazing.

Part of it, especially since my MS diagnosis, I know that I’m going to get tired. I may have issues with both my leg and my hand functions. You learn a lot more from your struggles than you do from your successes, and I’m much more aware of my body on a daily basis, even on a minute-to-minute basis of how I’m doing and what are my challenges at the moment. Hydration is critically important.

On election night, I might be standing for 16 hours. It’s probably closer to 12, but it might be 16. If you go back to 2020 when we had the election that went on all week, we didn’t call the election until Saturday. I was on my feet for 14 or 15 hours the first night, and then 12 hours the next day, and then 12 hours the day after that. That was a huge challenge.

That week was the longest. I’ve been on the road for campaigns covering candidates where it’s event, bus, presidential campaigns, you’re going from state to state. So it’s event, bus, plane, event, bus, plane, event, bus, plane. They’re handing you a box. You don’t even know what’s in it, but you eat it. Because it’s the only food you’re going to get for the next 10 hours.

In the studio, I’m standing on my feet the whole time, but I have access to cold water. Somebody can get me a coffee. If you’re on television, you’re just asking for it if you try to eat a salad or anything like that because it’s going to get in your teeth and you don’t have time to go down the hall. You just don’t. You’re on live television. So I get little tiny mini protein bars and put a few of them behind the Magic Wall. And if I feel like I just need a little energy boost, I’ll eat those. I drink more coffee than I should, but coffee will help me when I need a boost. And then when I’m done, I can go to sleep. I can have a Nespresso after dinner and go to bed. My colleagues hate me because of that.

When I am sometimes challenged by the MS issues I have, it’s when something surprising happens and I’m not prepared for it. And then an hour into having to do something that I didn’t know I was going to have to do, I might think, Wow, my legs are jello, or having, they call it MS brain. It’s like a foggy brain issue sometimes.

But I know the election is Tuesday. A lot of people ask me, “Why are you going on the road for five days? You don’t need to do that.” I do need to do it. Professionally, I need to do it. Physically, I will pay a price for this, but I know that. And so you just prepare for it.

The human brain is amazing. I don’t pretend to understand it, and MS has a lot of mysteries, but from when I was first diagnosed to now, I can prepare myself for something and I’ve been able to get through it, knock on wood, which is my skull.

I was diagnosed in 2008, and so we’ve had many big election nights since then, and I’ve gotten through them, and I’ll get through this next one. And then when it’s over, I will collapse. I’ll go home and I’ll fall into bed and I’ll sleep. But if I have to get up in four hours and do it again, I will. I’ve just trained myself to do that.

I work out a lot so that I stay fit physically and that I have good stamina. I’m 63 years young, as I like to say, so it’s important anyway that you stay more physically fit and more active in your body. Part of that is preparation.

In 2020, I was having issues with my hands, and my hands have to navigate a touchscreen Magic Wall. It’s hard for me to explain because you can’t see it, but the few people who have been around me when I’m having some struggles could see it. I have to watch more closely. I have to follow my hand because I can’t trust it to get where it’s going to go because it’s not connected to my brain.

I don’t know what’s in front of you right now, but if you have a notebook or a glass of water, you see it and you reach for it and then you stop looking. You don’t watch your hand anymore. You know where your hand’s going, your hand knows where it’s going. It picks up your phone. It starts typing on your keypad. Sometimes I have to watch my hands. I have to watch my hand pick it up, and then I have to check to make sure I have a good grip on it because I can’t feel my fingers.

I can’t feel the extremities. And so I will squeeze something extra tight. Sometimes, if you watch me on television, if my feet seem abnormally wide in my gait when I’m walking, that means I’m having balance issues and I spread my legs a little bit. To the naked eye, nobody sees that. If you’re around me all the time, you might say, “John’s walking a little weird today.”

MS is really cruel to some people. MS sucks for me. MS challenges me, but I have been able to manage it thanks to good doctors and thanks to personal discipline. I call it my visitor. If my visitor is active on any given day, my attitude is, okay, well, I can’t kick you out, so I have to figure out how to deal with you, and you just do it.

As much as I can. And then, if I have to come back at it the next day like I did in 2020, I was going home and getting three or four hours sleep, and maybe I could’ve had five or six hours sleep, but I know that I have to do that 45 minutes or 50 minutes of exercise to be able to function. So I have to cut my sleep short to get the exercise. That’s the way I have to do it to function. I’m able to get through it. Could I do that for a month? I hope I never have to answer that question. I was able to do it for a week in 2020. 2024, you’re still doing stuff the days after the election. We just didn’t have that long drawn out uncertainty.

I do not have trouble falling asleep. Even when I’m having a pretty good MS week, I have never had trouble sleeping. When my brain knows I’m done, I’m off the set, I’m in my office, I’ve had whatever post-action meeting I have to have, my brain tells me, “You’re done.” That means I’m going to find a bed, and I will sleep. I’ve been blessed so far, and I assume this will continue, that when I need to get back at it, I just get back at it.

Attitude is everything, especially if you’re tired or you’re struggling or things aren’t going well. Could I give you a list of complaints about my life? Could I give you a couple of complaints about where I work? Of course I could. Everybody can. As somebody who sometimes goes down to the gym in the basement of our office, why isn’t the office gym a little better? I mean, get over yourself. I think attitude is everything.

I say this as a sinner. When I was initially diagnosed, I was taking that out on other people sometimes. There were times when it was either the stress of work—this can be a stressful business sometimes—or the stress and the fear. I was being a grumpy person, not being a good colleague. If you feel that, take a walk, get away and just have a good attitude. In traveling the last two years, when you see the struggles that working families are going through across this country right now, I’ve always considered myself to be the luckiest person in the world. And when I feel myself starting to complain, I just give myself a whack in the head and say, “Dude, get over it.”

Attitude is incredibly important. That’s not a hack. It’s not a secret. It’s not a supplement, but look yourself in the mirror every morning and just go to it. Live your day in a way that you can look yourself in the mirror at the end of the day, and hold the gaze.

#6623. Amazon’s Lego Deals Are Rarely This Good

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Growing up, my dad did all of his holiday shopping on Christmas Eve (and still largely does), which gave me anxiety and turned me into the person who gets all shopping done well before Thanksgiving. As a collector myself, I like to gift Lego sets because a) there’s legitimately a set for everyone, and b) I want everyone to love Lego as much as I do. I will not rest until they do.

This year, I’m not waiting for the big Black Friday sales, because to my surprise as a full-time deals tracker, I’m seeing a lot of great sets for up to 43 percent off on Amazon already. I know you’re probably groaning at the idea of holiday shopping now, but come December, you will thank me.

My favorite deal right now is on this set of two Mercedes vehicles; they’re detailed models that look like the real deal with grilles, wheels, and even the logo on the hoods. It makes a great gift for kids if you’re trying to get them into cars—or for your own mini car collection. It’s also retiring at the end of the year, so now’s the time to buy.

If you’re collecting Lego sports cars, try the Ferrari SF-24 F1 for beginners or the Ferrari FXX K Toy Car for more advanced builders. It’s also a great time to be a Star Wars fan: AT-AT Walker, R2-D2, Anzellan Starship, Millennium Falcon, and Darth Vader bust are all marked down for $80 or less.

I will say this: A lot of the bigger sets aren’t marked down right now, so if you’re holding out hope for even a small markdown there, you might need to wait until Black Friday proper. Otherwise, have at it, because the deals below are genuinely good. And popular sets do tend to sell out during big sales events like Black Friday (as I saw on Prime Day earlier this year). Don’t say I didn’t warn you.

#6622. Chris Rock on Directing an A24 Movie and ‘Crying Like a Bitch’ in Therapy

Chris Rock enters a room a bit diffidently, like he’s feeling out what’s inside, making quiet observations about the world around him that sometimes tip over into jokes and sometimes don’t. Because today we’re meeting on the 24th floor of the World Trade Center, I will not repeat the jokes. He looks around at the setup for the interview, which is being filmed: lights and cameras and microphones everywhere. “I just shot a movie with, like, three people,” he says. “It was much lower-budget than this.”

That movie is Misty Green, Rock’s fourth feature as a director. It’s about an actress named Misty, played by Rosalind Eleazar, who has talent and who has had success but who cannot quite get out of her own way. Rock identifies and he doesn’t. “I like to say there’s a part of me that’s been every character in the movie,” he says—including Misty’s best friend, played by Anna Kendrick, who is mostly there to crack jokes and report on her own unsatisfying romantic life. The film is a portrait of Hollywood that is dystopian but not cliché; Misty goes through hell, much of it of her own making, but her talent never dims. It has a lived, almost casual quality that belies Rock’s grasp of his craft: At 61, he hasn’t lost his eye for the telling, damning detail or hypocrisy, but there is also a warmth here, a genuine love for his characters. With Misty Green, you can feel both Rock’s lifetime of experience making things for audiences, and also something new—and more finely observed emerging.

All clothing, tie, and sunglasses by Tom Ford. Pocket square by Charvet.

Misty Green is part of a growing tradition in Rock’s work, including the previous film he directed, 2014’s Top Five, about the highs and lows of show business—something Rock has also chronicled, in exacting autobiographical detail, in his past two comedy specials: 2018’s Tamborine (which is about, among other things, Rock’s divorce) and 2023’s Selective Outrage (about, among other things, his encounter with Will Smith at the 2022 Academy Awards ceremony).

Rock has been at times the most famous man in all of comedy. His 1996 special Bring the Pain was a cultural phenomenon on a level few humans will ever experience. But as a director, he has increasingly been drawn to smaller ’70s-style character studies of people making an earnest attempt just to figure out themselves and their demons. “I’d love to make Man on Fire or something,” he says. “It’s not really my thing. I make a tailored suit. My suits are made by hand, by one guy. And it’s okay.”

This interview has been condensed and edited; watch this cover story—an episode of GQ’s One More Question—above or on YouTube, and listen on Spotify or Apple Podcasts.

Chris Rock: No, no. We’re going to be good. I think.

“How’d you get started?” “Were you funny in school?” Stuff like that. Whatever. I get it. You guys have a job. I’m not Harry Styles. Sometimes it requires a reintroduction.

I mean, it’s a thing you worry about every day. Am I still funny? Well—you don’t worry about being funny. You worry about being professionally funny. On a world-class level. I always had a joke: If I ever did a talk show, I’d do a morning show. I wouldn’t do a late-night show. Because I would only have to be funnier than Al Roker. You know what I mean? Al’s lovely, but I think I could take him in a joke-off.

You guys have a job, and the job is to get clickbait and things you can turn into controversy, and what’s the headline that has nothing to do with the actual article.

I mean, it’s your job, though. You might have better intentions, and I appreciate your interest. But a lot of what I read, it’s like: “Oh okay.” People want to talk about your personal life. I get it. We’re in a gossipy culture. We live in a world where Andy Cohen is Norman Lear and Ryan Murphy is Steven Spielberg. That is the world we live in. So people want gossip. We’re righteous people that follow nine commandments. And the one commandment we don’t follow is Thou shall not spread false witness. We are all about spreading false witness now. That is the world we live in. And that’s what journalism, or at least entertainment journalism, has become. Not that everything’s false, but it is definitely amplified to create sides, no matter what the issue is. How’s that? How am I doing?

Some of it is I got divorced. Nobody has a successful divorce, so that takes time. Then once it’s done, okay, you’re not sitting on the money you had before, so now you got to really make some money. So you go on tour, or two tours. So there was a lot of stand-up. There was a lot of getting my life together. You got a pandemic, you got a bunch of nonsense going on there.

I was not a great husband. I watched a lot of porn. I don’t watch porn anymore. I haven’t watched porn in five, six years.

It’s not anything I’m proud of, but it’s something I had to go through. It’s like you watch a Marvel movie, and the guy, he has to test his powers. You know what I mean? Every Marvel movie, man or woman, they have to test their powers. They’re assholes. They get these powers and they’re assholes, and they’re racing trains and they’re lifting up ocean liners and they try to go to the sun, then they crash down because they realize they’re not God. And then they start helping people. I tested my powers. I definitely realized I wasn’t God or anywhere near him, and I was severely humbled, as I should have been.

It’s basically Bring the Pain to Kill the Messenger. That’s one guy. Tamborine’s the beginning of a new guy. And that guy’s in fucking pain in Tamborine. That guy is damn near—Tamborine’s like the blues, man. Where Selective Outrage is like—there’s some pain in that one. Well, there’s a lot of pain in that one, but Tamborine is like: What have I become? [Starts singing Johnny Cash’s cover of Nine Inch Nails’ “Hurt.”] Goes away, in the end. I’m Johnny Cash with Rick Rubin, singing about my empire of dirt. That’s Tamborine. Tamborine is the fucking blues.

Yeah. I’m not one for a hint. You got to tell me exactly what the fuck you want. You have to tell me exactly what the fuck you mean.

A friend just kind of blurted it one day. You ever have somebody say something and you’re like, “Okay, let me get this looked into”? And it’s before Tamborine, but around that time. So I’m already quitting porn. You know what I mean? I’m already in a place where, “Okay, what the fuck’s wrong with me?” And I got a cognitive assessment test. And it’s like eight hours of testing to basically see—it’s the kind of thing you would get if you were bipolar or something. So it’s a lot of testing, and that’s what they came up with.

It made me realize how insensitive a lot of my behavior was throughout the years. Because it’s weird. I’ve got a friend who always says, this is a side joke, but he says, “When a schizophrenic sees a famous person, they go, ‘Ha, see? Told you!’ ” Mental illness and celebrity—it’s a weird combination there, because you think people are reacting a certain way because of celebrity. Because it’s this weird thing that you can’t quantify. So I used to just blame everything on celebrity. It’s like, “Oh, we’re not connecting because I had a chronic unique-ism.” I thought I was so fucking unique.

But also, okay, what are the symptoms? Black-and-white thinking. It’s an advantageous thing to have if you’re a comedian, black and white. Catastrophizing, great. You know what I mean? When you see one of those specials—okay, the average comedian, or even above average, probably does 40 shows before they record a special. And maybe they did 20 club things before this, so that’s 60 times onstage. My average special, I’ve gone onstage over 250 times. So I’m working it. It looks like I’m just, “Oh my God, he’s got this great insight.” But it’s like, “Yo man, I’m grinding this stuff.” Also, I have a hard time picking up on social cues, but I have a weirdly accurate ear. I can pick up on things in my ear. Sometimes I can pick up on a cue, but I can’t decipher it. I don’t know what just happened for a week, and I’m like, “Oh, that’s what she meant. Oh, that’s what he was trying to say.” It’s like my mind is working on the case. It just can’t solve it at that moment. Does that make sense?

Well, yeah. We do have this thing where everybody’s like—they brag about their therapy. Put it this way, my personal life wasn’t what I wanted it to be. And I’ll leave it at that. And that’s my relationship with everybody. So yeah, I went in there crying like a bitch. Like: What the fuck is going on?

You never come out. It’s an ongoing thing. You know what I mean? You’re always an alcoholic. So it’s a combination of therapy and what I have, NVLD, nonverbal learning disorder. So I have a person that helps me with that, and I have another therapist. I mean, it’s okay.

I mean, it’s not like a threat or anything. I’m just saying it’s okay. Whatever happened happened. Everybody saw it. I don’t really like talking about it. By the way—any awards show.

Yeah. Something happened. It wasn’t cool. And I would like to move on. I would like to—I had a nice career before it, and I know there’s a part of that that’s going to follow me the rest of my life. I got jokes. I got scripts. I got movie scripts. I got TV shows. I’m writing a book. I’m doing shit. I have a plethora of art I’m trying to get out, and that’s what I want you to pay attention to, not some crazy night.

I don’t get a lot of stuff. I honestly don’t. I mean, I probably get more offers as a director, to tell you the truth, than as an actor. It’s weird. When you’re really famous, it works for you and it works against you. So you got name recognition, but you also have people thinking they know what you can do and thinking that there’s a ceiling on your talent. So you got to fight that. The hardest thing you can do in show business is change perception. Reinvention is the hardest thing. So it’s kind of relatively easy to get famous, in the sense that it’s twice as hard to become a new person that is already famous. You know what I mean? The John Travolta that was on Welcome Back, Kotter, that was probably easy compared to the guy that did Pulp Fiction. Like: Wow, I got to change everybody’s perception about who I am. I don’t get a lot of offers, but it’s okay.

They don’t really make comedies anymore. Not like they used to. I don’t want to be [cast in the role of] the judge. Plus, I’m at this weird place. People are like, “How old is he?”

I’m older than Morgan Freeman was in Shawshank. I’m older than Danny Glover was in Lethal Weapon. You know what I mean? So it’s like: “Is he the guy, or is he like Michael Douglas in Ant-Man?” Like: You go get him, Antie.

Hey, I’ll do it. I’ll shine Michael B. Jordan’s guns. “All right. They’re ready. They’re ready.” I don’t give a fuck. If that’s where I am now, you got to make these adjustments. I’m just trying to stay in the league, man. So I don’t get 25 shots anymore. Now I’m just boxing out. I’m playing good defense. Come in off the bench.

Oh, I totally identify. I mean, I like to say there’s a part of me that’s been every character in the movie. So I’m her, I’m the agent, I’m my character. I’m Anna Kendrick, even. I’m everybody in there. And yeah, Misty’s going through what I just said. It is hard when people actually think they know who you are to give you another chance, to think you’re worthy of another chance. And she doesn’t make it easy for herself. You know what I mean?

I mean, not to that extent. But here’s the thing about self-destructive behavior. Can you be nice while you’re doing it? Some addicts are better than others. I’ve never been that guy. I’ve never been poison on a set. I would even say, and I say this about most actors, 40, 50 percent of your work’s going to be from friends, from people just going, “Hey, you know who’s really cool to hang out with? Chris.” You know what I mean?

I’ve been in that audition. And then Kadeem Hardison walked in the room. You’re like, Kadeem! When I write a Hollywood thing—and I’m not trying to diss anybody—I’ve actually worked jobs. Most of my friends that work in show business have never worked a fucking real job. And if they worked a real job, they were working their way through college, knowing good and well they weren’t going to be there [forever]. So they never really thought the job was going to be their life. So when I write Hollywood, I’m still writing the guy that worked at Red Lobster. I’m writing the guy that loaded trucks at the Daily News. These are jobs that I had. It’s not a Hollywood thing. It’s an everywhere thing.

Why’d I cast myself? I don’t know. I guess I could have given it to somebody. I did think about it. It’s just like, “Eh, it’s not that time yet.”

You know what? It was like a quick on-set thing, basically, so I’m not the same guy in Top Five. A lot of directors, a lot of Black directors, have this hat uniform. They have a hat, and the hat’s got the name on it. So it’s Spike. It was the Hughes brothers. It was Matty Rich. There’s this uniform in being a Black director sometimes. It’s my homage to Robert Townsend and Reggie Hudlin and Malcolm Lee, and all these guys.

I mean, a little. I had a brother who struggled with alcohol, yes. It’s a lot of other people I’ve met. We just have so much mental illness now. And there’s something about—it’s weird. When you’re the sibling of somebody like that, or even my brother, your friends, everybody around you, only sees the drunk person, only sees the manic schizophrenic person, but you always see the person you grew up with. You still remember this brother that was like your best friend, and you don’t give that up. No matter what behavior you see, you’re just holding on [to] all the hope in the world that you can get that brother back. And that’s what Misty goes through. A lot of people are like, “Well, just leave him.” It’s like, “Nah, man. You didn’t know him like I knew him.”

I know some dead people. I know a lot of dead people. How old are you?

I’ll give you about 12 years, 15 years. You start losing the people that just don’t eat right. Just as simple as that. It’s not even the addicts who start going. And it’s not even that they OD; it’s just at some point your body can’t fight this fight anymore. So [in your] 50s, you start losing people. You start losing people to natural causes sometimes. It’s crazy.

There’s a weird statistic I read a long time ago. Of the 45 presidents, like 35 of them lost a father or a parent early in life. It makes you grow up. It’ll make you or break you. So yeah, it sobered me up somewhat. My father died in ’88; ’90, I’m on SNL. You know what I mean? So it’s like, Get serious, figure it out. It’s time to figure it out.

I was successful, I guess. But you got to also remember, at that time, all of us were in the shadow of Eddie Murphy. That’s what success was.

How do I explain it? I bought a house a year and a half, maybe two years before, bought a carriage house in Brooklyn, and I couldn’t afford it. And so I just had to get onstage a lot, and I had to work every gig. You ever read the Gladwell book? The Beatles basically just did more shows than everybody. So it was the Beatles, the Kinks, all these other bands. And the Beatles had the worst boss. The Beatles just did way more shows than everybody. We try to make it into this mythical thing. It’s kind of the same thing, man. I bought a house, and I had to fucking get onstage. Two things happened. I had to get onstage to pay for this house, but I also, privately anyway, I had quit show business in a sense.

I had given up because I’d done CB4, I’d done New Jack City, I’d been on SNL, and I was like, “Okay, I’m going to just be a comedian.” Not that there was any shame to it, but my goal was to be George Wallace. George Wallace is a great comedian, and I love George Wallace because all he did was stand-up and he had a real schedule in his life. Most comedians have no schedule, right? George Wallace works from here to here. He goes on vacation these months of the year. I kind of, in the back of my mind, always wanted a family, always wanted kids. So that was my goal. I do this special, and it’s hard to even explain. The world went crazy.

Here’s a funny story. I got Bring the Pain. I have a VHS of Bring the Pain. I left my agent, and I left my manager, but I have Bring the Pain. Now nobody knows what it is. It’s very similar to Dre and Snoop walking around with The Chronic, trying to get a deal. And by the way, it’s around the same time. So I go to this agent. I’m not going to say his name, whatever. He’s made up with me since. I still got a little ego, and I’m like, “Hey, I’d like you to be my agent.” I give him the Bring the Pain tape. He watches it. He says, “It’s okay.” He goes, and I quote, “I got too many Black guys.” He said, “I got too many Black guys.” Whatever. Cut to two months later, Bring the Pain airs and it’s like this big thing. I’m on Oprah, I’m on 60 Minutes, and that agent calls me back, “Hey. I’ll handle you for 5 percent.”

I thought about it, but then I couldn’t do it. I couldn’t do it. I got too many Black guys. A guy actually said that to me.

Bigger & Blacker is bigger than Bring the Pain. It’s like, what if Bad sold more than Thriller? I got a nice body of work. I’m fine with Head of State, I’m fine with I Think I Love My Wife, [which are] the two films before Top Five, but I think I could have been more serious about directing. That’s the only thing I would say. I never let my ego mess up my stand-up. Every special, I put a ton of work into. Kill the Messenger, I remember Mike Nichols calling me and he said, “I like it, but you got onstage too much. It’s like: You worked it too much. And I can see at points you’re a little robotic. You don’t need to work it that hard.” But yeah, I was learning a lot of things on the fly. I didn’t know anything about money. Lorne Michaels said to me a long time ago, “Everybody loses their first money. But if you’re talented, you’ll make more.” I knew nothing about just the dynamics of fame and how it affected the people around you and all that stuff. It was a lot.

No, I’m not doing it for money. I mean, I’ll make money off of it, but the thing with Misty Green, what I realized when I did Top Five, it’s like: This is my lane as a filmmaker. I’d love to make Man on Fire or something. I would love to make fill-in-the-blank big-budget movie and have this big-budget Rush Hour–esque idea. Those guys are really good at that. It’s not really my thing. I make a tailored suit. My suits are made by hand, by one guy. And it’s okay. A little movie is what I should probably be doing, and it’s where my interest is. And some of those other movies I did was kind of me trying to get famous.

Robert De Niro’s like 84. He’s got a little baby. He’s my man. He’s got literally a two-year-old or something. I mean, I never plan too much ahead. I’m just trying to get as much work done as possible. Having kids that are grown, it seems like I got a little more time on my hands. So hopefully that results in more work. I’m probably going to direct more. I don’t know how much [more] stand-up I’m going to do.

I got like six, seven specials, man. That’s a lot. So I don’t know. I’m not announcing anything or anything, but if I do it, I want to do it right. In sports, especially in football, they always talk about separation. “Can he get separation?” You know what I mean? “Yeah, he’s fast, but is he fast enough? Can he get separation?” And that’s the way I feel, like, as far as stand-up. As long as I can get separation, I’ll do it. Yeah, I’m going to write. That’s basically what it boils down to. Wake up, write. That’s it. Just write, write, write, write, write. Get it all out. ✦

#6614. Please Just Tell Me Who the Next James Bond Is Already

Callum Turner, Jack Lowden, and Josh O’Connor are reportedly all up for the role. But I’m not having fun anymore.

Some movie announcements arrive as a complete surprise. Ryan Gosling as Ghost Rider? Very cool idea. Nathan Fielder secretly spending weeks staring into Elizabeth Holmes’s eyes for You Can See Everything? Amazing. In other instances, film studios will delay an announcement everyone knows is coming until their audience finds itself sucked up into the maelstrom and spun around until they’re so dizzy that they’re puking up that morning’s Cheerios. Maybe they wish they never even got excited in the first place.

That’s where I’m at with the current race for James Bond, which has reportedly come down to three actors: Jack Lowden (Slow Horses), Callum Turner (One Night Only), and Josh O’Connor (Disclosure Day). The three finalists were announced via Puck three weeks ago on October 22, along with a mysterious fourth actor whose identity has not yet been revealed, if he even exists.

Since then, there have been no official updates from Amazon/MGM—which acquired full control of the franchise in February 2025—but there have been dozens upon dozens of interviews from former Bond actors as well as anyone remotely associated with the prior 25 Bond films and novels, with each interviewee expressing that all three men up for the role would probably be great.

“All the men who are on the list now are actors I admire,” Pierce Brosnan recently told Vanity Fair. Maybe true but after a year of this guessing game, it just isn’t fun anymore. Even worse, no one seems concerned with even making it fun.

“It's an incredibly bizarre pinch-me moment... even being uttered in that same sentence and being considered for something like that,” Josh O’Connor told BBC News on Tuesday. “But I am afraid, there is nothing to say beyond that.”

Callum Turner was even more direct: “I’m not going to comment on it just because there’s no point in commenting on it in this moment,” he told Esquire UK in August.

Then, Lowden uttered “Oh, Christ” in an interview earlier this week when he finally addressed the rumors. “It’s all very flattering. It’s nice to be mentioned—for any role—and I’m just not really going to add to the noise.”

Are they not allowed to even say they want the role? Am I supposed to act excited when they finally announce if they don’t even seem excited themselves? Do we even want to see a film with Turner, Lowden, or O’Connor as James Bond?

Please add to the noise, guys. I’m begging you. It’s time to tell us.

#6613. GQ’s 2026 Men of the Year Celebration Begins Now

GQ’s December issue has arrived, and with it comes our annual Men of the Year blowout. Except, for the 31st year, we’re going bigger than ever.

#6610. A Straight Razor Will Give You the Best Shave of Your Life. Here Are 6 Great Options to Buy.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

If you get razor bumps and ingrowns, don’t be afraid to go old-school.

We’re 120 years past the invention of the safety razor and 50 years past the invention of the cartridge razor. So yes, we understand how annoying it is to even suggest that you should shave with the thing that’s even older—technically as old as shaving itself. Go ahead, make fun of the old-school dandy men’s magazine saying you should be straight-razor shaving with a shaving cream that requires a boar hair brush and smells of tobacco. But to turn shaving from a chore to something you look forward to, well, the straight razor is a one-way ticket to hobby town.

Using an electric razor or beard trimmer is great for getting rid of scruff, but if you ever go for a clean shave, a straight razor is the best tool—full stop. They are the best for your face, don’t give you as many ingrown hairs, and (unlike the five-bladed cartridge razors) don’t get clogged up with hairs after two shaves. Plus they add a sense of elegance to your everyday routine, not to mention a bit of danger, à la Sweeney Todd. Only joking, of course.

So sure, challenge our claim that straight-razor shaving is as good as it gets. Or better yet, try it for a little bit and then tell us we’re wrong. If you clean shave and care about grooming, consider picking up a straight razor and doing it the old-fashioned way.

Frankly, most of these straight razors aren’t even technically straight razors. They are shavettes; hybrids with disposable blades so you don’t have to worry about honing and sharpening (and all the hazards that come with failing to do so). You still get the straight razor vibe, but it’s safer and more convenient. For a starter shavette, you can pick up the Parker SRX straight edge. It’s compatible universally with any double edge blades, so you won’t ever run out.

Take care of this stainless steel beauty and it will retain that sheen for years without a sign of rust. This all makes it a great, affordable pick for intermediate shavers who prioritize performance. For a more gateway razor, I’d pick the Dovo shavette

Fundamentally, this is the same thing as the previous Parker razor. It’s a shavette that uses double-edge disposable blades, so the experience of using it and the accompanying learning curve are the exact same. The difference is the wood handle. It looks great, but if you get anything other than shaving cream on there, it’s certainly not going to be as easy to clean as stainless steel. I still wouldn’t recommend a novice pick this up, but that’s what the safety razor is for.

If you are genuinely just curious about this whole thing, you don’t need to drop even $30 on something you may use once and then put down forever. You can pick up a Classic Samurai razor from Amazon for less than 15 bucks. It’s not the best ever, and you won’t be showing off to your friends, but it’s a way to get your feet wet. Plus it comes with enough extra (safety-razor-compatible) blades that if you do find the knack for straight-razor shaving, you can upgrade to a Parker or Dovo and have plenty of blades to use.

The straight handle is your less flexible, slightly sturdier alternative to your folding shavette. This Feather razor is Japanese made with a straight grip meant to give more fine tuning and control to the average shaver. I only wish that, like Parker shavettes, it was compatible with all double-blade replacements—not just the Feather branded ones.

Whether straight handles are actually easier to control and safer than folding ones is up for debate. Barbers have their preferences and if you are looking to start shaving with a straight razor at home, so will you. If you’ve tried the folding shavettes and can’t quite get a handle (sorry for the pun) on them, this one is worth a shot.

As we exit the shavette category, I turn once again to Dovo. If you want a razor that looks and feels as traditional as it gets while still being brand new, you need a Dovo.

This isn’t for beginners or really even intermediate shavers. If anything, it’s more of a novelty. If you want to use it for real, you should probably be a real barber. If you have the skills (and the disposable income), this will be something fun to whip out for your favorite clients in need of the cleanest shave of their life.

There’s something uniquely satisfying about buying vintage. These come from the era when these were the only razors men used to shave. In a way, that’s how they know they are quality. Just old-fashioned stainless steel blades and a design that modern “retro” can’t come close to replicating.

You can buy these (one per package despite the website’s imagery) sealed or opened and cleaned Friedrich Schlemper straight razors for less than the price of a Dovo. These Sistrum razors are German-made carbon steel, likely dating back to sometime between the 1950s and the 1970s.

Classic Straight Razor: Traditionally, a straight razor has one steel (typically stainless or carbon) blade. In the old days, before the safety and cartridge razors we know and love, this was the only option. Barbers had to hone and sharpen their blades using a leather strap—and if you go classic this is still a requirement. This makes a straight razor an investment that can go south if you don’t take care of it properly. Serious professionals only—it’s not easy to learn how to shave safely with one of these. There’s a reason that as a society, we don’t use them anymore.

Shavettes: This modern hybrid of the safety razor and straight razor is what I recommend over the classic style. They are much more affordable, for starters, and the replaceable blades are much safer, especially for at-home experimentation. If you are a casual looking into straight razors, I cannot stress enough to start and end with a shavette.

Folding vs Straight Handle: In terms of shavettes, it all comes down to preference. A straight handle can be preferable to a lighter, airy folding blade handle due to better ergonomics and greater control. But others find a folding handle to allow them better control and stability. It’s all about whichever you feel allows you more precision while leading to fewer errors.

Esquire has been helping men look (and be) at their best since the heyday of the straight razor. We lived through it. If 90 years of institutional knowledge isn’t going to convince you, nothing will.

#6609. The Women Who “Stopped the Wedding”—and Never Looked Back

“I'd already bought my wedding dress, asked my bridesmaids, and started imagining what was supposed to be one of the happiest days of my life,” 27-year-old Sacha Smith tells me. “I just didn't know that the relationship I believed I was in wasn't the reality.”

After discovering her fiancé had been cheating on her for the entirety of their engagement, Smith called off the wedding. She is one of hundreds of women who—as part of a viral TikTok trend—shared their story of coming to their senses and ending their relationship right before a wedding.

In these TikTok videos, women share photos and videos of themselves in their previous relationships—sometimes even trying their wedding dresses on—with the words of Ashe’s viral song “Stop the Wedding!” playing in the background. In the song, Ashe sings, “Even though the table's set, the guests are waiting, you can burn the dress and run away. Stop the wedding.” The song is catchy, but it’s also a warning cry to women that it’s never too late to save yourself from a lifetime of misery tied to the wrong person.

And it often actually takes something as monuments as an impending wedding to figure out a relationship isn’t working. “When you've spent years overriding your own feelings to maintain relationships, it can become really difficult to recognize what you actually want,” Cheryl Groskopf, LMFT tells Cosmopolitan. “A wedding makes everything feel real and tangible because now you're facing the possibility of committing to this dynamic for the rest of your life. And sometimes that forces you to confront something you've been trying really hard to avoid.”

This was the case for Katherine Woller, 39. There was no cheating partner or big fallout—just a gut feeling she couldn’t ignore. The feeling started the moment her boyfriend got down on one knee. Even with the gorgeous backdrop of Champagne, France, she couldn’t shake the thought that she was making a mistake by saying yes.

It was only two weeks before her wedding when Woller stood in front of the mirror wearing her would-be wedding dress during a fitting, feeling utterly detached. “I felt completely out of control and lost, as if I were watching someone else’s life,” she says. “At that moment, I couldn’t ignore my inner voice any longer and knew it was time to make a big, hard choice.”

Despite the magnitude of an “I shouldn’t be doing this” feeling, a wedding can make it seem impossible to walk away. This is called a Sunk Cost Fallacy. And even though, via its label you understand it isn’t true, in the moment, it feels like you’ve put too much in to the relationship to leave it. Therapist Justena Kavanagh, LCSW explains that once you’re engaged, the deposits, the guest list, and other people’s expectations build momentum. “Stopping can feel harder than going through with it.”

Tishayla Williams was 30 years old when she was engaged. She had bought the dress, finalized the venue, and was the only woman in her family who wasn’t married or a mother. The pressure was very real.

But, when she had been followed out of a cafe by a strange man and called her fiancé crying, his first response had been, “What were you wearing?”

“I told him to come collect his two-carat engagement ring,” she says.

When your gut is screaming at you that you’re with the wrong person, ignoring it feels like abandoning yourself. This is why so many women stop the wedding—and why Ashe wants to remind us that despite our fears of letting others down, it’s always better to walk away.

“I realized that the money spent and the embarrassment of cancelling a wedding were nothing compared to the cost of marrying someone who had betrayed my trust,” Smith says. “I’d much rather have a wedding dress I never wore than enter a marriage that was false.”

Women who call off a wedding usually think that facing their family and friends will be the most difficult part. But Smith, Woller, and Williams all agree that their loved ones met them with grace and understanding. Woller even says that confiding in her friends and family led to closer connections and more vulnerability.

“I think the hardest part was accepting that the future I'd imagined wasn't going to happen,” Smith says. “I think people underestimate how much emotion goes into that. You picture the day, the people who will be there, the life you'll build afterwards, and even the family you might have together.”

And despite knowing that the relationship is wrong or that calling the wedding off is the right decision, it doesn’t make ending the relationship any easier. “Even when you’re certain, you’re still human,” Williams says. “I spent the first few days sad, wondering if I’d walked away from my one chance at a fairy tale.”

In the part of the audio that doesn’t usually make it to the TikTok videos, Ashe sings, “This isn't cold feet, these aren't fireworks. The sky is menacing and that's an iceberg.”

But nerves and wedding day jitters are always advertised to us as a normal part of your big day. How do you tell when it’s something more?

“Cold feet are usually about the commitment itself: the finality, the change in identity, the idea of ‘forever,’” Kavanagh explains. “A bad gut feeling is about the person: how they treat you, whether you feel safe and respected, and patterns you’ve been explaining away.”

Psychologist Dr. Karen Stewart explains that anxiety alone isn’t typically proof that something is wrong in the relationship. Instead, if you feel like you can’t trust them, don’t feel emotionally safe with them, or have unresolved patterns of conflict, these are all typically signs that the relationship isn’t sturdy enough to support a marriage. She also emphasizes that foundational issues in a relationship will not magically improve after a wedding. In fact, they’ll typically only get worse.

Some people are just anxious, and it can be hard to trust their gut feelings. But when you analyze where your anxiety is coming from, you can typically pinpoint whether it’s just your body reacting to a huge life choice or something deeper about your relationship.

“Sometimes we know far more than we are willing to admit,” Woller says.

It always comes back to thinking about the connection and compatibility you have with your partner. “If you can picture yourself five years from now, crying in the bathroom at 3 a.m., wondering why the hell you married this person—there’s your answer,” Williams says.

Calling a wedding off can be devastating. You’ve often spent months (or even years) planning a day, telling everyone you know, and picturing a future with this person that you love. Losing all of that at once can feel like something you’ll never get over.

But this is why Ashe sings over and over again that none of it matters. Everything is secondary to your happiness and emotional safety. And as you can see through the countless TikTok videos, deciding to call off a wedding can save your life.

“I genuinely believe it was both the worst and best thing that's ever happened to me,” Smith says. Since calling off her wedding, she’s traveled extensively, made strides in her career, and learned more about herself than ever before.

Williams feels the same: “I’ve spent the years since investing in myself and my career, and I’ve learned that life doesn’t begin with a proposal or end with a canceled wedding.”

Although Woller regrets how she handled certain things in that season of her life—and is even sorry for the pain she caused her former partner—she’s learned to move on from the guilt and shame she felt in the aftermath of breaking her engagement.

“I still feel 100 percent confident that it was the right decision for both of us, and I am thankful every day that I dared to make such a life-altering choice.”

#6608. Owen Wilson Breaks Down the World’s Best Golf Courses

The PGA Tour may be wrapping up in just a few short weeks, but this week marks the official beginning of Stick Season. No, not the Noah Kahan song, but Owen Wilson’s charming Apple TV+ comedy, whose second season premieres this Wednesday, November 4th. The show follows Wilson’s Pryce “Stick” Cahill, a former pro golfer who crashed out in a Kenny Powers-esque blaze of almost-glory a couple decades ago. Season one tracked his reentry into the game as the mentor of a talented but troubled young golfer played by Peter Dager. But in Season two, Stick has his eyes on a new prize: becoming the oldest player to ever win the U.S. Open.

Wilson himself might not be winning majors any time soon, but his experience filming the show has turned him into a real golf nut. He hadn’t played much beforehand, he told me, so the last couple of years have been a real crash course in one of the most difficult sports in the world. Still, it hasn’t taken much for him to compile a list of his favorite courses—and a bucket list of ones he’s dying to play a round or two on. Ahead of the show’s return, the 57-year-old actor hit the links with GQ.

“I have gotten an invite from a member to play, so I think that will happen at some point. What's always funny about that is that they close right after The Masters, so they're closed all summer. I’ve got to find a time that works for them where I could go play. That would be incredible.”

“My brother says St. Andrews and some of the other courses in Scotland are incredible. Out there the links style is pretty different from the courses in Dallas. And the people in Scotland, Luke says, are so knowledgeable about the game and friendly. I have not played link style and sometimes I wonder, Well, maybe my game would suit that because sometimes I'm not getting a lot of lift on the ball. And I am very comfortable with a putter. So yeah, maybe that's where I'll really prove that I'm good at the game.”

“Playing in Makena on Maui, that's a beautiful course that I love playing where you're getting to see the ocean and the beautiful Hawaiian features.”

“I played this one in Sydney that's kind of their [version of] Pebble Beach. It’s on the ocean, and there are some dramatic holes where you see waves breaking. I played lousy that day, but I'd like another crack at it.”

“I remember asking Collin Morikawa what his favorite course was and he said Riviera. Getting to play Riviera, I kind of feel like I've gotten to know more about the game. In the beginning, it was sort of lost on me—like getting a really good bottle of wine, and I couldn't really tell the difference. But now that I'm getting better, I can appreciate how incredible that course is. That first hole, where you're kind of teeing off from way up above. Just the other day, on number 10, I had a par four where I drove the green and just barely made it to the front of the green. But I was immediately on the phone calling to let my brothers and friends know that I had an eagle putt, which I missed. But that kind of kicked off a good little stretch where I birdied three out of four holes. That was a rare kind of day. More often, I'm kind of experiencing how challenging golf is.”

“I’ve stayed [at the Manor] and the course where they’re going to hold the Ryder Cup next year looked beautiful.”

“When I was training for the show, I learned how to play at Maui Country Club, a little nine-hole course kind of near where I am on the North Shore. [Maui] and Mākena, those were the two places that I really played a lot.”

“I played my best round at King Kamehameha with [former NBA coach] Don Nelson. I'd only been playing for a year-and-a-half where I was able to shoot in the low 80s, and everything just was very simple that day. Like, you hit your drive on the fairway, and then on the fairway you just hit it on the green, and then you two-putt and move on. I was with Nellie when he got his last hole in one. At that point I think he was maybe 83, and he was able to shoot his age and get a hole in one. I have not gotten a hole in one yet, but it's very exciting to witness one.”

#6605. This Fan-Slash-White Noise Machine Solves Two Big Sleep Problems at Once

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

For as long as I can remember, I’ve had trouble sleeping through the night. I’ve tried taking supplements, cutting down on screen time before bed, and changing out my sheets to the softest and most lightweight ones I could find. I’ve even been to a sleep specialist. And while some of these things have helped me out a bit, it’s still pretty rare for me to fall asleep within an hour of getting into bed, and I almost never sleep through the whole night without waking up a few times.

I’ve tried white noise machines in the past, but I never found one that I truly stuck with. That changed, however, when Snooz sent me its new Breez 2 bedroom fan and white noise machine. I was immediately intrigued and excited to try it out, since my boyfriend swears by his Snooz original white noise machine—he says it’s the only thing that helps him sleep in his first-floor, street-facing apartment in midtown New York City. Plus, I’d just put my AC unit in storage for the fall and winter and desperately needed some airflow in my bedroom, so I thought, why not give it a shot?

Long story short: I’m really glad I decided to try it out, and I’ve been using it every night for the past few weeks. What makes this different from your typical white noise machine is that you can separately control Breez 2’s fan and sound, so you can use both at the same time or just one or the other depending on your needs. For example, I don’t need the white noise on during the day, but I keep the fan on to have a nice breeze going. At night, I’ve been using both, but once it starts to get a bit colder, I may just need the white noise without the fan.

And speaking of the fan, I’ve been quite pleasantly surprised with its power, considering it takes up less than a square foot. This thing actually blasts a breeze of cold air across my whole room (granted, I have a shoebox bedroom in a flexed Manhattan apartment), not just a little puff of air one foot in front of the fan. And without the white noise on, the fan makes a smooth whirring sound that’s not too loud, so if you do want a pretty quiet space, you can certainly achieve that.

When it comes to the controls, Breez 2 really sets itself apart from your typical fan or sound machine, thanks to the Bluetooth-connected app on my phone that makes it possible for me to adjust it from wherever I am (within Bluetooth connectivity, of course)—which means I can control it while I’m in bed and don’t have to get up in the middle of the night just to change the settings. Too hot? I can turn the fan up while I’m under the covers. Too loud? I can turn the volume down without even sitting up.

And if I really don’t want to do any work and let Breez 2 handle everything, there’s even an auto mode in which it detects the temperature of your room and adjusts the fan accordingly so it never gets too hot or cold. All you have to do is set a target temperature, and the fan will turn higher or lower to achieve that temperature.

I’ve also been making use of the timer feature and setting Breez 2 to turn off after two hours, so I don’t waste electricity while I’m asleep. The only issue here is that you can’t set a timer for just the fan or the white noise; it turns off the device altogether, so if I do want the fan running all night, I just have to keep the sound on, too. However, you can use the schedule feature to set a schedule for the fan and white noise ahead of time, so if you know you go to bed and wake up at the same time on certain days, you can go ahead and set that up. My sleep schedule is a little too wonky for that (but perhaps fixing that would be a good idea . . .).

Finally, from an aesthetic standpoint, it has a nice, modern, minimalist design with a light wood stand and a white body—there’s a very Scandinavian style to it. The controls on the face of the fan react with a light touch and light up, so they can even be used as a calming night-light if you want (the brightness of which can be adjusted in the app). So yeah, Snooz has really thought of everything here.
