#6603. We Found the Ultimate Cold-Weather Cologne

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Fulton & Roark's new scent, inspired by a church in Gary, Indiana, is phenomenal.

Welcome to the Esquire Endorsement. Heavily researched. Thoroughly vetted. These picks are the best way to spend your hard-earned cash.

Halloween is in the rearview, Daylight Saving Time is over, and the sun has decided it has better places to be (although I am grateful for that extra hour of sleep we got). You know what all that means: It’s officially time to bring out the poofy coats, the cable-knit sweaters, and the winter scents. We’re talking spicy, smoky, sophisticated, and a little dark. Think of it as the olfactory equivalent of a cashmere scarf or quilted jacket. But before you reach for the same old colognes of winters past, consider making room for something new in your fragrance rotation.

Allow me to suggest my new cold-weather go-to, released just last month by one of my all-time favorite niche fragrance houses, Fulton & Roark. This extrait has a pretty sick name: Cathedral of Ruins—and it smells every bit as atmospheric as you’d hope for with a name like that. It’s moody without being gloomy, warm without being cloying, and oh-so intriguing. Put a spritz of this stuff on and you’re guaranteed to start hearing the words, What are you wearing?

Fulton & Roark does what it calls “American fine fragrance,” meaning it doesn’t follow all the rules of your classic European perfumery. The perfumer looks to new ingredients, formulation methods, and scent combinations for all of its fragrances. Oh, and every fragrance is unisex, truly unisex. My boyfriend loves the scents as much as I do, and we often share them.

And since this is American fine fragrance we’re talking about, it only makes sense that each of the brand’s fragrances are named after and inspired by a real American place with a story behind it. That rule even holds for the brand’s name: Fulton is for Fulton County, Georgia; and Roark is for Roark’s Cove in Sewanee, Tennessee. So where is this Cathedral of Ruins? Gary, Indiana. Birthplace of the Jackson 5, and our clearest example of Rust Belt deindustrialization. The once-booming steel city is now also home to thousands of abandoned and decaying buildings, including the City Methodist Church—once one of the grandest churches in the Midwest.

Fulton and Roark co-founder Kevin Keller showed me photos of what City Methodist Church looks like now, which he took on his own trip to Gary, and the immediate sense I got from these photos was a devastating sort of beauty (or a beautiful sort of devastation). The church’s roof has fallen in; the only living congregation is the vegetation that reclaims more and more of the space every year. But, its Neo-Gothic stone walls still stand. It’s a dead relic, but it is not a dead space. It pulls up mixed feelings. This Cathedral of Ruins extrait de parfum is Fulton & Roark’s homage to it, and it nails everything you get from seeing a picture of the place.

Like I said, Cathedral of Ruins is the perfect cold-weather scent. But instead of the typical rich, gourmand notes you get in fall and winter fragrances like vanilla, caramel, and amber, Cathedral of Ruins relies on spices, smoke, and woods to create a warm atmosphere. This is not a syrupy sweet winter scent—it’s smoldering, Gothic-inspired, sophisticated, and unlike any scent you’ve ever smelled before.

At the top, you get resinous cypress, spicy black peppercorn, and, unexpectedly, floral rose. The lush, velvety notes of rose lead into the more delicate floral notes of saffron, symbolizing the grace of the cathedral (and possibly the new natural growth of vegetation over the ruins). Rich Peru balsam finishes out the heart with more warm, woody vibes.

But the base is where this fragrance really stands out. The star of the show here is burned frankincense, the classic scent of sacred ritual—imagine smoke rising above the walls, past the ceiling that’s no longer there. Frankincense is, obviously, smoky, but not in the same way as something like tobacco; the frankincense here is dry, resinous, and subtly fresh like pine. To me, frankincense even has a waxy-green quality similar to olives. The warm depth of leathered woods and musks finish out the fragrance, leaving the intrigue of smoky woods to linger on your skin.

#6598. 16 Best Automatic Watches for Men 2026, According to GQ Watch Nerds

A budding “watch guy” most likely starts off their collection with an assortment of quartz tickers (and let’s be clear, there’s nothing wrong with that). But as your interest in watches grows, it’s only natural for you to want something on your wrist you can brag about, which inevitably pushes you towards automatic watches.

Inside the case of an automatic watch is a weighted rotor that spins as you move your arm, a few little flicks of your wrist throughout the day keeps the internal mechanisms running long enough to keep that minute hand tick-tick-ticking across the dial.

But automatic watches also have a reputation for being finicky or costly. And while that can be true, the beauty of watches is the wide price scale in which the industry operates. From stunners for less than $250 to ones you’d have to mortgage your house (and maybe your parents’ house) to buy, here are the top automatic watches on the market today for any budget.

While not impossible to find a great watch at the $250-mark, you are going to be limited. Given the many internal pieces and complicated mechanisms needed to build an automatic watch, many brands have to charge a bit more for a quality automatic. Even so, brands like Orient and Seiko lead the way on well-made (and well-designed) options.

Orient’s range of inexpensive watches punch well above their weight-class and the Symphony III is no different. With its classic, clean design and movement that promises 40 hours of power reserve, this is a watch that’s made to be a daily driver without breaking the bank.

Timex’s Waterbury is a staple in their line-up and the extremely stylish Metrograph has an Art Deco-style case and dial that’s pared-down enough to keep things simple, but without looking boring.

The Seiko 5 is something of a cult favorite for the Japanese brand – and for good reason. It’s a perfect daily driver and a budget-friendly alternative to the Rolex Datejust. Best of all, it clocks in at just 37mm, which is the perfect case size for nearly any wrist.

As ever, Timex provides maximum bang for minimum buck with this classic "Pepsi" bezel diver.

At $500, the doorway starts to open up a bit more – but only a creak. The usual suspects like Timex and Casio, are populating this price point, Bulova and Sternglas are great alternatives who will give you a little more street cred among collectors.

Undoubtedly one of the best looking watches in the sub-$500 category, the Timex Marlin in this particular shade of green strikes the perfect balance between classic and modern.

Heavily influenced by motorsports, the Edifice is a bit of a departure from the digital, retro-charm of many mainstays for Casio. The integrated bracelet keeps it sporty, while the patterned dial makes it a little more interesting than other options on the market.

At 45mm, 200-meter of water resistance, and 42-hours of power reserve, the Bulova Marine Star ticks all the boxes for a great dive watch at a reasonable price – the two-toned bezel is just a nice bonus.

This fan favorite hits the bullseye of the style-quality-price nexus, and looks especially fetching with a bright yellow dial.

A thousand bucks is a perfectly reasonable budget to have when you want to upgrade your watch game without completely breaking the bank. Here, I’d recommend looking at smaller, or more indie brands to find some interesting designs not seen by more household names.

You’d be forgiven for not knowing their name, but Kuoe is starting to gain traction in the U.S.. The Japanese-based watchmaker produces high-quality, well-designed pieces that harken to a more classic watch style. The OLD SMITH is based on the military field watch, but with a slightly more refined design language.

A reborn grail from the Studio 54 era, with a vintage-correct 35mm case.

UNIMATIC uses the basic field watch as the springboard for their Modello Due, which strips it back to its barebones to make something a bit bolder, a bit more modern, and eye-catching.

Paulin is a Glasgow-based microbrand that’s known for colorful, unique, and whimsical watches, often playing with color to make their designs all the more interesting.

Needless to say, if money isn’t an issue, the sky’s the limit. Not all watches are created equal and buying just for the name recognition alone doesn’t always make for the best watch for the money. Instead, look for a price point that’s commensurate with the movement, design, and materials used, and you’ll never regret the decision to spend the big bucks.

Taken from a 1924 archival design, the Brunswick’s cushion case, clean lines, and mother-of-pearl dial may all feel a bit “precious”, shall we say; but this is a workhorse watch that’s destined to be a daily driver for anyone who is looking for a little eye-candy on the wrist.

The IWC Ingenieur is a masterfully made ceramic watch (and matching ceramic bracelet) that will no doubt get you a few compliments every time you wear it to the office.

Not many watches can be mistaken for a Bell & Ross watch with its larger proportions and signature square case shape. The BR-05, the brand’s most popular model, is a sportier, slightly bolder answer to Cartier’s Santos.

It’s hard to narrow down one Grand Seiko to choose, but the White Birch is subtle and versatile enough to dress up or use as your daily driver. Most impressive about this particular model is the Caliber 9SA5 movement, which promises a whopping 80-hours of power when fully wound – nearly twice that of many others on the market.

Mechanical watch movements fall into two basic categories: hand-wound and automatic. Before the invention of automatic wristwatches in the 1920s (and long before the invention of battery-powered quartz movements in the 1960s) all watches were hand-wound. That meant you needed to manually wind up your watch every day to keep it running. An automatic watch (also called a self-winding watch) doesn’t require this because it winds automatically with the movement of your body as you wear it.

Most automatic watches wind themselves via a weighted rotor, which you’ll be able to see if your watch has a see-through case back (it’s the half moon-shaped piece right on top). When you wear the watch and move around, the rotor winds a spring, which turns the hands, date wheel and everything else that moves on a watch.

To keep your watch from stopping when you’re sitting still (or when you take it off at night) automatic movements also store excess energy and slowly release it over hours or days while the rotor is stationary. This is called a “power reserve,” and it typically ranges from 40 to 80 hours, with some going as high as a week or more. They’re useful if you don’t wear a watch every day, and don’t want to have to reset the date and time every time you put it on—particularly with annual or perpetual calendar models that can be complicated to set.

It’s not a downside, per se, but they do require some upkeep. Unlike a quartz watch that requires nothing more than a new battery every few years, a mechanical watch is a finely-tuned machine consisting of many tiny, precisely calibrated parts. Watchmakers have made huge advances in shock-and waterproofing over the years, but there’s still only so much abuse a mechanical movement can take. And that’s the thing: repairs to automatic watches can be expensive.

If you stick to the manufacturer’s recommended service schedule (it’ll need an overhaul and lube every 4 to 10 years, depending on the brand and model) and don’t make a habit of wearing it in the sauna, you’ll probably be fine. Whether it’s a $200 Amazon special or a $200,000 grand complication, automatic watches are designed to be worn, so the best way to take care of your watch is to wear it as much as possible.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)

#6596. An Expert Guide to Nail Health, According to Professionals

Here’s exactly how to keep them strong and healthy

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As manicure trends evolve with new lacquers, changing shapes, and viral techniques, beauty lovers often compromise nail health for aesthetics. While many nail formulas have improved over the years to prioritize strength, uniformity, and finish, experts say incremental habits—from lifestyle shifts to resisting the urge to peel off chipped polish—can ultimately play the biggest role in keeping your nails in excellent condition.

“The future of manicures isn’t just about the latest color trends or how long your gel polish lasts, but how well we care for the nails underneath,” Krisztina van der Boom, founder of DryBy, previously told Harper’s Bazaar. Hand, foot, and nail expert Margaret Dabbs agrees, urging nail enthusiasts to “Adopt a dedicated nail care routine and stick to it.” But what does an effective routine actually look like? Along with keeping your nails clean and hydrated with an emollient moisturizer, like Caudalíe’s Vinotherapist Hand and Nail Cream, professionals suggest keeping a cuticle oil, such as Dior’s Huile Abricot, in your bag for quick touch-ups.

“Cuticle oil is the one product I would never skip,” says Peach Bling founder Sandra Wei. “Look for a formula with jojoba oil, which has small enough molecules to absorb into the nail and surrounding skin.” Wei encourages her clients to apply it daily, ideally before bed, and to treat it as a small ritual. “A rich hand cream and gloves for dishwashing and cleaning also make a real difference,” continues Wei, “since water and harsh detergents are among the most common causes of dry, weak nails.” Maintaining nail and cuticle hydration is also key for mature nails, as they tend to grow more slowly and hold less moisture as we age, which can contribute to brittleness and vertical ridges.

Professionals have debated whether it’s safe to trim your cuticles, since cuticles guard nail beds against bacteria and infection; that's why they often advise pushing them back instead of removing them entirely. “As long as you're not cutting any live skin and damaging your cuticles, gently pushing them back and buffing away excess skin will actually help improve the health and the appearance of your cuticles and will help make your manicure last as long as possible while looking salon-quality,” says Olivia Van Iderstine, vice president of education and manicure trend expert at Olive & June. Prep your fingertips with cuticle oil or a soak in warm, soapy water before a manicure, then use a disposable orangewood stick or stainless steel pusher, such as JinSoon’s HyperCare Cuticle Pusher + Reducer, to improve polish cohesion and create an elongated nail look.

Consistent, gentle filing can also keep nails strong, regardless of your shape preference, because a smooth surface guards against unwanted splits, rips, and tears that can cause long-term damage. While there’s nothing wrong with a budget-friendly emery board, professionals say investing in the right tools provides the right resistance without compromising your nails' integrity. Dabbs is a fan of crystal nail files because they can help prevent micro-splits that lead to peeling and breakage, though the best results depend on not overfiling.

And if you usually opt for gel polish manicures, they don’t have to be a deal breaker for nail health. Several options, from builder gel to Japanese gel and more, provide longevity, durability, and style without weakening natural nails.

“Japanese gel systems are known for their precision and for their focus on preserving the natural nail,” continues Wei. “The prep is minimal and gentle, so we avoid heavy buffing of the nail plate. The gel itself is applied in fine, structured layers that are flexible and sit close to the natural nail.” Peach Bling prioritizes formulas that are free of HEMA and TPO, two ingredients commonly linked to gel allergies and sensitivities. “The result is a manicure that looks refined, wears beautifully, and treats the nail underneath with care,” explains the founder. Bazaar editors love builder gel, as it works like Japanese gel, supporting natural nails with a rounded apex to reinforce their sturdiness.

Experts advise keeping a gel set on for no longer than four weeks, as regrowth changes the nail's balance, ultimately causing unwanted stress and making breaks more likely. Once gel polish begins to lift or crack, it’s often a sign that removal is necessary—and that process can often be more damaging than simply wearing a gel formula in the first place. “What protects nail integrity is how the gel is applied and, even more importantly, how it is removed,” adds Wei. “With careful, gentle removal, there is no need to take a ‘break’ between appointments.”

For more than 150 years, Harper’s Bazaar has been the preeminent fashion and beauty resource for women at every age. We cover what’s new and what’s next in beauty by working with the world’s leading authorities in dermatology, plastic surgery, cosmetics, skincare, hair care, and fragrance. Every story we publish has been thoroughly researched and vetted by our team of beauty editors and industry experts.

#6594. Hublot Just Relaunched Its First Watch. Founder Carlo Crocco Explains How It Changed Everything.

In Milan, celebrating the release of the Classic Fusion Tributo, I got to catch up with the guy who altered the aesthetics of modern watchmaking.

If you spend a lot of time following the world of watches, Hublot’s origin story might feel as familiar as one of the fairytales you heard at bedtime as a child. If you don’t, though, it might feel just as fantastical as some yarn about a princess who feels a pea under a stack of mattresses or a prince who slays a fire-breathing dragon.

You start with an unlikely hero. This time around, it’s an Italian entrepreneur with a big, bold, brazen idea. He wants to make a watch, something unlike anyone has ever seen. It’ll be gold, with an angular case shaped like the porthole on a ship. Not so crazy yet. But wait. Because, breaking entirely with the conventions of the day, the strap won’t be made of exotic leather or precious metal but rubber. Humble, industrial rubber.

This is exactly what Hublot founder Carlo Crocco envisioned nearly half a century ago, and what he brought to the Basel Watch Fair—pretty much the place to be if you wanted to be a part of the business of timekeeping—in 1980. He spent three years developing the idea, all without doing a bit of market research. He just believed in his vision. And then the fair began.

“I was very anxious, because for the first two days, no one came into our booth,” he says. But the anxiety didn’t have long to fester. Word was spreading. And then? People galore. “On the third day, we realized that everyone was speaking about us because it was really something different from all the other watches.”

That first watch—36mm in diameter, with a quartz movement and a black rubber strap—quickly became a hit. Industry buzz gave way to the kind of clientele most brands can only imagine. The King of Greece bought one in Gstaad for himself and another for his brother-in-law, the King of Spain. More royals followed. (“By the way, they’re all related,” Crocco quips.)

But the biggest thing wasn’t getting insiders to a booth at a trade fair or convincing kings to strap Hublot watches—named, if you’re curious, for the French word for porthole—on their wrists. It was changing how the industry thought about the way watches are made.

“At that time, the mentality of watchmaking was very classical,” Crocco says. “They couldn’t think, normal people, of putting together rubber and gold. I personally thought it was a really good idea because it was against the current. It was very risky, but if you don’t risk, you have no result.”

The result was a watch industry that completely recalibrated itself. Crocco says that within four or five years of the original Hublot’s release, the brand was already dealing with imitators. Now, the idea of combining disparate materials is firmly embedded in the minds of both consumers and designers. You can see it on watches from brands that sell at the local department store and brands that only sell to folks who haven’t seen the inside of one of those since the first time their private jet took off. Crocco’s big idea changed the whole game.

Which means it’s a pretty good time to go back to the beginning of this thing. Last month in Milan, Hublot debuted a new take on that first, groundbreaking watch. Dubbed the Hublot Classic Fusion Tributo, it’s an homage but not a nuts-and-bolts recreation. Instead of the original 36mm case, it’s been upsized to a more 2026-appropriate 38mm. And instead of a quartz movement—Crocco thought it was “more practical” at the time but has come around to the enduring appeal of mechanical construction—a self-winding HUB1711 movement with a 50-hour power reserve keeps things ticking along. Oh, and now you can get it in white gold, too.

Then there’s that rubber strap. When Crocco designed the original Hublot, he was insistent that the strap was entirely straight; he felt it was crucial to the aesthetic proposition. On the Tributo, it’s ever-so-slightly, almost imperceptibly tapered, a necessary concession to keep the proportions intact after increasing the case size (which, handily, lets that automatic movement sit comfortably inside).

But, as with the first Hublot, it’s not all about tech specs and watch-nerd particulars. It’s about the spirit of the thing. And the spirit of this watch stays true to that pioneering design. There are twelve screws around the bezel, so you can keep an eye on the hour despite the dial’s lack of indices—something Crocco says was a non-negotiable when he started consulting on the new release. The yellow gold version keeps the abyss-like black lacquer dial and the white gold throws us a tiny (and welcome) curveball by using onyx. The combo of precious metal and rubber still hits, 46 years later.

If you prod him how he feels about the way that he shifted the watch industry, Crocco won’t brag. He left Hublot nearly 20 years ago, when it sold to LVMH. He knows his idea changed things; he’s happy about it. But this isn’t his day job anymore. Still, when he’s asked if he could be considered the godfather of fusing high and low in watchmaking—and you give him a minute to ponder it—he’ll tell you the truth.

#6593. Simu Liu’s First Favorite Brand Was Abercrombie & Fitch

Simu Liu is no stranger to fashion’s biggest night, so, when I met him at his hotel in SoHo Monday night, he figured the Council of Fashion Designers of America Awards show should be a cinch. “I think everything’s a little bit more chill than the Met Gala,” Liu says. “So I’m looking forward to having a little bit of a more relaxed environment to talk to fashion houses and meet people from the different brands and also just to see friends.”

We speak just a few hours before the 37-year-old actor is about to make his first appearance at the Oscars of fashion and it’s hard to tell that he just hopped off a plane from Los Angeles this morning. The Shang-Chi and the Legend of the Ten Rings actor has had a packed year, from making his Broadway debut in Oh, Mary! between February and April to getting married back in October. He’s preparing to start promotion for the highly anticipated December release of Avengers: Doomsday, in which he’s set to reprise his role as the superhero Shang-Chi. Marvel fans haven’t seen him on the screen since his record-breaking film debut in 2021. “It’s been a long, long time,” he jokes.

For now, he’s clad in a robe sitting barefoot on the sofa, swiping a metallic device across his unblemished, glowing face. “I think it’s like a gua sha, but it’s cold,” he speculates. (His makeup artist, Alex Keating, confirms that it’s Shark Beauty’s Depuffi, a hot-and-cold contrast therapy gua sha that basically “gives you a full facial.”) A room service trolley is sitting inches away from him, allowing him quick access to a plate of chicken tenders and fries. And a Diet Coke. Don’t forget the Diet Coke.

Keating makes good use of Liu’s wavy locks, hairspraying a Superman-like curl into permanent place on his forehead as he sips his fridge cigarette. The actor’s dad cut his hair until he was 18 and he says it took him a while to learn what worked for his own mane. He envied the typical stick-straight Asian hair others had, but through his work with groomers learned the power of salt sprays to lean into his hair’s natural texture. It admittedly took a while for Liu to develop his own fashion sense as an adult, too.

“When you grow up working class and you grow up with parents whose purchasing habits were just the furthest thing from luxurious, I think it just takes time for you to learn about fashion,” he says. “The first brand that I remember really caring about was Abercrombie & Fitch.”

Tonight, though, Liu has moved far beyond the mall. He’s attending the CFDA Awards on behalf of White Rabbit Trust, an organization that supports AAPI creatives and entrepreneurs. It's hosting the award’s Emerging Designers Table, which allows industry leaders—this year, Jason Wu—to mentor AAPI designers who create custom looks for the muse of their choosing. In Liu’s case, he’s been dressed by Asian-Canadian designer Haoran Li of the brand Private Policy.

The look for the evening was a matching set made from black silk jacquard woven with bamboo paired with a shiny new silver wedding ring and a Rolex Batman GMT-Master II. His cropped trucker-style jacket, accented by hand-knotted Chinese buttons, sat over a crisp frayed-hem white button-down with wide-legged pants that draped over low-profile black shoes. “In Chinese culture, bamboo is one of the Four Gentlemen, a symbol of integrity and resilience,” Li says. “It bends in the storm but never breaks. That felt like Simu, who has spent his career pushing through doors that weren’t built for us.”

I ask if he’s ever bothered by the responsibility of representing the Asian diaspora. “Right around the time Crazy Rich Asians came out, it was the first studio Asian movie in 25 years,” he recalls. “Then Shang-Chi was the first Asian Marvel superhero movie. For a while it was just like first this, first this, first this. So I felt like innately the stakes were high and I put pressure on myself. I don’t regret doing that. I just remember looking around and not really seeing anyone doing that. So I thought, well it might as well be me.”

To that point, this isn’t the first time Liu has used his name to promote an indie AAPI brand. At the the premiere of Shang-Chi in 2021, he spotlighted Singaporean-British designer Chelsea Scott-Blackhall’s label Dzojchen, wearing the brand’s silk kimono tuxedo.

Prominence in Hollywood has meant more to him than giving him the opportunity to wear really nice clothes. He’s decided to take more agency in his career, he says. Last year, he starred and executive produced the spy thriller series The Copenhagen Test, which despite favorable reviews was cancelled after one season. That hasn’t slowed his aspirations for new creative outlets: "I think actually right now I’m really excited by the idea of becoming a director."

But before we get to see “Directed by Simu Liu” on the big screen, the star has an awards show to attend—and a plate of tendies to devour. Just as quickly as he’s touched down here in New York, he’s off again to Hong Kong tomorrow.

#6592. The Remarkable Election Night Story We Should All Be Talking About

But his campaign for governor was something of a rollercoaster.

#6590. Kim Kardashian’s Latest ‘Raspberry Cream’ Nails Are So Unexpected

The manicure is a sharp departure from her typical style.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Like the rest of her beauty looks, Kim Kardashian’s nails are often bold. She’s been known to sport long, almond- and coffin-shaped styles that grab your attention (she even added a bedazzled “P” to a Barbie pink manicure once in honor of her then-boyfriend Pete Davidson). But at the season 2 premiere of All’s Fair this week, Kardashian stepped out with short, square-shaped nails in an opaque light pink color. We’re dubbing them “raspberry cream” nails.

“It’s a beautiful interpretation of futuristic minimalism, featuring a soft, milky, almost aquatic pink with a delicate translucency,” says celebrity nail technician and creator of eNailCouture Max Estrada. “I believe the decision to keep the nails short was very intentional. The manicure wasn’t necessarily meant to be the focal point. Instead, it allowed the extraordinary jewelry and the other elements of the look to take center stage.”

And while square nails have taken a backseat to more rounded shapes like almond in recent years, Estrada says that when paired with feminine colors like raspberry cream, they can work beautifully. “Square nails have a certain architectural quality, but when the edges are gently softened, as they are here, they can communicate incredible delicacy and softness,” he says. “In this particular manicure, the square silhouette feels refined rather than severe.” Orly manicurist Riyah Martin agrees, calling short and square nails “cute and chic.”

To re-create the look, start with a short, square nail, and buff out the corners. “The idea is to retain that beautiful square architecture while removing any harshness from the edges,” says Estrada. “For the color, I would apply a thin layer of semi-sheer pink, followed by a delicate milky pink to create that ethereal, translucent neo-milky effect.” Add a glossy top coat, and to emulate Kardashian, be sure to finish with sparkly jewelry.

#6589. After I Kicked Drugs and Alcohol, I Found a New Addiction: Cheating on My Fiancée

Thirteen years of sobriety taught me that the most dangerous thing for an addict isn’t getting caught—it’s getting away with it. And I keep getting away with it.

Ethical nonmonogamy relies on a foundation of total transparency. Partners openly agree to terms and continually negotiate them together. Betrayals such as infidelity can take many shapes and break the agreement a couple has made, monogamous or not. For people in recovery, however, the adrenaline of secret keeping can feel familiar. Clinicians call this behavioral, or process, addiction—in which compulsion lies in the thrill of the chase rather than the payoff. It doesn’t excuse betrayal, but it can help explain how a person committed to honesty ends up living a lie.

For this installment of the Secret Lives of Men, we spoke to Owen,* a 36-year-old brand strategist with 13 years of sobriety who spent most of the first two years of his relationship cheating on the partner he plans to marry. His story begs the question: What does it mean to carry out “living amends,” an Alcoholics Anonymous term for permanently changing your behavior for the better, when you aren’t ready to confess?

*Names and identifying details have been changed to protect anonymity.

Once a week, my fiancée has a therapy session over a video call that she takes from our bedroom. We both work from home, so while that happens, I’m usually barricaded in my office with noise-canceling headphones on, keeping up with work. Recently, I came out to the kitchen to pour a coffee after her hour was up, and she wasn’t home.

She takes walks, so there was no reason to panic. But my mind went where it has gone a lot lately: She found out. By whatever cruel trick of fate, she knew, and she was out there walking it off, letting the anger curdle. When she came back, she would bring hell with her.

I called once. She didn’t pick up. I didn’t try again. For half an hour, I paced the apartment unable to get a single thing done. I checked my phone every few seconds. I rehearsed what I’d say when she walked through the door: which parts I’d confess, which parts I’d still try to save. I worried myself sick. When she finally walked back in, it turned out she’d just been on a long phone call with her sister. She took off her headset, kissed me hello, and asked what I had in mind for dinner. My heart kept pounding long after she’d walked into the kitchen.

Until two years ago, I could say with real pride that I had never cheated on anyone. Not once. Not even when I was drinking and doing all those drugs, back when I lied about nearly everything else. I’d had opportunities, but infidelity was the one line I never crossed.

Advanced 12-step work taught me a strange paradox: Sometimes you have to sit with the agonizing truth that you are doing something wrong. That you aren’t ready or able to stop. I’ve built 13 years of sobriety on the idea that we are only as sick as our secrets. But I should admit something up front: I’m good at telling stories, especially about myself. Even now, I catch myself deciding which parts to share. So here it is: For the better part of two years, I cheated on the woman I’m going to marry. She still doesn’t know.

In recovery, you learn that the most dangerous thing that can happen to a person with an addiction isn’t getting caught—it’s getting away with it. And every time you skate by, the lie gets a little easier to tell. The disease gets a little more room to grow. I first learned that at 23. I had to learn it again in my 30s.

I come from a long line of talented, tortured people. My father sold commercial real estate and spent years in and out of a quiet, high-functioning addiction to painkillers. He went away for treatment twice. My mother was raised by an alcoholic and carried a lot of anger. My parents divorced when I was young, both remarried badly, and neither of their houses was a safe place emotionally.

At 19, while in college, I fell into a relationship I can only describe as wickedly codependent. We went through two miscarriages together and became trauma bonded. I didn’t know how to grieve, so I was drinking heavily and taking almost anything I could find: stimulants, psychedelics, dissociatives. Just before I turned 21, I caught my girlfriend in a lie so profound it ripped the wool off my eyes: She had been sleeping with my best friend for nearly the entire time we’d been together. One of the miscarried babies was actually his. I ended things the day I found out and took the situation as the perfect excuse to keep killing myself slowly.

I didn’t make it a year out of college before the consequences caught up with me. The fall after graduation, I was driving home from a concert when I got pulled over for a dead taillight. I was relatively sober and polite and was sure I’d talked my way out of it—right up until the officer asked me to empty my pockets. Inside were a bag of powder that I’d been promised was Molly and a few pills I didn’t have a prescription for. They booked me and sent everything off to the state lab. For three months, nothing happened. Meanwhile, I painted myself further into the corner before the floor ultimately fell out. When the charge finally came, I began a year and a half of court dates and probation.

As a first offender, I pled into a court-mandated, recovery-focused diversion program with regular drug tests. I followed the program to a T, at least for a while.

One weekend, when house-sitting alone for a neighbor, I took a drink out of boredom and pure spite. No test caught it. Then I lied about it to my counselors, the court, and my family. A month later, guilt finally won out, and I admitted it to my sponsor. A few days later, he brought it back up and told me I had to come clean to everyone.

“The fact that you were willing to conceal that from everyone for this long,” he said, “is the only evidence you need of how sick you are.”

The problem wasn’t the drink. It was how easily I hid it. I surrendered to the program and handed my life over to a higher power. I haven’t had a drink since.

I went celibate by choice for three years. I did a lot of therapy and learned that I had swung from codependent to avoidant. I had finally regained control, and I didn’t want to hand myself to anyone. Then, in my late 20s, I decided to give dating another shot.

Before long, I leaned into ethical nonmonogamy and dated as “solo poly,” which means I was seeing multiple people without a primary partner. Casual dating became my primary hobby. Some connections were purely physical; some were friends with benefits; some went emotionally deep. At my peak, I was sleeping with six people, two or three times a week. I felt I was winning at something. I was always honest with every partner about who I was, sobriety included. But I wasn’t honest with myself about why I needed so much of it.

Then, later in therapy, I came to understand that the dopamine that keeps people in an addiction loop is driven less by the thing itself than by the anticipation of getting it. Dating apps are built on that anticipation. For me, there were three hits: the match, the first nudes, the first hookup. In the program, we say the disease of addiction wears as many faces as it needs to. The chase was one of those faces.

I matched with Maya as she was getting ready to leave on a six-week work trip to Thailand. We messaged far longer than usual, trading long voice notes across the 11-hour time difference—her from hotel lobbies at night, me on morning walks. By the time we finally met, I already knew how she laughed. Our first date was electric, the best of my life. We sat at the bar until they started stacking chairs, talking about family, grief, books that had saved us. She was self-sufficient and emotionally deep. She was great at conversation. She was beautiful.

A few months into our relationship, we curled up on her couch on a Sunday afternoon and talked about ethical nonmonogamy. We’d both dabbled in it, but neither of us had done it inside a primary relationship. I told her I wanted it. She was open to it. We agreed to start monogamous and build the foundation first, but we never said for how long.

The truth is, our sex wasn’t great. Early on, I suggested something new in bed, and she went quiet and turned toward the wall. Neither of us brought it up the next morning. I internalized the idea that I couldn’t ask for what I wanted, that this was just how it would be until we opened things up. Instead of communicating, I let the resentment build.

Then, one afternoon at a coffee shop across town, I ran into Bree, a woman I used to hook up with. She had been the perfect friend with benefits: incredible chemistry but no desire for a relationship. We stayed and talked for an hour. When she mentioned she lived ten minutes away, neither of us pretended not to know what that meant. By that evening, I was at her place. It was the first time in my life I had been unfaithful in any way, and I felt awful. When I went home, I waited for the consequences. They never came.

A big part of addiction is justification. We were planning to be nonmonogamous, I told myself, so the way I’d just conducted myself was basically equivalent. It wasn’t that bad. I can see now how thin that logic was. Nonmonogamy runs on transparency and consent. I was keeping a secret.

Over the next year and a half, my infidelities came and went. A few more times with Bree. A handful of times with anonymous women in other cities. Trans women, sex workers. I traveled for work, and I took advantage of the distance. I kept my phone face down, hid dating apps, used end-to-end-encrypted texting. For each time I followed through, there were five times I stopped myself. Each close call made me feel like I had it under control.

Strangely, the woman I cheated with most also kept me from making worse mistakes. Bree became my confidante, the only person I was fully honest with about any of it. One night, I arranged to meet a sex worker. I was sitting in my car outside the hotel when clarity hit. The only thing I could think to do was call Bree. She talked me down. She and I ended up having sex that night. The program has a phrase for that kind of thinking: I want what I want when I want it.

Meanwhile, the relationship I was betraying kept getting better. We slowly began to practice nonmonogamy, and it took us far longer than either of us expected. We listened to the audiobook version of Polysecure by Jessica Fern together. We’d pause so often to talk that a half-hour chapter took two hours. It gave me a way to ask for what I wanted without it feeling like an attack on her. One night, Maya paused the book and asked me, plainly, how she could do better. For the first time, I believed she meant it, and I told her. The sex got better. Then, much better.

The final close call came in June. Bored at my desk, I connected with someone on an anonymous hookup site, and we moved to texting. A few messages in, after I’d sent a couple of nudes, my phone lit up with a long blackmail threat. Attached was a screenshot of Maya’s Instagram. This kind of thing had happened four or five times before, back when I was single. Go ahead, I would say. I never considered paying, and no one ever followed through. But this time, I closed my laptop, walked to the bathroom, sat on the edge of the tub until my hands stopped shaking, and blocked the number. This time, I had something to lose. I was forced to face how stupid I was being.

I shared the story in therapy. My therapist, who usually lets me do most of the talking, leaned back, looked at his clipboard, and said, “What the fuck are you doing? This is like the 20th time something like this has happened.”

I didn’t have an answer. Nothing had ever happened to me. That was the whole point.

This past August, on a weekend trip to the Catskills with friends, I proposed to Maya. She said yes before I could finish the question. I meant every word I said that weekend.

Maya and I have now officially opened our relationship. We’ve mostly played as a couple. When she sees someone on her own, she tells me everything the next morning. Last month, I sexted with a woman for days and chose not to tell her. Though I have permission, I can’t seem to give up the secrecy.

Growing up in the houses I did, I hid the truth to keep the peace. Recovery taught me to say it anyway, but I’ve only ever practiced with people who can’t leave, like a sponsor or a therapist. Telling Maya is different. Sometimes, I reverse the question: What if she were the one carrying a secret like this? I like to think that I’d find a way to accept it, because through everything I’ve done, I have never loved her any less.

In the program, when you can’t make amends without causing further harm, you make what’s called living amends. It starts with stopping the behavior. Then you try to love better, act cleaner, and stay present day after day, without asking for absolution. That’s where I am now. I pray regularly that this pain is my consequence. If that’s my penance, I’ll carry it.

#6587. 25 Great Gifts for New Dads, According to GQ

Becoming a dad changes a lot of things. Your sleep schedule, for one. Your relationship with a clean shirt, for another. But just because a guy has entered his daddy era doesn't mean he's stopped caring about the things he cared about before.

With that in mind, we've rounded up the best gifts for new dads, with a little something for him and the little one. Some are practical things that'll make those early days of fatherhood a little easier. Others are simply things that'll make him feel good, look good, and a little more like himself.

Nothing doing? Check out the rest of our gift guides and sign up for the GQ Recommends newsletter for more gift ideas worth your money.

At some point, he'll emerge back into the world, and there's nothing like a great shirt to ease the reentry. This swaggy checked shirt from Beams has all the layering built right in, so he can look like he hasn't missed a step.

There's a lot to be said for clothes that don't require much thought, especially when you've got a new bundle. Take this double-faced merino wool bomber from Quince: warm, unfussy, and easy to throw on with just about anything.

Some brands really are thinking of the children. Everybody.World makes its Trash Zip Hoodie from 100% recycled cotton waste, giving discarded material a second life as a very good sweatshirt.

A good pair of sweatpants is nonnegotiable. From the true masters of the category, these midweight terry sweatpants from Reigning Champ come with a relaxed silhouette, an open hem, and a nicely lived-in patina.

The undisputed king of dad shoes has entered its seventh generation, with its first major redesign since 2022 bringing a sleeker suede-and-mesh upper, softer cushioning, and all the Made in USA pedigree you'd expect. For a guy who's just become a dad, the timing couldn't be better.

He's a dad now. A man of refinement, responsibility, and a pressing need for a do-it-all boot. Enter Blundstone's new Heritage boot, a top-shelf take on its legendary shit-stomper, upgraded with premium leather and Goodyear-welted construction.

The baby's developing a sense of sight, and there's no reason for dad to sacrifice his. Lalo's Play Gym II is a far better-looking alternative to what's out there, with plenty to keep little hands and eyes occupied, including sensory toys, teethers, and a bell. The brand's set of high-contrast art cards wouldn't hurt, either.

If they've already got a Tripp Trapp, this one's a no-brainer. The iconic high chair is part of MoMA's permanent collection, and now the museum's Design Store has a set of colorful cushions to go with it.

One thing new parents can never have enough of is a good place to put the baby down. Part quilt, part playmat, these organic cotton numbers from House of Noa are among the most handsome we've seen, with an heirloom quality that makes 'em worth holding on to long after the baby has outgrown tummy time.

The problem with BabyBjörn? Too many good things to choose from. But for new parents, the gift of a few hands-free minutes is hard to beat. The new Bouncer Begin is designed specifically for newborns, with a shallower seat than the brand's wildly popular Bouncer Bliss.

Bottle-washing is a dad duty. We don't make the rules. Baby Brezza's Bottle Washer Pro 360 makes the job considerably less miserable, washing, sterilizing, and drying bottles in one go.

Two things you can never have enough of with a newborn: muslin and blankets. Coyuchi's wonderfully soft muslin quilt takes care of both.

New dads live by the clock. Three hours between feeds, 20 minutes until the next nap, two minutes to themselves, if they're lucky. Braun's Time Teacher, with its clean, easy-to-read dial, colorful hands, and markings for phrases like "quarter past" and "half past," makes keeping track a little easier. And when the little one is old enough to tell time, it'll come in handy all over again.

Apple's finally getting into the smart display game. The new HomeView is the company's first dedicated smart home hub, bringing home controls, FaceTime, and the usual Apple ecosystem conveniences to one ittle screen. Its 12-megapixel camera should come in especially handy for keeping the grandparents supplied with baby sightings.

A sleeping baby is a beautiful thing. Hatch's portable Go sound machine is a little godsend, with soothing sounds, a rechargeable battery, and a handy clip for attaching it to the stroller or car seat.

Bose's new sport-minded open earbuds let him enjoy his music or a podcast without tuning out what's happening around him—like, say, an actual human baby. And on the rare occasion he gets out for a run, they'll be just as useful for logging a few miles.

For the dad with his hands full (read: all dads), Ray-Ban's Meta Zena glasses pair a snazzy cat-eye silhouette with the ability to capture first steps, film stroller outings, or video-call the grandparents, all without reaching for his phone.

Tiny Vinyl's miniature record player and four-inch records are pure novelty, but there's something rather charming about a tiny turntable for a tiny human. Start building the little one's record collection now, and they'll have something to grow into.

Fujifilm's new Instax Pal 2 gives dad another way to document his little one, packing a digital camera into an adorable, palm-sized package. He can send shots straight to his phone, or pair it with an Instax printer for a few keepsakes the grandparents can stick on the fridge.

there's something to be said for having a few memories you can actually hold in your hands. This new 35mm point-and-shoot from Kodak lets him capture the little one on film, with actual prints to show for it.

Coterie diapers really do live up to the hype. And the brand's skincare lineup is pretty terrific, too. This set brings together full-size bottles of its hair and body wash, all-over moisturizer, and diaper and dry skin balm.

Dad's going to need all the help he can get in the sleep department. No surprise there. What might surprise him is how much magnesium can help, from supporting normal muscle and nerve function to regulating the body's stress response and potentially improving sleep quality.

The Fitbit Air might be the sleekest screen-free fitness tracker around, and at a price that makes it an easy sell. For the new dad who just wants to keep tabs on his sleep and daily steps without getting too deep into the fitness-data weeds, it's a straightforward proposition.

A little time for some om-ing is a fine idea for any new dad. Walden's meditation set gives him a dedicated place to find his center, and the accompanying mat doubles as a nice spot for the little one to stretch out.

#6583. Ralph Lauren’s 17 Best Outfits of All Time

On Monday night, the Council of Fashion Designers of America crowned Ralph Lauren as its menswear designer of the year—his 12th win since the show’s inaugural ceremony in 1981, minting him as the most decorated designer in CFDA history. The council even has a tradition of creating awards in Lauren’s honor, as they did with the Fashion Legend Award in 2007 and the CFDA Members Salute in 2018.

At age 87, Lauren is the consummate businessman of American fashion, and he’s still producing masterclass-caliber collections that build upon his label’s established codes. In addition to his business savvy and keen eye for tailoring, the designer remains as relevant as ever in 2026 because of something far more ineffable: a deep-rooted sense of personal style. For Lauren, clothes aren’t just a wardrobe; they are identity. His own relationship with getting dressed, from his deep and abiding love of Westernwear to his knack for blending Waspy blazers with rugged workwear and military pieces, has always been at the core of everything he does. Here are some of his best looks from the last 50 years.

Technical hiking gear was a relatively late addition to the RL oeuvre, but the man was wearing hikers in the city long before hipsters discovered Salomons.

Like a skilled party DJ, Ralph Lauren has an uncanny knack for combining unlikely elements—like a yachtsman’s sweater and a pair of worn-in blue jeans—with complete ease.

There’s black-tie, and then there’s RL black-tie. There’s no doubt that Lauren can wear a pair of tuxedo pants and patent leather shoes as well as anyone, but he is much more himself in jeans and cowboy boots.

A two-year stint at Baruch College in Manhattan was the closest Lauren came to the Ivy League, but that didn’t stop him from adopting repp ties, Oxford collar shirts, and other hallmarks of the East Coast elite. The juxtaposition of oversized aviator shades with this solidly trad look is quintessential Ralph.

Think a funky belt can’t be the defining element of an otherwise understated fit? Lauren would respectfully disagree.

Few people will ever look as good in a biker jacket as Ralph Lauren did at age 70. The difference is in the details—the flannel shirt unbuttoned and tucked, the crisp white henley—and the confidence.

Lauren is a master editor, but the magic of his greatest looks is in details like a vest tucked into trousers, a knotted belt, and a pair of precisely rolled sleeves.

Yacht club up top, honkey tonk downstairs. Another Lauren signature.

Ralph Lauren never learned how to sew, but he has an instinctive understanding of tailoring. By blending the stiff silhouettes of Savile Row with the breezy swagger of American ivy style, he created a look that was all his own.

Style is all about understanding the rules before you can break them. Lauren certainly does, and that’s why this ensemble—from the wide-spread collar and slim tie combination to the double-down on stripes—works as well as it does.

There’s perhaps no more challenging look to pull off than “office cowboy,” but Ralph Lauren makes it look easy.

Leather pants and a denim shirt under a tweed jacket is another decidedly advanced maneuver, but (if executed correctly) a potentially powerful one.

In recent decades, Lauren has increasingly swapped his signature cowboy boots for La Sportiva hikers, anointing gorpcore in the process.

Lauren has always revered the look of traditional British tailoring, from safari suits to Savile Row, but he’s just as likely to pair his flannel suits with cashmere turtlenecks and cowboy boots as neckties and Oxfords.

#6582. These Immersive Coffee Tours Follow the Beans From Tree to Cup

Travelers who can’t go a day without press-and-go espresso pods or a ­meditative pour-over ritual, rejoice! Coffee tourism is on the rise, and, thanks to expert tour operators with access to the best farms and baristas, your next visit to Latin America, East Africa, or Southeast Asia can be as caffeinated as you want. That could entail lingering with like-minded locals in cafés or stomping on the very soil in which these prized beans grow. If you’re eager to geek out on the whats and hows of it all, immersive education is always on the table—and in the cup.

In the Antioquia region, a custom itinerary with True Colombia Travel balances exclusive access to three of the country’s best producers with myriad adventures (like coffee in a village where locals still get around on horseback). Enthusiasts will marvel at coffee trees on volcanic slopes and learn about fermentation techniques. A visit to Café Cocondo Orgánico, where coffee is cross-­pollinated with mango, avocado, orange, and soursop trees, is especially thrilling.

Known for high-quality ­arabica and robusta beans, this East African nation is paradise for those who crave a brightly flavored brew. Arusha Coffee Lodge by Elewana is conveniently located between Mount Kilimanjaro and the Ngorongoro Conservation Area. It’s also surrounded by 100 acres of coffee trees. Hotel guests can tour the roasting facilities and sample as much of the final product as they’d like. Don’t miss the day’s rotating selection of java-flavored treats, which might include nutty cakes, tiramisu, or scones.

Every year, Augusto Carneiro—founder of Nossa Familia Coffee in Portland, Oregon—leads tours of Guatemala City’s third-wave scene. Travelers start by sharing breakfast with the owners of the trendy café Coffee District, then help with the harvest at a farm partway up Volcán de Agua. Later, they head to Finca San Jerónimo Miramar in the Atitlán region to learn about grafting arabica plants onto robusta roots. There are also many sorts of excursions, from a hike on Acatenango mountain to swimming in Lake Atitlán.

Guided experiences around the globe offer a chance to meet the makers behind your single-origin roast.

#6581. Everything We Know About The Batman: Part II Thanks to… Heidi Klum?

If you’d told me even a month ago we’d get significant spoilers for The Batman: Part II, the next Robert Pattinson-led Batman movie, in a photo Heidi Klum posted for her annual Halloween costume, I would find that both weirdly specific and entirely unbelievable.

And yet, that looks to be exactly what happened this past weekend when prosthetics supplier The Makeup Armoury posted BTS photos of the work prosthetics expert Mike Marino (who also worked to transform Colin Farrell into The Penguin for the first film and subsequent HBO Max series) was doing for Klum. If you’ve somehow been off-grid for the last few years, you may have missed that the famed supermodel takes Halloween incredibly seriously, frequently donning costumes with extensive makeup and designs that make her unrecognizable. This year was no exception, but it might have gone largely unnoticed had that photo not accidentally revealed clues about the upcoming superhero sequel, which is currently in production with an expected release date of February 18th, 2028.

In the photo, Marino, who provided a helping, ahem, hand in Klum’s costume together for the year, had a number of molds lingering in the background, which have seemingly confirmed that Brian Tyree Henry will play Two-Face, Sebastian Stan will play Victor Zsasz, and that Part II will feature the famed Court of Owls.

Henry’s mold appears to show the Atlanta actor with a disfigured face that mimics the iconic villain. Some, including me, in this very outlet, have, speculated that he might be playing either Dent or Detective Harvey Bullock, a hardened Gotham City cop who frequently works alongside Jim Gordon. But this seems to confirm it. Considering writer-director Matt Reeves has cited Jeph Loeb and Tim Sale’s The Long Halloween as an influence on his version of the Caped Crusader, it’s not ultimately a huge surprise, but perhaps a slight disappointment considering Reeves had stated his intention to move the sequel into uncharted territory by featuring a villain that had “never really been done in a film before.”

Which brings us to Victor Zsasz, who Stan seems to be playing. There was some speculation when he shaved his head upon joining the production he might be playing the character, but the cast looks to confirm the speculation. While the serial killer—who carves tally marks into his body for every person he’s killed—shows up in Batman Begins as a minor enforcer in Carmine Falcone’s crew, he’s got a much larger role in the comic books themselves. In Long Halloween, Zsasz is hired by Falcone to assassinate Dent before he's intercepted by Batman. Might Reeves look to flip the script here and disfigure Henry’s Dent in the process? This interpretation of Zsasz would certainly fit the extremely grounded approach Reeves took in the first installment. But there’s also some confusion here, as Stan was rumored to be playing Harvey when casting first broke and even went so far as to say he’d be playing the role in an interview over the summer. The French publication Le Journal du Dimanche did, however, later retract his statement.

The last accidental spoiler is a big scoop for comic book fans. Simply labeled “Owl 3,” this likely confirms that the Court of Owls will finally make their silver-screen debut. Created in 2012 by writer Scott Snyder and artist Greg Capullo, the Owls are one of the biggest new additions to the Batman canon in recent memory. The Court is a clandestine criminal society made up of Gotham’s wealthiest and most influential citizens, whose power and influence stretch back to the city’s earliest days. They have immeasurable resources and frequently deploy assassins, known as Talons, to do their bidding. This tracks with the story Reeves told in The Batman of a Gotham beset by a history of corruption. Owls are also bats' natural predators; so that, too, is bad news for Battinson.

While this potential leak is exciting and unexpected, it also raises doubts about the identity of Scarlett Johansson’s character, as she was rumored to be playing Harvey’s wife, Gilda Dent. Either way, never in a million lifetimes could we have guessed that Heidi Klum would be the one to spill the beans about one of the most anticipated comic book movies of the next few years.

#6577. Zillennials Don’t Feel the Need to Grow Up, Club Kid Knows Why

Jordan Firstman’s deferred coming-of-age tale speaks to the realities of living in an unwieldy time

I’m 29, an age that has become a catalyst for conversations about what it means to be an adult, about settling down (or not), and, perhaps above all, how to show up for my own well-being (do I need to party less?) It’s a volley of topics that are often the subject of yearly think pieces, even when we know the conclusions they’ll reach. Millennials and Zillenials feel pressured by older generations to live a conventional and ambitious life, even as they’ve squeezed young people’s hope for a future.

The New York Times detailed this dilemma just last month, speaking to thirtysomethings across New York City who felt discouraged by their situation and, to put it plainly, left behind. The article pointed out that the long-running Harvard Youth Poll found, for the first time ever, that most young Americans don’t believe they will surpass their parents financially. In the last 30 years, the cost of living has skyrocketed, and, today, mounting loans and debt among young people have made a house feel out of reach. Looking even younger, Gen Z is feeling the brunt of the economy, with Fortune finding that two-thirds of parents say their Gen Z adult kids rely on them (and Playboy going so far as to connect the housing crisis to the sex decline). Why stop partying if the rent’s too high, if there’s no reasonable expectation that you’ll find security, buy a house, or live easily past 2050?

This growing frustration is likely why audiences—including myself—are connecting so deeply with influencer-turned-director Jordan Firstman’s sensational feature debut, Club Kid. The film offers a tender look at arrested development and unexpected fatherhood, but above all, it delivers an incisive look at the struggle to let go of youth when the world feels increasingly grim. The idea of partying instead of worrying is alluring: to exist in a space free from responsibility. Peter (Firstman), a thirty-something party promoter deeply enmeshed in New York’s underground queer club scene, lives in the apartment his late mother left him, sells drugs on the side, and his only real daily concern is recovering for the next party. The moment he crosses the threshold of any club, he plunges into a fugue state fueled by drugs and sex. Peter clings to his youth (his name aptly echoing Peter Pan syndrome), shirks all responsibilities, and actively defers a much-needed inward look.

Peter’s wake-up call arrives as a 10-year-old boy named Arlo (Reggie Absolom)—a son he unknowingly fathered years after hooking up with a man who insisted they turn their dalliance into a threesome with a woman. A decade after that fateful night, the woman’s best friend (played with drug-fueled frenzy by Kirby Howell-Baptiste) shows up at his door, a young child in tow, with news that the mother has died by suicide and Peter needs to step up. It’s a parable tailor-made for a worn-down generation that sees no reason to stop the party. It takes a ketamine stork of sorts to change course.

Club Kid revels in the growing pains. Firstman avoids reducing the conflict to a simple clash over parenting Arlo or his personal struggle to break free from the party lifestyle; instead, Club Kid unfolds as a tender parallel coming-of-age, where Peter grows up as he learns to parent Arlo, a quiet, curious kid grappling with the loss of his mother. Firstman embraces the missteps, letting us see the beauty of his life as it intermingles with his newfound parenting. The whirlwind of responsibility—of unwitting fatherhood—plays like a fairy tale, a wondrous reality that feels increasingly distant from what a thirtysomething can reasonably expect today.

Watching Club Kid, I considered my own parents, who conceived me (by happy accident, I’m told) at 27 and 29. They worked at a pizza restaurant together before opening their own bar. My childhood felt wrapped up in Atlanta’s nightlife scene; somehow, completely inexperienced, they still learned to balance their lifestyle with their newfound roles as parents. I lived as the only kid (with my two-years-younger brother) in my parents’ world for several years, often sitting with the bar regulars for hours while my parents worked, or being actively watched over by the cooks. It felt like tapping into another world, but also brought us closer. They didn’t give up their life for me; they made me a part of it. These days, this story sounds like a romantic ideal, a whirlwind adventure only possible without looming loans and the high cost of living. I simply couldn’t imagine doing the same today.

What makes Peter’s story compelling is how he navigates the responsibilities of fatherhood without fully forgetting himself. Some critics have challenged the film, claiming that it’s stigmatizing the queer party scene, but in his telling, he doesn’t rebuke his former lifestyle, just the parts of it that were bad for him. His friend even welcomes the news of his fatherhood with excitement, spending time with Arlo without passing judgment. Even if he eventually needs to distance himself from these friends’ party habits, it doesn’t need a blow-out fight. Life happens, and people move on.

That’s why it’s important that Firstman returns to the real problem at the end of the movie. A vindictive call to social services by Peter’s ex-best friend (Cara Delevigne) leads to Arlo’s deportation and the new father-son duo’s separation. It brings to light the real-life anxieties looming over this heartwarming story. Sure, Peter did not file the proper adoption papers, and his lack of money becomes damningly apparent when he stares down health-care premiums and legal bills in the face of an unrelenting bureaucracy.

Firstman refuses to provide a pretty picture; he instead studies the anxieties of millennials, a generation perennially unsure of the world around them, and distills them into a heartwarming, deferred coming-of-age story. Firstman dreams of who we can become, if only given the chance to grow up in a world where steady ground is hard to find. Peter is growing up alongside his son, hiccups and all.

#6576. The Clarks Wallabee Goes Gorpcore

Clarks has been on one lately. Over the past few weeks, Moncler worked up the British footwear brand's signature Wallabee, while A.P.C. took on the Desert Boot and the Weaver. Now, Clarks has called in Italian shoemaker Diemme to drag three of its biggest silhouettes up a mountain.

(In case you don't spend all day thinking about Italian hiking boots, here's a primer: Diemme was founded in Montebelluna, a town in northern Italy with a very serious footwear-making history. The brand is best known for taking the kinda boots originally built for mountains and making them nice enough to wear with your very best trousers. Every pair is still made at its factory in Veneto, and the upcoming Clarks collection is, too.)

The main event is the Wallabee, of course. Diemme reworked the familiar moccasin-shaped shoe and covered it in pale “champagne” suede from Charles F. Stead, the British tannery responsible for some of the nicest fuzzy leather in footwear. So far, so classic. But then you get the thick rope laces and rubber mudguard wrapped around the bottom. Underneath, the OG crepe sole has been swapped out for the chunky, lugged unit found on Diemme's Roccia Vet and Cornaro boots. It makes the whole shoe sit higher and gives it enough tread to deal with more than a slightly sticky pub floor. Your ’Bees are now ready to hit the trails.

There are two more styles in the cross-branded collection. The Desert Boot comes in the classic “cola” brown suede, only now it's been fitted with a heavy-duty sole. Then there's the Desert Trek, which has been transformed into a mid-cut boot and made from black full-grain leather. It keeps the signature center seam and little Trek Man logo, but it's easily the biggest and baddest of the three, with metal D-rings running up the lacing system and a beefed up ankle for extra support.

The Diemme x Clarks collection is set to drop on November 5, purchasable through Diemme, Clarks, and select stockists globally.

#6575. Maxx Royal Bodrum Reimagines the Mediterranean Escape

The French and Italian Rivieras have long been popular summer destinations, but there’s another scenic stretch of the Mediterranean worth your immediate attention. Located on Turkey’s southwestern coast, Bodrum is growing in popularity as a vacation spot due to its beaches, waterfront restaurants, and yacht-filled marinas. It‘s also home to Maxx Royal Bodrum, a luxury resort that opened in May 2024, with private villas, restaurants from world-renowned chefs, and an expansive spa.

Set on a private peninsula overlooking the Aegean Sea, the property is surrounded by pine trees and has its own beach. It’s the third Turkish destination from Maxx Royal Resorts, following properties in Belek and Kemer, and features 294 rooms across a collection of suites and villas.

Designed with input from Turkish architect Mahmut Anlar, the resort incorporates stone, wood, and other natural materials that complement the coastal setting. Its Island Villas, for example, take inspiration from traditional stone houses found throughout Bodrum. Designed with local stone, wood, and large windows that bring in natural light, the villas each come equipped with private gardens, terraces, and infinity pools overlooking the sea.

Guests can also choose from larger villas better suited to families or groups or suites with direct pool access. The five-bedroom Presidential Hill Villa, in particular, boasts its own heated seawater pool, fitness room, sauna, and private butler’s room.

In terms of dining, Maxx Royal Bodrum is home to Spago by Wolfgang Puck, the chef's premier restaurant concept, as well as Caviar Kaspia, which specializes in seafood and, of course, caviar.

More recent additions include Leña, a steakhouse from Spanish chef Dani García, and Safraan, which serves up seafood and Mediterranean-inspired small plates.

There’s also Seavore, for updated takes on traditional Turkish dishes, and the on-site ice cream parlor, which stocks unique flavors like Turkish pistachio and Bodrum tangerine.

For those looking to spend some time away from the beach, the Maxx WellBeing Center spans approximately 61,900 square feet and includes 20 treatment rooms. Its offerings range from traditional Turkish baths and Biologique Recherche skin care treatments to reformer Pilates, yoga, and sound healing.

At Maxxi Land, children can participate in activities organized by age group, including arts and crafts, games, and film screenings. And beyond the resort, boat excursions or visits to nearby historical destinations, including the ancient city of Ephesus, are on offer.

Getting there is relatively straightforward, too. Maxx Royal Bodrum is about an hour from from Milas-Bodrum Airport, with boat and helicopter transfers also available for the jet-set crowd.

#6574. The Best Early Black Friday Clothing Deals 2026 Are Here

Before you start questioning your own reality, Black Friday is still a ways away. But it's not too early to start taking advantage of some early Black Friday clothing deals that already sprouting up. Retailers seem more eager than ever to get the sales started early this year, and at this rate, Black Friday will be here before you know it. And some of the hardest-hitting discounts land before the official day even kicks off.

And it’s not just like the stalwarts like Gap, Banana Republic, Abercrombie and J.Crew in the mix, although they are heavily in the mix. Killer indies like Lost & Found and Haven also have a host of discounted goods, and outdoor behemoth REI is feeling especially generous for early November. So, if you’re trying to shop away the daylight savings blues or just feel like getting a jump on holiday shopping, you’re in the right spot.

Abercrombie doesn’t have any specific promotions that are live at the moment, but the sale section was recently stuffed with some ideal fall building blocks—most notably some baggy cords—that will make a refresh as easy and affordable as you’d ever hoped it could be.

Banana Republic has been on a heater recently, and the perfectly-curated Archive Reissue collection is leading the charge this season. It should also be noted that anything on the site that is on sale also gets an additional 20% off.

Don’t tell anyone at Gap that Black Friday is a ways off, because there are a ridiculous number of in-season pieces up to 50%—sweaters, flannels, cords, jeans, jackets, and more—and it feels like raising too much awareness to that might cause some reconsideration and none of us want that.

Haven isn’t promoting a sale anywhere on the site and you might have to dig a little bit depending what size you wear, but if you’re looking for some of the sickest IYKYK brands on the planet at insane discounts, there aren’t many places I’d rather start.

If you’re not familiar with Huckberry, they preside over one of most thoughtfully curated assortments of menswear, footwear and gear anywhere, whether that’s IRL or URL. They haven’t kicked off anything Black Friday specific yet—again, it’s early—but if you haven’t gone through the sale tab in a while, you’re missing out on some preposterously good deals.

Nearly all of J.Crew’s spring-summer collection is on sale at the moment, over 300 pieces up to 40% off through May 26—including a whole bunch of heat-beating shirting, lightweight pants, archive classics and basically anything else you’d want to be on sale.

The first name in denim has a pretty nifty “buy two, get 30% off” promotion going on site-wide, so if you’re searching for some jeans, you might just want to buy a couple pairs. (Or maybe a trucker. Or sherpa-lined trucker. You’re in charge.)

Diving into Lost & Found’s sale section is one of the more enjoyable shopping experiences one can have online right now: the selection is great, the brand list is a top-five worldwide and there’s plenty of inventory, which puts it in stark contrast with some of the less rewarding sale sections.

One of the most quietly great, easy-to-wear brands in the game recently reloaded the sale section, and basically no category was left unturned. Of particular note, are the killer sweatshirts currently available with a healthy discount applied.

Mr Porter’s sale section is the kind you can lose an hour in without realizing it, even months after a seasonal sale has ended. Case in point: there’s roughly 500 pairs of shoes and 900 jackets on sale at this very moment, and while sizes might be hit and miss, the deals aren’t.

REI’s Deals section always lives up to the billing, but right now it’s about as good as it’s ever been. Hundreds of pieces from heavyweights like Arc’teryx, Patagonia, The North Face and more are discounted up to 50%, including plenty of winter-specific gear for anyone lacking in that department.

Todd Snyder presumably won’t be partaking in the Black Friday festivities this year—they announced a new sale structure a while back—but that doesn’t mean the already-on-sale fare doesn’t hold up to the promotions sure to fire up across the board.

This year in the US, Black Friday is on Friday, November 27, a day after Thanksgiving. While that’s the official date, many retailers roll out deals throughout November. You can expect the biggest discounts to happen between Thanksgiving and Cyber Monday, but be on the look out for retailers to launch sales early.

Cyber Monday lands on November 30 this year, the Monday after Thanksgiving. Most brands keep the deals rolling through the week—but that doesn't mean a deal will last all day, so act fast.

Are clothing deals better on Black Friday or Cyber Monday?

Black Friday and Cyber Monday both deliver a plethora of clothing deals, but they play a little differently. Big-name retailers and legacy brands usually kick things off on Black Friday, while smaller menswear boutiques tend to hold their fire until Cyber Monday. Expect solid discounts starting around 20% off—and keep your eyes peeled for the coveted sale-on-sale action that’s become the norm in recent years.
