#6571. What Goes Into Making Real-Deal Prosciutto di Parma PDO

There are precious few food products that have been enjoyed for as much of human history as Prosciutto di Parma. The signature silky texture and buttery, sweet-savory flavor of the “King of Hams” is recognized around the world as the result of carefully controlled production methods, dedication to quality, and traditions continued for centuries.

The history of Prosciutto di Parma reaches back more than 2,000 years, to Roman times, when the art of preserving pork through salting and natural drying was already well established in the Po Valley. As early as the 3rd century BC, Cato the Elder described methods for curing pork legs with salt and allowing them to dry and mature. Today, Prosciutto di Parma is produced exclusively in a strictly defined area of northern Italy, in the province of Parma in the Emilia-Romagna region, where local know-how combines with distinctive climatic conditions. Humid breezes from the Tyrrhenian Sea become drier as they flow over the Apennines and down onto the hills. Prosciutto producers discovered that this created an ideal environment for dry aging, and they built storerooms with tall windows that could be opened and closed as necessary, allowing the air to circulate through the racks of hams for gradual drying.

The specifics of aging, like every step in the process, are governed by a consortium—the Consorzio del Prosciutto di Parma—that was founded in 1963 to protect the authenticity of the product. In 1996, Prosciutto di Parma obtained the European Union’s Protected Designation of Origin (PDO). These have a red and yellow seal to show that a product is made in a certain geographical area using time-honored, traditional methods. Grana Padano, a nutty, aged hard cheese also from Northern Italy that’s often paired with Prosciutto di Parma on a charcuterie plate, was awarded a PDO the same year.

Grana Padano PDO and Prosciutto di Parma PDO also share the distinction of being fire-branded—a sign for consumers that you are buying the real thing. Only Parma hams that meet the PDO’s specifications can receive the five-pronged Ducal Crown that signifies a true Prosciutto di Parma.

Well before that brand is applied, production starts with the pigs. Which breeds, where they can be bred and raised (in 11 Italian regions), and what they are fed (a list that also includes whey from local cheesemakers) are all precisely detailed. Within the 28th day from birth, the piglets receive a breeder tattoo that will also appear on the final product along with the crown brand. The fresh pork (hind legs only) sent to prosciutto producers must weigh between 26 pounds and 40 pounds, with a set amount of fat coverage. After they arrive at the production facility, the legs are trimmed to form the characteristic rounded shape, chilled to a precise temperature, and salted by hand with sea salt, which joins the pork as the only two ingredients that go into the making of Prosciutto di Parma PDO.

Following a resting period of at least 90 days, the hams are washed then hung in windowed storerooms to dry slowly. The next step is called sugnatura: The parts of the ham not covered by rind are rubbed with sugna—a mixture of pork fat, salt, and pepper—to keep the meat from drying too quickly. For final aging, the hams hang in cool, dimly lit cellars. From start to finish, the process cannot last less than 14 months (many producers continue aging for 24, 30, or even 36 months and beyond). The last step before inspection may be the most fascinating: The hams are tested using a needle made from horse bone, selected because the bone is able to absorb the aroma and quickly shed it. By inserting the needle into different parts of the cured meat and smelling it, experts can determine if a whole ham leg has the qualifications to be named Prosciutto di Parma PDO.

The Consorzio, which started with 23 producers, now numbers 125, and in 2025 they turned out 6.75 million Prosciutto di Parma PDO hams. Beyond its economic significance to the region, the “King of Hams” is a cultural icon and a tourism magnet. The annual Parma Ham Festival is held in September, and there’s even an entire museum dedicated to the region’s signature product—with a tasting room, of course.

However you experience the exceptional flavor and texture of Prosciutto di Parma—on a visit to the museum or from your local specialty store—look for the crown and PDO seal, and take a moment to remember that you are tasting a distinctly delicious slice of history.

Head to DistinctlyDeliciouslyEuropean.eu to learn more about Prosciutto di Parma PDO and Grana Padano PDO.

#6570. Andrew Garfield as Sam Altman Is the Scariest Thing You’ll See All Year

In 2009, Andrew Garfield found a thread. He was preparing to play Facebook CEO Mark Zuckerberg in a little-hyped film called The Social Network, and he had located the thing in Zuckerberg’s story that he could pull on—the through line that turns personal experience into empathic performance and on into universal storytelling. Then he lost the part to Jesse Eisenberg. Director David Fincher had seen what, today, is obvious: Humanity flows fast and deep through Andrew Garfield. Fincher cast him instead as Eduardo Saverin, the betrayed cofounder and emotional counterweight to Zuckerberg’s opacity, who cries, sneers, throws a feint, and smashes a laptop (that’s one scene).

The movie made him a celebrity, and he brought an unusual depth of emotion to this job too. While serving as Spider-Man, he begged the public, via a handwritten sign held up for paparazzi, to care about a more important cause than what’s up with famous people. While promoting 2021’s Tick, Tick…Boom! on The Late Show With Stephen Colbert, he talked about the death of his mother; the clip still gets shared by grieving people.

Boxers by Hermès. Sneakers by Bottega Veneta. Socks by Jacques. Watch by Rolex.

Now, 15 years after The Social Network, Garfield has picked back up the tech-CEO thread to play Sam Altman in Artificial, Luca Guadagnino’s film that covers the founding of OpenAI up to the coup that led to Altman’s firing—and his re-entrenchment. (OpenAI has an agreement with Condé Nast, the owner of GQ, which allows OpenAI to display its content in search results for a limited term.) The movie seems destined to occupy the same orienting role as The Social Network, helping us understand the technology and the men who are shaping our futures, almost in real time. But whereas that film was a legal drama about status (Harvard guy vs. hotter, richer-born Harvard guys), Artificial plays as a horror movie where the monster, AI, is real. (And it just learned how to do theoretical physics.)

Garfield brings Altman to life as a mad guidance counselor: rubbing the shoulders of engineers, taking security meetings cross-legged, and sucking green juice in the faces of his adversaries. Here, finally, is an ascendant tech overlord that makes unsettling use of Garfield’s famous sensitivity. When Garfield-as-Altman cries, you’re not sure whether they’re the tears of a true superintelligence believer or a duplicitous power-seeker. Or which one would be scarier.

When we meet in early October, Garfield is transformed by a beard and shoulder-length hair. He grew it all out to play the magician Roy Horn, of Siegfried and Roy, in next year’s show Wild Things, though they ended up putting a wig on him anyway. He was filming that limited series in Berlin immediately after wrapping The Uprising, a Paul Greengrass movie about the Peasants’ Revolt of 1381, which shot before Artificial was even technically wrapped. “I did these three things back to back last year, and I did have a bit of burnout, to be honest. I was kind of sleepless in Berlin for a while, and not in a romantic way,” he says. “It was rough, dude. I should have just gone to Berghain and just rode it out.” Now, though, Garfield is visibly buzzing, both from watching Artificial with an audience at the New York Film Festival the night before—and from the chatter coming out of Silicon Valley.

This interview has been condensed and edited.

Andrew Garfield: I’ve listened to his podcast.

The walk was a key for me, and the clothes and the physicality. He’s a very slight, slim guy, Sam, and seems to be very image-conscious. Which is interesting to me, which is something that I felt compelled by. There’s a great video of Sam, in what looks like a diner, when he’s talking about Loopt [the company he cofounded before running Y Combinator and cofounding OpenAI], when he’s in his early 20s, and he was much freer with his physicality back then.

Sam did an interview with Vanity Fair [for the magazine’s New Establishment issue]. I think it came out yesterday, the day of our premiere, where he was very sweet about the walk as well. It’s a very generous thing for the boys to acknowledge that the walk was close, a good approximation of Sam and his vibe. I think that’s a very classy thing, to acknowledge the thing that seems to be working. But who knows what’s happening in their hearts and minds right now or within the family? I can project myself into that experience and imagine what it must feel like for an actor to be so presumptuous to play a character called Sam Altman in a major film. I can imagine that must be really strange, and I feel for…. I don’t know. In terms of taking it on, what outweighed that sense of responsibility for me was this sense of it is the defining inevitable invention of our times. It felt like a great responsibility to interrogate not just the machine, if you can even call it that, the kind of the consciousness or the technology itself, but to interrogate the people who are building it and who are putting their hearts and souls and life’s work into it, and where they may or may not be leading us.

That was deeply researched. This is what I gleaned. There’s a lot of people that know Sam. There’s a lot of people that are happy to talk about Sam. Some very, very protective; others, slightly more inside information. And I’m not going to name who these people are. But it was a kind of adding to the cauldron and through my lens of the interpretation of who this person is, combined with what was on the page and the specific cinematic story we’re telling and how we’re telling it, and Luca’s tastes and sensibilities. And, yeah, a lot of people said he would never sit conventionally. He was always sitting like a little boy.

Intimacy, but also ownership of a space—a kind of subtle, very soft dominance of a space. But also, as you say, a kind of, “Hey, we’re all just hanging out doing the coolest job ever. So why can’t we just sit like we’re in a dorm room?” It’s very psychologically interesting to me and smart. And who knows if it’s all calculated? Who knows if it’s not? I don’t know. What was interesting, in tracking his physicality in all the videos I saw is, at the beginning, he’s very free in how he twitches and moves. He’s just freer in his ability to be his true self maybe. And then as he starts to become more and more powerful, and ultimately becoming the face of the new world, this behemoth company that’s going to define where we are going—even if he doesn’t know where we are going, and none of them do—he becomes a slightly more homogenized version of a tech CEO. That was a really, really interesting kind of journey. And you can see, now, in [Altman’s recent interview with Vanity Fair], he’s very image-conscious, and I am too. I think we all are.

I mean, yes. One has to. This is the job. And this is why I wanted to do it: Because with The Social Network, I was getting ready to play Mark Zuckerberg, and then David Fincher calls me in and says, “We found someone that is just going to do it better. It’s Jesse, but we think you could also play this part. And you’re a very emotional actor, Andrew, so why would we waste all of that on someone who doesn’t seem to feel many emotions?” So Eduardo became my kind of guy, and I was very happy with that. But it left a little dangling thread, and I was like, “I need to understand.” Because I started to explore what it takes to win, to win on that level.

I think I’m a competitive person. I’m an athlete. I was raised by a swimming-coach father and, at that time, a proud capitalist. My dad has evolved his view of the world over the course of the last decades, which I find very moving and touching, as I have evolved my view of winning and losing and the beauty of losing and how humility is the only thing that actually grows us. It’s fucking awful. I hate losing.

No. It’s so much easier. Because you’re on this fucking false pedestal and it’s so much easier for the shit to get kicked out. It’s like, Oh, wait a minute. I forgot that I got above my station for a minute, and it’s a wonderful, horrible gift to be forced to crawl back down into the mud of what it is to be a human being. And actually I think it does relate to the story that we’re talking about, the film that we’re talking about.

When I was in my 20s, 30s, it was a time of slaying dragons and trying to plant flags and say, “This is who I am.” And then I think maybe as you enter middle age, a bunch of unresolved wounds from childhood that you kind of patched over with success just go, “Hey, we’re still here, dude.” And then it’s time to realize that there’s still a lot of work to do on maturation. I’m sorry, but to keep bringing it back to the film, I think what we’re dealing with is we’re in a very immature culture. We’re being still defined by this kind of adolescent impulse to win at all costs and that losing is weak and vulnerability is weak and that humility is actually not allowed.

What was that T-shirt in the fashion show that was happening recently? “Bring Back Shame” [in designer Conner Ives’s spring-summer show this past September]. I mean, people will respond to that as they do, but how I respond to it, positively, is, Bring back accountability. Bring back accountability for our inhumanity to each other. Humility is a surefire way to get us back to our collective humanity, I feel. But it’s hard. It’s hard. I love winning. It’s so much easier when things are going great, but I think it’s probably better for your soul when things aren’t going so great.

I’ve had a weird few years just in my own life, in a way that I don’t think it’s appropriate for me to go into, just because it’s my private life that I really care about protecting and holding on [to] for myself. But I think it is related to what I feel in the world in terms of the transition we’re in. It feels like we’re in a falling-apart culture, that there is no center anymore. It’s hard to find our own center within that lack-of-center in the world. A lot of people my age who I speak to are feeling a similar sense of a kind of falling apart, which feels right, because as a thing falls apart, the seeds of the future put back together start to get planted. Something else being born. I don’t mean to be glib, I don’t mean to be evasive, because I do like talking. I remember being a young person and wanting people I saw on magazine covers to say something real. So I feel a responsibility to that young person that is out there as well.

I think I was a real pain in the ass to my family because I was like, “What’s that? What’s happening there? That’s fucked-up.” And they’re like, “Shut the fuck up, Andy. Shut the fuck up.” I was that kid pointing out the stuff that wasn’t being said and was being felt, because I could just feel it all. It’s mysterious to me, but it was a hassle for me as well. I was like, “I don’t want to be so sensitive. I don’t want to hug my bully after he beats the shit out of me.” I could just feel his pain. It would be a lot easier to not have to see and feel all of these things that I may just be making up, but I’m pretty sure I’m not. It was a radar that I’ve had since I was a kid, I guess. I think my family would say the same thing, and I think they would also agree that it was a bit of a pain in the ass.

I need a place to go and express all of the most difficult shit about me to other people in private where they’re expressing their most difficult shit about them. I would venture to say, and this is a bit of a bold statement, that we all need that. I happen to have that in my life because I fucking sought it out and I searched it out and I crawled through the woods and into the dirt to find it. I have a really great group of people.

It really happened when I was doing Spider-Man, and I was like, Oh shit, I’m in danger of losing myself. It’s like what they say about famous, really, really famous people. They walk into any room and they’re like, “Everyone’s so nice.” And it’s like, no. I don’t know this person, but you could say, “No, you’re just Tom Cruise, and everyone wants to hang out with Tom Cruise.” I mean, I do know Tom, and I haven’t seen him in a long time. I only bring him up because he’s one of the most famous people in the world and I happen to know him a little bit and really have enjoyed and have a lot of admiration for him. But for me, it was a big danger that I would start to think that was a reflection of who I am. Do you know what I mean? And so I think in order to hold on to my ability to be messy, my ability to lose, my ability to be “weak,” my ability to be all of the things that our culture says a man should not be or even a person should not be, I sought out places and people and spaces and groups that would allow for me just to stay in touch with all of the messiest parts of myself, all the stuff that I’m not going to talk about here because it’s not safe to talk about them here.

Pleasantly. It was completely unintentional. Literally before I was going on to do the show, Stephen’s assistant came into the dressing room with me and my dad and my brother and said, “Hey, he might bring up this. Is that okay?” And I’m like, “Give me a second.” And I checked in with myself. I was like, “Yeah, sure.” Because I love Stephen, I trust him, and I know that he’s very good with those kinds of topics. He’s a really great container in that way. So it was very spontaneous. And then I think I said a prayer before I went on. I was just like, “Hey, Mom, whatever you got, just let me be the channel. Whatever you want me to say, whatever you don’t want me to say, just use me. I’m here to be used for the good, whatever that means.” And then the rest is just kind of a spontaneous moment that was lucky enough to get captured. I got texts about it yesterday, people who lose people, they get sent that clip. There’s a taboo around the subject in our culture—“No, come on, get on with it. Move forward, move forward, move forward”—and also people are just so scared of dying. We’re all so fucking scared of dying because we’ve somehow been led to believe that “Well, not me. I shouldn’t have to fucking die. I know we’re all going to fucking….” It’s this ascension obsession.

Well, it’s immature, and it’s deluded, and it’s a little adorable. It’s like, Oh, that’s so cute. You think you can outsmart the great mystery? Oh my God, it’s adorable. And it’s kind of sweet, but it’s infantile. And it’s cowardly. It’s the scariest adventure, and we’re all going to go on it. Every single one of us, every single one of us, and it doesn’t make sense. I think that’s the place that I find really exciting, is the not knowing. That’s when people get interesting. And I say that with some irony, I suppose, because we are being led by a bunch of younger guys right now who don’t know where this technology is taking us.

There was an overwhelming amount of reporting done about Sam having a specific relationship with the truth. And it was obviously a really vital ingredient in our story. And so I had to get into that for myself. Because he’s very charming by all accounts. There’s the quote in the movie that if Sam Altman was dropped into an island of cannibals, he would be their king in five years. All of these things were so juicy for me to imagine and to merge with and to interpret. I had to look at myself with my relationship to the truth, to universal truth, to personal truth, to objective truth, to subjective, someone else’s truth about me.

But my relationship to the truth, I try to be rigorous with myself. I try to do a fearless searching moral inventory of myself in every single moment of difficulty or tension. I try my hardest. I’m a human being. I’ve definitely not been a perfect human being. I sound like Sam right now. I think in that Vanity Fair interview, he was like, “I’m not a saint.” I’m the same. I mean, maybe this is a question better asked to people who are closest to me in my life, to be honest. I would say that I wouldn’t be a reliable narrator in terms of that. I think there’s a survival instinct in lying. There’s a self-preservation in the lies we tell each other and the lies we tell ourselves, but I wonder how that plays out in terms of the technology that it’s being baked into.

Yes, I do. I think at that time, before The Social Network came out, people were scoffing at the idea of a Facebook movie. No one was really onto the tech industry and the people that were governing it in the same way, because they didn’t consciously pose as much of a threat to our existence. Now we’re all a bit more hip to it. By the public becoming more hip to it, but also powerful people becoming more hip to it, realizing that that’s where the power lies, means that a company like OpenAI will have much more authority and power. For regular people like you and I, it’s higher stakes because we all feel like we have our eyes slightly more open now to the real-world personal consequences of these technologies. So we’re healthily more invested in shining light where that power lies and where we’re giving our autonomy, but also simultaneously there’s a much larger vested interest in protecting those people and their technology.

I feel afraid now. But it’s outweighed by a faith in humanity, but also a faith in the mystery of life. It’s the artist’s responsibility to reflect the times we’re in, and I think it’s getting harder and harder to do that without fear of consequences from those in power who you are rocking the boat for. I was excited the morning of the premiere yesterday. I woke up because I saw Sam’s interview on Vanity Fair and I was like, Oh shit, I’m playing that guy and people are going to see it tonight. And he’s just been named the most powerful man in the world by this publication. And it was kind of like, this is genuinely a cool thing to be a part of and feels courageous being led by Luca, because he says he’s not afraid, which I really admire and respect. I am afraid, but I’m still willing to put it on the line, but we’ll see.

That being said, there’s another part of me that’s like, “I don’t think this is going to touch them in a way.” You know what I mean? There’s a part that’s like, “I wonder if they’re too big to fail, they’re too big.” They’re able to go, “Well, they got the walk right, but everything else is kind of bullshit.” And then they move on because it’s small potatoes to them. I’m not sure. I’m not in their sphere, but I’m really, really, really curious to see how it all plays out.

But then there’s always people that come out and defend them. It’s so strange. Whether they’re bots or otherwise, I’m not sure, but there’s just an army of people that are like, “Leave those rich guys alone.” I’m like, “What?”

No, I didn’t have a body double. No, I saw that interview and I replayed it a few times to try and discern what he was actually saying, because I was very confused by that. I never had any interaction with him. I’ve never seen him in real life. We were shooting outside of his house. It was an eerie day, for sure. We were told that he wasn’t home and his security people were there. I think I saw one security guard. But, no, I never saw Sam, so he maybe mistook someone else for me. I have no idea, but I’ve never waved at Sam. I’ve never seen him. I was curious about that. [A representative for OpenAI told GQ that Altman was home during the filming and reiterated that he waved at Garfield.]

Yikes. That’s scary. It’s not scary in terms of the link. It’s fine by me. It’s more maybe I’m getting into some survival stuff for myself. I don’t want to make really powerful enemies. I don’t want to do that, but I’m not saying that that’s certainly what’s going to happen, but I don’t know if he’s going to feel warm towards me, and that’s okay. But there’s still a part of me that wants everyone to be okay all the time and therefore I’m okay. You know what I mean? It’s a slightly backtracking impulse, but it’s an interesting position to be in.

I’m not concerned about that. And I don’t think Jesse should be either. I think he’s such an incredible artist and an actor and director [and] writer. I feel safe in that I will make other things and this will be a part of my work and that’s that. And there’ll be other things that take away from that. I know the beginning of my obituary will be “Erstwhile Spider-Man.” You know what I mean?

I haven’t really considered it. My desire is to make work that I feel is speaking from my soul every time. That’s kind of where I’m trying to get to in terms of the feeling of relationship with failure and success. You know what something feels like for you, and you know what it means to you, and then the rest is kind of none of my business. So I’ll make a movie and I love it, and then it just doesn’t work for people or it just doesn’t hit in the same way that something that felt very difficult.

And I’m really glad I had the experience, because it was the ultimate version of fame and visibility. To taste that, and to know what that is, felt really healthy in all of its unhealthiness.

I think I’m the same as everyone else who’s even remotely cognizant of where we are and where we’re heading. I look at my friends who do have kids and the beauty of it obviously, but also the struggle of it and the inescapable agony of young people’s longing to belong to the screen rather than longing to belong to the real world. It gives me pause for sure, like any person, I suppose. That being said, there is that kind of biological imperative, and there is that just kind of instinct, and there’s an image that you live with for so long—I have, anyway—of the concept of being a dad and going on that adventure. It’s mysterious to me.

I know that all tech-CEO people, they’re very, very anti-technology in the house across the board. That just is objectively dark, because they know what it does.

It’s the most obvious cliché stuff. I went to an Ezra Collective gig at the Roundhouse in London. It’s a British kind of conglomerate of amazing musicians in the realm of Afrobeat, jazz-inspired. That was a night where you go, Oh, life and humans and arts and bodies and sweat and creativity. I was in Devon recently with my nephews, and we were surfing and swimming and playing American football on the beach. And eating fish and chips. We had a friends-and-family screening in London for this other film I did with Paul Greengrass called The Uprising, which I’m really proud of and I really love, and I love Paul…. And it’s moments like that when a bunch of friends and family come up, and I don’t care how they feel about the film, but the fact that they want to support and be there and that I can be vulnerable with them after the screening and they can be vulnerable with me. And my friends who I hadn’t seen in a long time, we can catch up and be like, “How you doing?” “I’m struggling a little bit.” “Me, too.” Just as simple as that. ✦

#6567. WNBA Finals MVP Gabby Williams on Game 7, Trans Athletes, and Her Cat, Halle Berry

“Print this,” said Joe Lacob, the billionaire owner of the Golden State Valkyries, at the start of the team’s playoff run. “Gabby Williams is the best player in the world."

It was one of the coldest quotes in recent sports memory. But at the time, Williams, the 30-year-old French-American wing, hadn’t yet won a WNBA Championship, much less been named Finals MVP. And, truth be told, no one expected this from the Valkyries, an expansion franchise in only its second year of existence, and one without a single All-WNBA selection on its roster.

No matter. After defeating the Atlanta Dream in a winner-takes-all Game 7 (the first in WNBA history) this past weekend, the Valkyries brought their first ever WNBA Trophy back to the Bay. And it was largely because of Williams, who lived up to Lacob’s promise by executing the most memorable second-half takeover in WNBA Finals history.

Here’s how it went down: With three minutes left in the third, the Valks trailing by six, Williams called for the ball, went between-the-legs, and took a step back jumper from beyond the perimeter. She followed that up moments later with a catch-and-shoot three in Angel Reese’s grill. The following possession, Williams freed herself up from a double team, then sprinted out past the three-point line for a money ball.

Williams’ back-to-back-to-back triples broke Atlanta and gave life to “Ballhalla,” San Francisco’s fortress of 18,064 violet-clad, women’s basketball fanatics. After trailing for most of the game, Golden State snatched the lead and didn’t look back. Williams finished with 28 points in the second half (a WNBA post-season record) on her way to a game-high 30. When it came time to name the Finals MVP, there was little question about which player on an otherwise balanced roster had been most crucial to the Valkyries’ fairytale fun.

Watching Williams, you might not have guessed that, when she’s not hooping, she simply wants to cuddle with her cat and binge-watch anime. Just before the Valks’ championship parade, GQ got to know this smooth assassin a bit more intimately.

No matter what, when I was overseas, as soon as I came home, I would tell HB how my game went. It's the first thing I do. I walk in the door, “Oh, HB, we lost today. I played so bad.” Or “Oh, HB, we won. And this happened.” First thing I do [after winning the WNBA Finals], “HB, we freaking won. Can you believe it?” And she was like, “Can you feed me?”

I love animals, and I do have two family dogs who I absolutely love. But a cat is just like, they have boundaries. Dogs are kind of dumb and just they'll love whoever walks in the door. Once you earn a cat's love, there's no better feeling than that. I'll want to cuddle with HB and she's like, “No, not now.” But when she wants to cuddle, it's like, Oh. And she's definitely a Velcro cat. She sleeps right here [next to my face] and there's just no better feeling.

The coaches knew that Atlanta had been putting a lot of tension on me defensively all series and it was causing me to… It was really tough to find my rhythm. So Nat [head coach Natalie Nakase] was just like, “Let the game come to you. Stay patient. The game will open up.” She's like, “Just wait till the game opens up.” At Halftime, V [Veronica Burton] looked at me, she was like, “What do you need?” And I was like, “I'm good. I'm going to get going. I got it.” She's like, “Okay, I got you.” So V was a really big one in helping me out of that locker room because she just looked at me just like, “I trust you.” And that was huge just to see that in her eyes. And then [assistant coach] Sugar Rodgers, who I've leaned on all season, she was like, “Go off.”

She's like, “I'm waiting for you to go off.” And I was like, “I got you.” KP [assistant coach Kasib Powell] and [assistant coach] Landon [Tatum], both of them were like, “We got the first play for you. Get going, open it up, and the rest is yours." Having that trust after seeing how that first half went and everyone just going like, even with 20 minutes left in the last game of our season, that's not as much of a testament to me, but a testament to the trust that we have as a team.

When I hit that shot, I wasn't thinking about it in the game, but as soon as I hit it and I heard the crowd pop, I was like, “Yeah, they've been waiting for this.” I could tell that the fans were like, “Finally, Gabby. Dang.” But in the most positive way ever. So I saw one go through, and I didn't realize until I came home hours after the game, and then I finally opened social media after the party and stuff, and I was like, “Oh, those three’s were three possessions in a row.” I didn't realize that in the moment.

I've been in deficits before where you come back and all you can think about is stop, score, stop, score. You're not thinking about what happened in the last possession. So I swear, I didn't realize that it was literally three possessions in a row until I came home and watched something I was tagged in. And I was like, “That's kind of crazy.”

I love how he's just so aloof. I think that's what everyone loves about him. My partner has never seen Attack on Titan. I'm like, “There's no way.” So we're actually watching all of it. Well, I'm rewatching it. She's watching it for the first time. I'm like, “This is exactly the show I need to be watching right now during this final series.” So yeah, I channeled Levi. The night before the Finals game, she just started season three, and it's the scene when you first see Kenny [the Ripper] and the famous [scene of Levi] yelling “Kenny” [in battle]. I felt like I needed that.

It's so fulfilling to play for something so much bigger than yourself. I think it's very easy as an athlete to get caught up in personal accolades, check boxes in your career. But I mean, I can compare it to the national team where there’s just a whole different kind of pressure that I love when you're putting a country on your shoulders.

I could feel that here where I'm like, I don't want to let this community down. And seeing that these fans show up from our first game of preseason before anyone knew we would be good, or maybe they did. I didn't want to let them down. I wanted them to celebrate. I wanted my AAU coach to freaking celebrate. I want him to say that he has an ex-player who's a champion. My family, they've been taking flights back and forth to Atlanta, to Vegas. They've been driving up from Reno every day, and I'm like, I want to give this back to them for doing all of this for me.

I never want to compromise my character for my career. I just don't want to compromise my character. At the end of the day, I’m dribbling a basketball while someone is fighting for their lives. And obviously, I’m part of the queer community. Of course, I’m very close to a lot of trans people, and they’re humans. We’re all just human, whether you agree with me or not.

At the end of the day, we all just deserve to have a chance. So it's a no-brainer. I just speak my mind. I speak how I feel. I'm not going to try to be, I guess, media-trained. If I'm saying something and it pisses you off and you want me to compromise my character so you can consume my media better, don't consume anything. Don't watch me play. If it bothers you that much that I care about human lives or whatever it is, that's fine. If I pissed off a bigot, great.

I just hope there's more player relations. What I mean by that is, I wish we had a commissioner who would come and speak to us, have dinner with us, ask us what our experiences are personally, know us on a human level. I think that can go a long way. Obviously, the business of the W is growing exponentially, but it's more than a business, too. We used to be a family, and I'm hoping this next commissioner takes the time to hear us out on our experiences in the league and not just the numbers.

#6566. What You Need to Know About Volufiline, Everyone’s Latest Skin Care Obsession

Social media is full of products that promise to transform your face. The latest one making the rounds? Volufiline, an ingredient being likened to natural filler. Guys are swiping and dabbing it onto their undereyes, smile lines, and lips in the hopes of restoring volume and looking younger with no needles, lasers, or scalpels in sight. Fans credit the topical for improvements in undereye hollows and skin quality and making them get carded again. One X user even claims that it looks like “you’ve genuinely gotten a facelift” after two weeks of use.

The pitch is that Volufiline is basically filler in a bottle. But does it actually check out?

Volufiline is a trademarked cosmetic ingredient made with Anemarrhena asphodeloides root extract (a source of the compound sarsasapogenin) and hydrogenated polyisobutene (an emollient that softens and smooths skin). “The theory is that sarsasapogenin encourages fat cells to develop and store more lipids, making them fuller,” says Kseniya Kobets, MD, director of cosmetic dermatology at Montefiore Einstein Advanced Care. Essentially, the hope is to counteract volume loss that naturally occurs as we grow older.

We’ve spent the last year sampling the latest releases in men’s skin care, hair care, and more. Our favorites will help you level up.

In a small study by Sederma, the French company that manufactures Volufiline, 28 women applied a cream containing Volufiline to one breast for 56 days. While the treated breast’s average volume increased by 2.2%, the result wasn’t statistically significant. Kobets also points out that the comparison was with an untreated breast—rather than the same cream without Volufiline—so it’s unclear if Volufiline or other ingredients in the cream drove the increase in volume. Plus, we can’t exactly extrapolate the results seen in a female breast to a man’s face.

Despite the lack of robust clinical evidence supporting its anti-aging claims, Volufiline is the latest topical du jour that people are crediting with their plumper, more supple skin—namely in areas prone to volume loss like the undereyes, smile lines, cheeks, and lips. Some before-and-afters on social media are visually compelling. But the bigger question is whether Volufiline meaningfully increases facial volume or if the improvements are simply due to a temporary plumping effect—or even the other ingredients paired alongside it.

“Some improvement likely comes from the moisturizing formula: hydration softens fine lines, emollients smooth the surface, and that finish changes how light reflects off skin,” Kobets says. (Changes in lighting and specific camera angles can also make the after photos look more striking.) While Volufiline might help your skin look better, the jury’s still out on whether it meaningfully restores lost volume. “Making skin look hydrated and plump isn’t the same as rebuilding the fat underneath it,” Kobets says.

As for those filler in a bottle claims? Alexandra Bowles, DO, a board-certified dermatologist at MONA Dermatology, sets the record straight. “Volufiline is designed to support volume in the skin, and some people may notice slightly plumper, fuller-looking skin over time,” she says. Meanwhile, injectable filler adds immediate volume and structure and can reshape areas like the tear trough, lips, and jawline. “A cream or serum works at the surface and builds gradually, so it can’t create that kind of change,” she says. Read: While it may complement your skin care routine, it won’t replace or replicate results from filler.

Both derms are open to patients experimenting with Volufiline, but with a few caveats. “I think Volufiline is worth trying if you have realistic expectations, but it’s definitely overhyped as an alternative to filler,” Bowles says. If you really want to (pardon the pun) move the needle on undereye hollows, loss of lip volume, or a less-defined jawline, she says carefully placed filler or other in-office treatments may be more effective.

Kobets doesn’t rule out trying Volufiline, either, so long as you’re not expecting to restore facial fat. That said, she advises being cautious if you have a chronic skin condition like eczema or rosacea, or if you’re acne-prone. Try a patch test first and stop if your skin stings or burns.

Bowles suggests prioritizing reputable brands with clear ingredient lists and modest claims around a gradual improvement in the look and feel of skin. Be wary of products that promise instant results, have vague ingredient lists, or come from unverified sellers. “Don’t experiment with raw ingredients or assume a higher percentage works better,” Kobets adds.

The TL;DR: Volufiline is intriguing, but the hype is outpacing the evidence. More rigorous research is needed to address its volume-enhancing claims for facial skin, so take them with a grain of salt. Consistently using a well-formulated product might help your skin look more supple and hydrated; just don’t expect any miracles or a true dupe for injectables. “Good skin care improves skin quality, but it can’t do every job we’re asking of it,” Kobets says.

#6563. Everything You Need to Know About Fiber and Crohn's Disease, According to Dietitians

Just thinking about fiber can make many people with Crohn’s feel nervous. After all certain fiber-rich foods can be tough to tolerate, but the ones that cause trouble aren’t always obvious. Even a seemingly harmless omelet or a fruit smoothie can trigger symptoms in some people. So if you’ve been avoiding the nutrient, that’s understandable—and not uncommon. Research suggests people with Crohn’s tend to eat relatively little fiber, even during remission. One 2015 study found the average daily intake for individuals with the condition was just 16 grams. For context, experts recommend that men aim for 38 grams a day.

But here’s the thing: Having an inflammatory bowel disease doesn’t necessarily mean you should stick to a low-fiber diet. Some studies have found that eating foods rich in the nutrient may actually lower your risk of future flares, improve gut health, and reduce inflammation not only in your gastrointestinal tract but throughout your body.

We get it, though—navigating the sometimes confusing and land-mine-filled world of fiber can feel overwhelming, especially post-flare, and the stakes are high. No one wants to feel crummy or worry about being stuck near a toilet all day. That’s why we asked a group of registered dietitians to answer some of the most pressing questions about Crohn’s and fiber. Here’s what they said about getting enough without upsetting your stomach, which types may be harder to tolerate, and more.

Let’s take a step back and review some nutrition science 101.

In the simplest terms, fiber is a plant-based carbohydrate our bodies cannot fully breakdown or absorb. It resists digestion in the stomach and small intestine and depending on the type, is either partially or fully fermented by gut bacteria in the colon. This makes it different from other nutrients like fats and proteins, which the body breaks down and absorbs.

There are two main types of fiber, each with its own functions and benefits. Here’s a quick overview of each:

What it is: A type of fiber that passes through the GI tract largely intact, adds bulk to stools, helps keep you regular, and aids in regulating blood sugar. Foods rich in insoluble fiber are typically the ones we think of as hard to digest.

Where to find it: Pear and apple skins, raspberries, wheat bran, leafy greens like kale and collard greens, nuts, seeds, and legumes, among other foods.

What it is: A type of fiber that dissolves in water and forms a gel in the gut, slowing carbohydrate absorption. It helps control cholesterol, increases satiety, and makes stools easier to pass.

Where to find it: Beans, oats, barley, carrots, and the flesh of pears, among other foods.

The short answer: yes. But proceed with caution. During an active flare your fiber intake should depend on what you can tolerate, with amounts gradually increasing as symptoms improve.

“The goal is not to eat as much fiber as possible,” says Anya Argosh, MS, RD, a registered dietitian and the founder of Birchwell Clinic. “It’s to build as much dietary variety as your gut can comfortably and safely handle.”

A lower-fiber diet is often recommended during flares to give the GI tract a “rest.” However, Dahlia Marin, RDN, LD, CGN, a registered dietitian specializing in GI issues and co-founder of Married to Health, says limited evidence supports this approach for people without strictures (abnormal narrowings in the GI tract). “Historically, people with active Crohn’s were often told to follow a low-fiber diet, particularly when experiencing diarrhea, abdominal pain, or increased bowel movements. However, current research does not strongly support eliminating all fiber during a flare,” Marin says.

Instead, Marin recommends tailoring food choices to your symptoms and tolerance.

For example, if certain textures are hard for your body to handle, changing how you prepare foods may help.

Consider “choosing well-cooked, peeled, blended, or puréed fruits and vegetables rather than large amounts of raw, fibrous foods,” she says. That might mean roasting carrots or pears instead of eating them raw in a salad or opting for puréed vegetable soup or smoothie.

During a flare foods high in insoluble fiber—such as fruit and vegetable skins, fibrous leafy greens, nuts, seeds, popcorn, and corn on the cob—may also need to be temporarily limited until symptoms improve.

If you have a stricture, eating softer foods or modifying textures, limiting sources of insoluble fiber, and chewing thoroughly are especially important to help reduce the risk of an obstruction. However, fiber tolerance varies from person-to-person and if you have a stricture, you should work with your healthcare provider to come up with an individualized plan.

Kelly L. Burgess, MBA, RD, LDN, the founder of To Better Health LLC and the creator of the HealthyBites HealthyLife app, says she sometimes suggests a low-FODMAP diet during an active flare. This short-term approach may help people whose symptoms are triggered by FODMAPs, fermentable carbohydrates that can be hard on the gut. High-FODMAP foods include certain beans and legumes, as well as vegetables such as cabbage, cauliflower, garlic, and onions, which can cause symptoms like gas and bloating in some people.

A low-FODMAP diet is typically used on a temporary basis in people with irritable bowel syndrome (IBS) to identify symptom triggers and may help relieve symptoms in people with Crohn’s who have IBS-like symptoms.

Whether you’re coming off a flare or in remission, it’s best to take a “low and slow” approach to adding more fiber to your diet, Argosh says. Introducing too much too quickly can worsen symptoms, leaving you bloated and uncomfortable. “Pace your fiber—don’t force it,” Burgess says. “Begin with small amounts and increase them gradually as tolerated.” In practice, this might mean starting with small portions of well-tolerated fiber-rich foods spread throughout the day, then gradually expanding the types and amounts of fiber you eat at meals and snacks.

Some experts suggest a multi-step approach, starting with easy-to-tolerate, soft fruits, veggies, and grains—think potatoes, white rice, and bananas—and gradually progressing to harder-to-digest salad, seeds, corn, celery and dried fruit.

It’s worth noting that hydration should also increase as fiber intake rises to soften stools and prevent constipation.

When it comes to getting fiber, the dietitians agree that food is generally the best place to start. Fruits, vegetables, whole grains, and other plant foods offer more than just fiber—they also provide other vitamins, minerals, and antioxidants that support overall health and may help with inflammation.

That said fiber supplements can sometimes come in handy, especially when you’re traveling or don’t have access to the foods you typically eat. Finding one that works for you may take some patience, though.

Marin says different supplements can affect your gut in very different ways. “For example, psyllium is a soluble, gel-forming fiber that holds water but is not heavily fermented, while fibers such as inulin and fructooligosaccharides are much more fermentable and can have a greater effect on the gut microbiome.” In practical terms these variations mean one supplement might sit just fine with you and another could leave you feeling gassy or bloated. Which brings us to…

If you’re having trouble eating enough, losing weight without trying, finding yourself cutting out more and more foods, or feeling unsure how to add fiber into your diet after a flare, consider seeing a registered dietitian who specializes in inflammatory bowel disease (IBD). These professionals can help you get the nutrients you need while figuring out which foods and textures work best for you so you don’t unnecessarily restrict your diet. They can also check for nutrient deficiencies and help tailor your diet if you have a stricture, have had surgery for Crohn’s, or are dealing with other Crohn's complications.

A registered dietitian can also help you identify your trigger foods. Triggers generally vary from person to person, which can make them tricky to pin down. Some people tolerate oats and nuts, while others can’t eat either. The same goes for raw fruit and veggies, whole grains, and other fiber-containing foods. It comes down to trial and error: figuring out what works for you—and what doesn’t.

Your gastroenterologist should be involved if you have new or worsening symptoms, particularly if you’re experiencing persistent abdominal pain, frequent diarrhea, blood in your stool, unexplained weight loss, worsening fatigue, and/or vomiting. Get urgent medical attention if you have severe abdominal pain, repeated vomiting, abdominal swelling, or can’t pass stool or gas. These can be signs of an intestinal obstruction, which is especially important to watch for in people with Crohn’s-related strictures.

#6562. A Chef’s Guide to the Best Restaurants in Asheville, North Carolina

By Meherwan Irani as told to Jamie Feldmar

Part of what gives me so much joy about eating in Asheville is recognizing that we’re all part of a very integrated ecosystem. It feels like every restaurant I love is either run by an alum of one of the places I’ve been involved in, or by people that I’ve worked side by side with at hurricane-relief events, or by someone I know I’m going to see at the farmers market.

That’s what makes Asheville special. The folks that come in from out of town are lucky that there’s a virtuous cycle of talent here to keep the pipeline full. It’s so cool to see the evolution of this place, even if we locals don’t always realize how good we have it.It’s almost funny how not a big deal eating out here is. It’s so laid-back. We take it for granted that it’s going to be a great restaurant—of course it is! Having said that, the sheer range of restaurants is incredible. Given how small our town actually is, I sometimes find myself thinking, How lucky are we?

A hallmark of most of the restaurants I love is their connection to nearby farms. At my last count, there were more than a dozen farmers markets happening in Asheville, and there are probably more than twice as many local farms within an hour of here. Most people know the farmers by name, and nobody is precious about it. So when you go to a restaurant, they’ll just say, “It’s Evan’s greens tonight,” and everybody knows who Evan is.

The last thing is that whether it’s an Indian restaurant or a Filipino restaurant or Italian or Spanish, I love the Southern throughline in all of the dishes and hospitality here. That doesn’t mean there’s cornbread or okra in every dish; it’s honoring the fact that we all chose to open Indian or Italian restaurants here in Appalachia, so let’s not divorce the two from each other.

Let’s find a way to make it feel holistic. We do that really well. I was worried after the pandemic and Hurricane Helene that Asheville might not bounce back, but we have. I know that this place is going to continue to trailblaze for years to come.

20 Battery Park Avenue, Floor 1(828) 271-9808

Luminosa is hands down my wife, Molly, and my favorite date- night restaurant. It checks every box so beautifully. It’s intimate, but also a showstopper. It’s visually exciting to be in there, and yet [the design] doesn’t detract from the food, which they describe as “hyper-local Italian.” In the two years that they’ve been open, they’ve picked up a Michelin Green Star and a Michelin Bib Gourmand, and they deserve it. Everything about it just feels very intentional. The food is brilliantly delicious and the menu changes a lot—which sometimes drives me crazy, but it works here, because I know there’s going to be something fun and new every time I go. Their style of service and hospitality is so old-fashioned it almost borders on being antiquated, but I love it. It’s a small town at the end of the day, and everybody touches your table.

One word: respect. Katie Button and her crew have been at the top of their game for so long, you almost forget that continuing to win the championship is really hard to do. But they execute at the exact same incredibly high level as they did the first time I walked in years ago. I go there because what they do is incredible, and it’s always good to be reminded of how well they represent the tapas side of Spanish cuisine. It’s also a really exciting restaurant to be in—there’s an energy and a vibe because of the open kitchen, and it feels so intimate and fun. It’s always packed—they’re a victim of their own success—but even if I’m just going in for a glass of sherry and a plate of jamón ibérico, it’s worth pulling whatever favors I must to get in.

701 Haywood Road, Suite 102(828) 552-3880

This is a true only-in-Asheville pick, a chest-swelling-with-pride kind of choice. The chef, Silver Iocovozzi, worked with me at [two of Irani’s erstwhile restaurants] MG Road and Buxton Hall, and now they get to showcase their own cuisine, Filipino. Neng Jr.’s feels like the kind of restaurant that this city is built for. It’s so tiny and so intimate, with maybe 12 seats, so you’re packed in and you really feel like you’re eating dinner at Silver’s house. The food feels so personal—it’s brilliant and fun and totally them, with flavor profiles that remind me of Indian street food in some ways, full of fermentation and funk.

First of all, this space was converted into a restaurant without doing much to disguise the fact that it used to be a house. You’re dining on the front porch or in the side yard or in the living room, but somehow it manages to make you feel like you’re in a small village in Provence. The food has a French focus, as well as an Americana element. They win all kinds of awards for their wine program, deservedly so. Leo’s is hard to describe. It feels like this rustic little bistro where you might order something as simple as a grilled cheese sandwich and tomato soup, but when it shows up, you realize this could actually be the best grilled cheese and tomato soup you’ve ever had in your life. It’s totally unique and transportive, and it’s become my go-to spontaneous dinner recommendation for friends visiting from out of town.

I’m going to throw it back a little to one of the first places that really popped in West Asheville. Ten or 15 years ago, The Admiral was essentially a dive bar in a cinder-block building next to a gas station where probably 80 percent of Asheville’s pot deals went down. There, a young chef named Elliot Moss [ed note: later Irani’s partner at Buxton Hall] was putting out gastropub food that was blowing people’s minds. After service, they’d push all the tables to the side to clear room for these legendary dance parties. Now the neighborhood isn’t as sketchy, Elliot is long gone, and they no longer push the tables aside, but the food has not lost an inch. When you’re eating, you can just feel that it’s world-class cuisine, and reservations are hard to get, so it still feels special to get to go to The Admiral.

The best way I can describe chef Matt Dawes’s food, or at least my experience of it, is that it’s elevated British pub cuisine with a French influence and a Southern twist. The menu swings from steak frites au poivre to the best shepherd’s pie you’ve ever had to a pitch-perfect Welsh rarebit (it’s also the first place I had Welsh rabbit). The restaurant is located maybe 30 feet from the railroad tracks, next to a dirt field and one of the noisiest outdoor breweries in Asheville, but don’t let that deter you. It’s one of the most reliable and satisfying restaurants in the city.

This is a newcomer to the scene. Chef Ashleigh Shanti Santi, a James Beard Award–winner and prominent Top Chef contestant, has been a passionate advocate for Black Southern foodways. Good Hot Fish is exactly what it sounds like: a fish counter with amazing hot fish sandwiches and seafood, plus the perfect sides. All of the fish she serves comes from local waterways. There’s fried catfish and Gulf shrimp burgers and fish dip with fried saltines. It’s literally just a counter with a few stools against the wall, but the technique is immaculate. The sandwiches are served on squishy white bread with buttermilk tartare sauce, and it delivers on its promise of being the best damn hot fish sandwich you’ve ever had.

I have to give a shout-out here to one of the OGs. To call All Souls just great pizza does it a disservice; you need to understand the backstory. David Bauer, was one of the first bread gurus/savants of Asheville. Farm and Sparrow, his bakery, helped usher in the modern renaissance of heirloom grains and bread that you now see everywhere. He grew his own grain, milled his own flour, and made these amazing breads. Then he helped open All Souls, which makes pizza using stone-ground heirloom grains, in a style I can only describe as Carolina pizza. It’s our own thing, with our own grains, cooked in a wood-fired oven with toppings that come from within 50 miles or so. It’s in a building that was a diner for decades in West Asheville, next to a big old field where you can spread out a picnic blanket if the restaurant is full. All Souls is a classic example of doing one thing, doing it well, and not being too uptight about it, which is everything I love about Asheville.

244 Short Coxe Avenue, #110(828) 505-1003

I have to include two bakeries here. Mother serves a full lunch and dinner menu, so it’s a little more on the restaurant side of things. It’s again in a weird part of Asheville, sandwiched between a sauna and a tire shop, with garage doors that open to a front patio that looks like a loading ramp for trucks. And yet, like so many places here, it’s totally transportive. You feel like you’re eating in Europe somehow. Their jambon-beurre sandwich is the closest I’ve had to Fauchon’s in Paris, and their pumpernickel bread is divine. It feels like a restaurant run by a group of passionate friends that care deeply about food and hospitality, who all got together to open a little place as a gift to the community, because that’s exactly what it is.

I would describe Owl as a bakery with Southern sensibilities and French technique. I’d put the lamination on their croissants up against anything in Stockholm. They’re known for their almond rose croissants, cardamom buns, and squirrel bread [a seed-and-nut loaf], and if you don’t get there right at 8 a.m. they’re going to be sold out. Sometimes I look at the people lining up, and I want to shake each and every one of them and yell, “Do you have any idea how good you have it right now?!” I actually have to resist doing that as I see them gabbing away over their morning pastries and lattes without a care in the world, not recognizing that the rest of us have to schlep around the world God knows how many times to find anything even close to this good in terms of quality and execution. And that’s just life in Asheville.

It’s an honorable mention, because I haven’t stepped foot in the restaurant for 17 years, but I get takeout from Rocky’s almost every Sunday, when I host a men’s group at my house where we discuss things like “How do you actually define electro music?” and play Crocodile, a weird Canadian board game. In the very crowded field of hot fried chicken, Rocky’s stands at the top, because their chicken is actually melt-your-face hot, not “oh-look-how-cute” Nashville hot. Beginners should get the medium. The hot is for veterans, and the extra hot is something the CIA should use to extract information. The crust is thin and it has the perfect amount of crunch, and the seasoning goes all the way through. But most importantly, every week we get the same order, and never once in 17 years have they messed up that order. I feel like a highly underrated aspect of a great restaurant is doing exactly the same thing 52 weeks out of the year, and I have mad respect for Rocky’s.

Recommendations on where to dine, shop, and stay, wherever you’re headed next.

Meherwan Irani, of Chai Pani and Spicewalla, shares his go-to places to eat and drink in his adopted hometown.

By Meherwan Irani as told to Jamie Feldmar

By Meherwan Irani as told to Jamie Feldmar

Part of what gives me so much joy about eating in Asheville is recognizing that we’re all part of a very integrated ecosystem. It feels like every restaurant I love is either run by an alum of one of the places I’ve been involved in, or by people that I’ve worked side by side with at hurricane-relief events, or by someone I know I’m going to see at the farmers market.

That’s what makes Asheville special. The folks that come in from out of town are lucky that there’s a virtuous cycle of talent here to keep the pipeline full. It’s so cool to see the evolution of this place, even if we locals don’t always realize how good we have it.It’s almost funny how not a big deal eating out here is. It’s so laid-back. We take it for granted that it’s going to be a great restaurant—of course it is! Having said that, the sheer range of restaurants is incredible. Given how small our town actually is, I sometimes find myself thinking, How lucky are we?

A hallmark of most of the restaurants I love is their connection to nearby farms. At my last count, there were more than a dozen farmers markets happening in Asheville, and there are probably more than twice as many local farms within an hour of here. Most people know the farmers by name, and nobody is precious about it. So when you go to a restaurant, they’ll just say, “It’s Evan’s greens tonight,” and everybody knows who Evan is.

The last thing is that whether it’s an Indian restaurant or a Filipino restaurant or Italian or Spanish, I love the Southern throughline in all of the dishes and hospitality here. That doesn’t mean there’s cornbread or okra in every dish; it’s honoring the fact that we all chose to open Indian or Italian restaurants here in Appalachia, so let’s not divorce the two from each other.

Let’s find a way to make it feel holistic. We do that really well. I was worried after the pandemic and Hurricane Helene that Asheville might not bounce back, but we have. I know that this place is going to continue to trailblaze for years to come.

It’s always packed—they’re a victim of their own success—but even if I’m just going in for a glass of sherry and a plate of jamón ibérico, it’s worth pulling whatever favors I must to get in.

#6560. 5 Cookbooks That Go Deep on a Single Ingredient

If you’ve ever been starstruck by a perfect Cinderella pumpkin or a stately stalk of Brussels sprouts, you likely understand the appeal of a book focused on a single fruit or vegetable. While books like this aren’t always long, they typically go deep, with the fervor of an author who is fully obsessed.

One charming example, The Core of an Onion: Peeling the Rarest Common Food by Mark Kurlansky, melds authority with a dose of irreverence. First published in 2023, it’s a slim 200 pages, but the writer (who’s also written nonfiction books dedicated to oysters, salt, and cod) packs a lot in. “Onions have a way of taking on unexpected significance,” he posits. The first section covers the allium’s history and cultural impact. (Did you know that ancient Egyptians tied them to mummies’ feet as a preservative?) Kurlansky then offers loosely structured recipes for breads, sauces, and more, all centering the humble bulb.

Elevating a perhaps overlooked ingredient is a core tenet of single-­subject cookbookery. In The Compleat Squash: A Passionate Grower’s Guide to Pumpkins, Squash, and Gourds, a canonical 2004 ode to members of the Cucurbitaceae ­family, master ­gardener Amy Goldman Fowler’s passion is abundantly evident. “Sometimes I don’t know where pumpkins begin and I end,” she writes, before segueing into a series of squash and gourd portraits, each showing an individual specimen more striking than the last. Fowler shares recipes too, in addition to breed spotlights, resources for seed ­buying, and advice on growing your own.

Some such tomes aim to introduce the reader to the joys of less ubiquitous ingredients. Lane Selman and Margarett Waterbury’s Radicchio: A Celebration of the Beloved Italian Vegetable tells the “human and natural history” of the bitter chicory, along with instructions for how to grow it and cook with it. Selman is the founder of the Culinary Breeding Network, an initiative that rallies scientists, farmers, and chefs to geek out over new produce varieties. The book gets weird in the best way possible, with profiles of Italian growers and recipes including gnocchi with radicchio pesto, radicchio with prosciutto ­gratin, and chocolate–radicchio cake (why not?).

Radicchio reads almost like a community cookbook, and that’s intentional. Selman’s inspiration to pursue the topic was the sheer lack of writing on this oddball vegetable: “All of these people that we’ve met on our way down the rabbit hole needed to be included,” she says. “They’re the ones who have given us all this information. Sometimes it takes a village.”

Other single-subject cookbooks showcase pantry ­staples that are rarely in the spotlight. Anna Hezel’s Little Book of Olives: A Guide to Cooking, Eating, and Drinking for the Brine-Inclined presents recipes including cantaloupe and crushed-olive panzanella, Castelvetrano and pistachio pesto, and a whole chapter of olive desserts, plus shopping tips and a breakdown of varieties. The book’s retro aesthetic lends ­gravitas to an ingredient often relegated to garnish status.

Finally, some books exist more to dazzle than to inform, as is the case with pop artist Ed Ruscha and River Café chef Ruthie Rogers’s 2025 release Squeeze Me: Lemon Recipes & Art. In the pair’s high-gloss love letter to the lemon, pop art–coded photos with cheeky captions and a suite of minimalist recipes—such as lemon–­mascarpone tart and limoncello—make this ode to the simple citrus equally at home on a coffee table or the kitchen shelf.

While styles and forms vary, these books serve a ­common goal: to illuminate the sometimes surprising pleasures of a particular fruit or vegetable. That they manage to do so in such delightful fashion is proof that, once in a while, ­working with less really is more.

Hungry for even more stories to sink your teeth into?

These laser-focused titles geek out on radicchio, lemons, olives, and more.

#6559. Silver Fox at 16. How Men Are Embracing Gray Hair at Every Age

At 16, James Chudley’s first gray hair was spotted by the girl sitting next to him in Biology. He thought it was a joke. She took a photo to prove it. There it was: one silver thread, just above his ear. “For the rest of the year,” he says, “every Biology lesson, it was: ‘Oh, there’s another one!’”

Chudley, now a personal trainer-in-training from Surrey, England wasn’t too bothered. “I thought I’d just pluck it out,” he says. By 19, though, there were too many to pluck. An anti-gray shampoo turned his bath blue; dye was another option, but he had reservations: “I didn’t want to end up looking like Dracula.”

Away from school, Chudley gradually got comfortable with his grays. Even so, he mentioned them before anyone else could, “almost as a defense mechanism.” Usually the response was playful roasting, sometimes even a compliment. But then someone would catch him off guard. Chudley, now 22, was recently working as a delivery driver when a customer looked him up and down. “Bit young to be going gray, isn’t it?” she said. Almost as an afterthought, she added, “Is it stressing you out?”

Gray hair is an inevitability for most men. Live long enough, and the color starts to drain. For many people, that begins in their early 30s and becomes more visible with age. By 50, suggests a study by the British Association of Dermatologists, up to 23% of us will have half a head of gray hair. The science is straightforward. Hair follicles contain melanocytes, the cells responsible for producing pigment. As we age, the stem cells that replenish them become less active, and the color fades. It’s like a printer cartridge running out of ink: the paper keeps coming, but eventually, there’s nothing on there.

Going gray in your teens and early 20s, however, is another matter. It’s relatively uncommon, although anecdotes are easy to find. Spend five minutes on TikTok and you’ll see plenty asking why they’re suddenly gray. Reddit has versions too, with users swapping stories and theories about why they—and seemingly everyone around them—are losing their color so young. “I don’t know if you’ve noticed, but people’s hair seems to be turning white really fast lately,” says one post. “How did we end up in the worst century where literally anything can go wrong?”

It’s hard to say if we’re actually going gray earlier, or if it’s just that more people are talking about it. The theories, meanwhile, keep on coming—from mineral deficiencies to heavy-metal exposure. There’s also an optimistic explanation: More people are open to embracing it.

Young men are spending more money than ever on looking good—even on their grays. Rather than shopping for cheap cover-ups, they are forensically researching science-backed cosmetics. Part of this also means a rejection of chemical-heavy products and a more skeptical attitude towards unnecessary cosmetics. Hair dye, after all, is not entirely harmless.

Online communities like “Gray and Proud” and “Silver Revolution” have tens of thousands of members. Paul Mescal and Jeremy Allen White have also done their part for gray-hair representation among young(ish) people: The Times dubbed them “silver fox cubs.”

Maybe things really are shifting. But if the demand for anti-gray products is any indication, the revolution is still a way off. Anti-gray serums are becoming big business. The global market is expected to jump from around $1.3 billion to $2 billion by 2031, according to Deep Market Insights. Cosmetics Business magazine calls it the “next men’s grooming trend,” with growing interest in peptide-based products. On the internet, you see both sides. On Reddit, a 26-year-old guy shares a photo of his gray head of hair, saying that he’s already got more than his dad. The top comment reads: “Do your due diligence, of course—bloodwork, diet etc.” Someone else follows up with: “[Guys] with gray hair are [hot].”

One person making the case for Gen Z’s grey-haired men is model and musician Charlie Jeer. The 24-year-old first found fame on the Netflix dating show Too Hot to Handle. He has the chiseled jawline and cheekbones of a conventional heartthrob, but there’s another feature viewers can’t seem to get past: his hair. “I feel like it gets brought up in conversation every single day in my life,” he says.

Jeer first noticed the grays as a teenager, and mostly shrugged off the comments. Online, it’s a different story. “Why are you going gray?”; “Can’t tell if bro is 15 or 60; ” “Err this old man.” “It’s relentless,” he says.

Now, Jeer sees the grays as an opportunity to stand out. They’ve helped build his profile as a model, too, partly because of the “age ambiguity” they give him. He’s picked up credits with brands like Davidoff and Adidas. “There’s a massive commercial potential for me to make a black hair dye and just start marketing that,” he says. “I never did because I was like, ‘No, I’m proud of my hair the way it is.’”

On TikTok, Jeer takes on his critics with captions like: “Joke’s on them when I’m a silver fox by 30.” He really likes it when he hears from other young guys going gray. “I’ve had a little gray-haired community of people come forward and present themselves,” he says. “It’s nice to hear someone else say that they’ve had people come at them for being gray-haired and that they’re going to continue to wear it with confidence.”

Jeer might be on the young end of the gray-hair population, but as a guy, he’s riding a well-worn track. Men have always been afforded the privilege of associating their grays with looking more distinguished. You don’t have to be George Clooney (or a Too Hot to Handle contestant) to benefit from that. Women, meanwhile, aren’t part of that deal: gray hair is more often simply read as old, an obviously unfair double standard.

It still come with baggage for men, though: age, lost virility, fading relevance. “The first gray” is practically a trope in its own right—a visual shorthand in films, books and TV for a character beginning to age, often followed by the inevitable midlife crisis. There are dozens of those scenes, in everything from The Simpsons to Vanilla Sky.

Matt Newman is a New York–based hairstylist who hates his gray hair. “There are so many examples of men with premature grays that people love and adore,” he says. “But in your 20s, that might not be an aesthetic you’re ready to dive into.”

Perhaps that’s why Jackass star Johnny Knoxville waited until 49 to finally embrace his natural hair color. He started going gray in his 20s and dyed it for most of his life. Then, during lockdown, he asked his wife to give him a buzz cut to reveal the silver underneath, on Instagram. “I should start a challenge but I don’t want to call anyone out,” he wrote on Instagram. Going gray, declared one Mr. Porter headline, was now a “punk power move”.

That’s not quite how Saavan Varambhia, 25, a data analyst from Buckinghamshire, would have described it. Not at first, anyway. Like Chudley, he was at school when a group of female friends spotted his grays and tried to pluck them out. “I was like, ‘Hmm, I don’t know how I feel about this,’” he says. A few years later, he had enough gray hairs on his head to “stop people in their tracks.”

For Varambhia, the contrast was hard to miss. He’s South Asian, with naturally deep-black hair, so the grays stood out. The Journal of Clinical and Aesthetic Dermatology reports that people of Asian descent also tend to go gray later, often in their late 30s. According to Professor Desmond Tobin, a professor of dermatological science at University College Dublin, many Eastern cultures have traditionally been less accepting of grays than the West, with men and women both more likely to dye them.

“At first, I felt like I didn’t want to seem a lot older than my peers,” Varambhia says. Over time, though, he came to see the silver as something that set him apart. “I’ve always been one to stand out, and it was another attributing factor to that.”

When there are too many gray hairs to pluck, do you reach for the Just for Men, or join the silver fox cub club?

Turning gray at any age can make you wonder why it’s happening. According to Tobin, the answer is usually in your DNA. “The first thing to do is just to look at your [previous] generation of relatives,” he says. “They’re going to give you a road map for where you’re going to be. And if you have early grayers—teens, early 20s—then that’s really mapped out quite starkly in the family.”

Other things can speed up the process, too. Stress is a big one. Tobin says it can damage the cells responsible for producing hair pigment, acting like a “sledgehammer” if the process has already begun. Just look at Barack Obama’s hair before and after his time in the White House. Nutrition can play a role, too, and a blood test can pick up any deficiencies. But Tobin says the nutritional deficiencies linked to premature graying tend to show up in countries where malnutrition is more widespread.

Crucially, though, Tobin says he hasn’t seen hard evidence that young people are going gray earlier across the board. Modern life might come with its share of chronic stressors, he points out, but humans have been enduring trying times since long before social media, climate change, and cost of living crises entered the picture.

Tobin is skeptical of the “overstated claims” made by supplements and serums that promise to “tickle your stem cells.” If you want to slow the process, he says, your best bet is the boring stuff: less stress, plenty of sleep and an antioxidant-rich diet. That might hold things off for a while. But for anyone genetically predisposed to graying, it might not make that much of a difference.

“Would maximum antioxidant intake really stop somebody from graying who’s genetically headed that way? It’s not likely to be a very significant contributor,” Tobin says. “It might, in a multi-prong way, add up to something, but if you’re graying significantly in your teens, that is probably the die that’s being cast for you.”

The good news? Going gray early doesn’t mean the rest of you is aging at the same rate. In fact, there may be a silver lining. People who go gray prematurely are more likely to keep their hair; “nearly all people who bald [do so] with their natural hair color,” says Tobin.

But for anyone who doesn’t want to live with their grays, the hunt for a treatment is on. Research into gray hair has surged alongside the wider boom in longevity science. Tobin says the past five years have felt like a return to the 1980s, when the topical hair-regrowth drug Rogaine first shook up the haircare world. He has consulted for startups developing anti-gray treatments aimed at reducing oxidative stress or extending the life of melanocyte stem cells. The science is promising, but so far, nothing has produced evidence strong enough to satisfy regulators.

Researchers are beginning to understand that graying may not be irreversible, as once thought. Melissa Harris at the University of Alabama has studied the melanocyte stem cells behind hair pigmentation, and immunotherapy as a way to reverse graying. Other research has shown that some hairs can spontaneously regain their pigment, with changes appearing to track periods of stress. It hints that some aspects of aging may be less linear than we assume.

Dr. Greg Williams, a hair restoration surgeon at the Farjo Hair Institute, has watched the field develop. He hosts conference sessions on gray hair and, a couple of years ago, set up a WhatsApp group where specialists around the world could swap ideas and compare notes. “Gray hair is very poorly understood,” he says. “There’s not a lot of research in humans around it.” Maybe that’s surprising, given how universal it seems to be. But commercial fallout is huge. The global hair-and scalp-care industry is worth nearly $9.4 billion, and Williams thinks anyone who figures it out “would be a Nobel Prize winner”.

As for Varambhia, the science seems to fit his experience. Genetics are clearly part of the picture: his dad’s hair has been white for years, and he still has a full head of it. Varambhia also suspects stress sped things up. His grays arrived rapidly as he settled into corporate nine-to-five life. Since hitting the gym regularly, he thinks the process may have slowed.

Knowing other people going through the same thing has helped. “I know eventually I’ll go fully gray. I hope it’s a bit later on than sooner,” he says. But when the time comes? “I’ll embrace it.”

A version of this story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of British GQ with the title “Gray Ahead of Schedule”
