#6556. Why Do We Keep Coming Back to Godzilla?

Like most people born in the western world after 1954, I knew of a monster named Godzilla before I’d heard of a country called Japan. It’s not a rare perspective. For as long as nearly any living person can remember, Godzilla has been hugely popular, far exceeding the global recognition afforded most leaders, athletes, and certainly his human co-stars, those men and women whose dramatic dealings are the universally agreed-upon bargain for apocalyptic havoc that millions upon millions have gladly paid to see for over 70 years.

What Godzilla hasn’t been is prestigious. The irradiated dinosaur of very few words—mainly deafening screams—is a beloved figure more in the key of Harry Potter, Batman, or James Bond than the loftier fare that typically gets recognized by major festivals or awards bodies. But exceptions to the rule do exist. The Criterion Collection issued Ishiro Honda's 1954 original on a deluxe Blu-ray in 2012 and marked their 1,000th release with a lavish set of Godzilla’s Shōwa era (1954-1975), which both documents film history and calls to mind John Huston’s Chinatown line about sticking around long enough to become respectable. Writing an introduction to Honda’s biography A Life in Film, from Godzilla to Kurosawa, Martin Scorsese deemed the series’ first director "an extraordinary artist and craftsman" and placed Honda’s 1954 film (alongside spin-offs Mothra and Rodan) among “pictures that haunted the imaginations of young moviegoers like myself and millions of others for years to come.” Kiyoshi Kurosawa—perhaps Japan’s greatest living filmmaker, and certainly its finest practitioner of austere horror—deemed Honda’s film an early lesson in national trauma. Thomas Pynchon, too, had ambitious plans to write a novel about the insurance adjuster who responds to Godzilla’s wreckage (the idea never fully materialized, though it did become a nice tangent in his 1990 novel Vineland). We can look at the films and countenance such appreciations from geniuses. In the meantime, good luck to anyone counting the exact number of action figures, coloring books, sticker packs, and lunchboxes boasting Godzilla and friends.

A critical shift started somewhere in the last decade. Toho ostensibly shelved Godzilla with 2004’s Final Wars, a grand finale that was the big-budget equivalent of a child smashing toys. (That is a compliment.) Godzilla LLC boasts enough films and ancillary products to be financially solvent until the sun burns out (or an indomitable monster rises from the sea), but that retirement was short-lived. Toho resurrected the franchise in aggressive form with 2016’s Shin Godzilla, both a complete series reboot and (across several stages, each grotesque and terrifying) character reinvention that dispensed with typical monster-movie hijinks. An unholy experiment that would make the series’ many mad scientists proud, Shin Godzilla fused the hilarious bureaucratic hallway-rushing of In the Loop or Veep with cosmic horror befitting its director, Hideaki Anno, whose contributions to Japanese culture are nearly Godzilla’s equal on the strength of Neon Genesis Evangelion alone. With time, it’s downright eerie to see Anno’s foreshadowing of the global unrest of Trump, Brexit, COVID, and our endless misinformation era. But rather than keep the great filmmaker on retainer for any number of sequels or spin-offs, Toho regrettably followed Shin Godzilla with an anime trilogy produced for Netflix, a rare class of films I’ve been too embarrassed to even log on Letterboxd.

Whether genius or dreck, none of these films quite anticipated 2023’s Godzilla Minus One. It’s one thing to become the highest-grossing Japanese-language release in U.S. history—any Godzilla movie that doesn't at least aim for such a distinction isn’t worth its salt. It’s harder to imagine anyone, even or especially the franchise’s most ardent fans, anticipated lavish public praise from a quintet of Best Director winners in Steven Spielberg (who claims to have seen the film at least three times), James Cameron (who offered to work on a sequel), Christopher Nolan, Guillermo Del Toro, and Bong Joon-ho. In a recorded conversation with Yamazaki, Tom Cruise and Christopher McQuarrie credited the film as an inspiration; the actor went so far as to call it “perfect.” Which is to say nothing of the fact that Godzilla Minus One earned the series its first Oscar, a VFX prize awarded to none other than writer-director Takashi Yamazaki—a nice encapsulation of the film’s balance between spirit and spectacle.

Because Godzilla Minus One resonated more than anyone could have anticipated, Godzilla Minus Zero, which arrives in theaters this weekend, shoulders the burden of expectation. The first shot across the bow was its world premiere at last month’s New York Film Festival, where Zero debuted alongside prestige fare like Luca Guadagnino’s Sam Altman biopic Artificial and Tony Gilroy’s star-studded Behemoth! There, NYFF’s artistic director Dennis Lim introduced the movie to a sold-out crowd at the Alice Tully Hall, praising Yamazaki for exemplifying "the endless possibilities of creative VFX and genre filmmaking." Minus Zero indeed thrives on spectacle: its opening sequence blazes a merciless path of carnage aided by an elegant long take, while subsequent scenes centered on one of Godzilla’s classic foes, the three-headed Ghidorah, inspire genuine terror more than cheap thrills. This is the first Japanese film shot for IMAX, a large-scale exhibition that’s often torqued for discomfort. Yamazaki’s centerpiece battle pitting the monster against a squadron of fighter planes is dogged by fatalistic doom: a mother and child screaming as their house crashes to the ground don’t get the last-minute salvation we’ve come to expect, and even the sentimental-seeming inclusion of a pilot named Honda is complicated by his eventual fate.

If not quite so grave as the visions of death that imprinted upon a young Kiyoshi Kurosawa, Yamazaki’s narrative—including a face-melting sequence that U.S. distributor GKIDS have asked not be spoiled—provides a stark contrast to the bloodless carnage of American blockbusters. It’s quite the cultural coup, too, that a film this brutal and generally skeptical of American imperialism has secured a simultaneous international release: every English-speaking actor (none recognizable, each portraying arrogant allied military) bears an amusingly off-market quality à la the nefarious white time-travelers of 1991’s altogether underrated Godzilla vs. King Ghidorah.

Nearly all Godzilla films are a total restart. Minus Zero is immediately notable as a direct sequel. The results suggest that Yamazaki overestimated his initial project. Godzilla is not much of a presence in Minus Zero, first appearing around the 50-minute mark and then again in a suitable, albeit brief climax. Far more time is spent with those we came to know and like during Minus One, each reprised by an entirely competent ensemble that fails to elevate interest higher than a certain co-star’s 50 meters. (The protagonists’ adopted daughter from Minus One returns here for the sole purpose of standing in place and crying.) Whatever its pretensions towards grandeur, this is still a film where a scientist explains his master plan by smashing together two monster figures. It takes little to delight in this moment, a literal and figurative collision of serious storytelling and childlike abandon. More of that spirit might’ve served the final picture.

My mild disappointment is nothing compared to the agony Godzilla has wreaked on the district of Ginza, which last week hosted the first public screenings of Minus Zero through the Tokyo International Film Festival. Godzilla’s annihilated the city’s landmarks enough times that when we think of all-out destruction on the sprawling megalopolis that is Tokyo, we’re probably picturing its uber-fashionable shopping sector laid to ruins. When he emerged from the sea in 1954, he wrecked the district’s historic Nichigeki Theater, which was again destroyed (in real life, of humans’ accord) and reopened as the Yurakucho Mullion building in 1984—the same year as The Return of Godzilla, where he inflicted yet more damage on the brand-new structure. Godzilla Minus One recreated the Nichigeki Theater in loving detail only to destroy it again in a sequence that deployed modern visual effects to cause maximum tactility and human misery. That Hiroshima-like site is revisited in Minus Zero, leading one character to declare that “Ginza is again the battleground.” Time’s healed those wounds, however. The night before Minus Zero’s Japanese premiere, a small crowd gathered outside the Yurakucho Mullion’s Seiko watch for an extravagant display: the clock lifted, and out came a small set of Godzilla puppets that strike chimes while Akira Ifukube’s iconic theme plays.

Ginza’s Wako Clock Tower has fared no better: it was torn to ribbons in the 1954 original, 1995’s Godzilla vs. Destoroyah, Shin Godzilla, and Minus One. After a time you must wonder what keeps bringing Godzilla back to the neighborhood. Is it animal instinct, or mere coincidence, or an atomic hatred for its glowing consumerism? The Wako Annex, a nearby pastry shop that Google not-inaccurately describes as “chic,” currently advertises a Christmas-themed cake recreation of the oft-doomed Clock Tower with Minus Zero’s monster looming over it; accompanying text informs us that “Godzilla strikes Ginza’s Wako once again.” The nearby Toho cinema is within Hibiya Godzilla Square, featuring a small but impressively detailed statue and a plaque reading, “The human race must coexist with Godzilla.” Immediately adjacent is a three-story shopping mall hosting a Toho store selling numerous Godzilla ephemera. (I bought the tea cup.)

Even Americans accustomed to the fetishization of disaster would be impressed by Japan’s ability to marry suffering and spectacle to great financial gain. Perhaps it’s the same haunted fascination of a young Martin Scorsese or Kiyoshi Kurosawa cast unto the world at large; Godzilla might be scary as he destroys Ginza, but he’s something to admire when shrunk down to human size and displayed inside the Yurakucho Mullion’s lobby. (The photo-snapping crowds that gather every time I walk past suggest that bit of marketing paid off.) Even as Minus Zero’s first reviews show a less rapturous response than Minus One, this series’ viability—and its newfound prestige—shows little indication of flagging. Ginza’s residents were beyond happy to see Godzilla, at least in ossified, action-figure form. But who's to say they wouldn't be just as excited if, one day, his path of destruction broke through the screen?

#6553. 35 Gifts for Boyfriends Who've Been Very Good This Year 2026

The internet has some peculiar ideas about what constitutes a good gift for a boyfriend. Search long enough and you'll find yourself in a purgatory of ergonomic footrests, rechargeable headlamps, novelty T-shirts, and footwear that seem designed to extinguish whatever romantic spark remains. Surely the person you love deserves something a little more inspired.

So we've assembled a collection of gifts that reflect what we hope is a more generous understanding of the guy you're actually dating: the things that'll actually tickle his fancy. Because whatever the gifting occasion, it doesn't call for another set of monogrammed rocks for his bourbon.

Still on the hunt? Explore the rest of our gift guides, and sign up for the GQ Recommends newsletter for a steady supply of gift ideas that have our seal of approval.

A mainstay of Antwerp label Howlin's knitwear lineup, the Birth of the Cool is made from 100% Scottish Shetland wool. A very good sweater with a very good name.

A rich shade of green, a great collar, and that little pony perched on the pocket. This cotton corduroy chore coat from Polo Ralph Lauren is just the layer for the cooler months.

The first step in closing the swag gap is a first-rate white tee. Merz b. Schwanen makes them in all manner of cuts and styles, but the 215, famously worn by Jeremy Allen White in The Bear, hits the sweet spot.

Buying pants for a guy can be a delicate business, but Buck Mason makes it easy. The brand's Big Chino, with its generous, full-legged silhouette, is a safe bet for just about any wardrobe. Worth browsing the rest of the lineup, things tend to sell out quickly.

Earlier this year, a patchwork Shetland sweater from a 1996 J.Crew catalog became an unlikely object of obsession in certain stylish corners of the internet. This one isn't quite the same, but it's got enough of that vintage J.Crew magic to scratch the itch without having to scour eBay for the original.

Colbo and Sperry are on a roll. Their latest collaboration, the Sirah loafer, is an new-new silhouette that lands somewhere between a vintage dress shoe and a ballet flat, with a low-slung profile and Sperry's signature grippy sole.

This Timex and Todd Snyder watch takes its cues from the watchmaker's 1960s Marlin, with a slightly smaller case, a classic dial, and a handsome suede leather strap. A lot of watch for the money.

It wouldn’t be a GQ gift list without a nice tie. This silk-jacquard number from mfpen fits the bill, and won’t set you back too much, either.

Tekla and Auralee are back for their second collaboration, and these checked pajama pants sit right in the middle of the Venn diagram of cozy and covetable. Definitely a pair he should wear out of the house.

Handsewn in Maine, Easymoc’s Cozymoc pairs a full-grain leather upper with a 100% shearling lining for shuffling around in comfort. A Vibram sole makes it fit for trips outside, and it’s resoleable, too.

If he’s taking skincare cues from anyone, it should be Pharrell, who apparently doesn’t age. The multihyphenate’s skincare brand, Humanrace, has a Rejuvenating Face Mask featuring a peptide trio complex to keep your lucky guy looking chipper.

When it comes to skincare, sometimes it takes a tool to help a guy take care of himself. This one from Korean skincare brand Abib packs retinal to help reduce the appearance of under-eye bags, with a built-in metal rollerball that makes application a breeze.

The whole point of a gift set is to give him something he wouldn’t try himself. Fortunately for you, after using this Mojave Ghost body wash, lotion, and hand cream, he’s never going to want to go back to whatever he was using before.

The Gap '90s nostalgia machine is running at full tilt, and this year the brand went digging through its fragrance archives, bringing back the scents that once perfumed an entire generation of mall-goers. T

Big news in sunscreen: BEMT, a UV filter long used in European formulas, has finally made it stateside. It offers broad-spectrum UVA and UVB protection and helps make those lightweight formulas we’ve long envied across the pond possible. Sunscreens containing it are starting to hit shelves, and one of our office favorites is from French pharmacy staple La Roche-Posay.

Couldn't care less about BEMT? Fair enough. If convenience is what he's after, Freaks of Nature's SPF 50 stick makes a strong case for keeping things simple. It's pocket-sized, glides on without a fuss, and offers 80 minutes of water resistance. No excuses for skipping sunscreen now.

Shaving with a safety razor is a lost art, and we say it's high time for a revival. Bevel's shaving kit has everything he needs to get acquainted with the old-school ritual, and maybe even start looking forward to his morning shave.

The Philips OneBlade 360 is a little groomer we love for its versatility. There’s really nothing quite like it out there, and it can handle just about any hairy situation.

The new Beats 360 headphones sound great and look the part. Swappable performance-knit cushions let you customize the look, while a sweat- and water-resistance rating makes them a rare pair of gym-worthy over-ears.

Ray-Ban’s Audio Clubmasters pack open-ear audio and six built-in microphones into a classic frame. Best of all, he can enjoy the latest tech without making everyone wonder if they’re being filmed.

Because you deserve more attention than his algorithm does, there’s the new Brick Night, which automatically blocks distracting apps whenever his phone comes within range. Unlike the original Brick, no physical tap required. Just park it on his nightstand and let it do the rest.

Teenage Engineering's Pocket Operators are calculator-sized music machines with a cult following. The PO-12 Rhythm packs 16 drum sounds, a sequencer, and a slew of effects into one tiny device, and syncs with other Pocket Operators to build out a whole little setup. Don't be surprised if he starts collecting them.

Keychron's new K3 Ultra is one dapper keyboard, with a low-slung profile, metal-and-wood frame, and three switch options to choose from. A fine introduction to mechanical keyboards, and plenty to geek out over for the already converted.

UAG’s ultra-thin tracking card might be the most practical gift in this guide. One of the few that plays nice with both Apple and Android, it lets him pinpoint his wallet’s location on a map or make it chirp when his scatterbrain misplaces it.

I picked up Nanu’s Arc earlier this year, and I’ve gotten way more joy out of it than I expected. It’s a well-built, thoughtfully engineered analog clock with a discreet digital display and a lovely mechanical chime. Not cheap, sure, but having one less reason to reach for my phone has been worth every penny.

If you're going to veg out on the couch with your better half, do it under something sumptuous. Brooklinen's Super Fluff throw is all plush faux fur and silky-smooth shag, made for the cold winter ahead. And yes, it's machine washable.

John Waters warned against going home with someone who doesn't own books. I'd say the same rule goes for wine openers. And this one from Craighill really is the best.

Our Place’s pans are take-it-or-leave-it, but the Wonder Oven Pro is the real deal. Yes, it’s an air fryer, but precise temperature and time controls mean it can bake, roast, grill, dehydrate, and even proof dough. With three rack positions and enough room for a whole chicken, it’s a serious little oven. Looks damn good, too.

You could get him a candle. Or you could get him this handsome little contraption: a glass teardrop set in a brass cradle. Fill the glass with fragrance oil, light the tealight underneath, and voilà: instant home aura.

Hoto’s sleek PixelDrive electric screwdriver comes with 30 interchangeable bits and a nifty pixelated display that shows exactly how much power he’s working with. That wobbly dining chair doesn’t stand a chance.

You’ve probably seen Satisfy’s hole-riddled MothTech tees everywhere. Those strategically placed perforations probably do wonders for ventilation, but let's be honest: they just look sick.

Cold-weather running is a lot more appealing when you've got the right gear. Lululemon's swishy Pace Breaker track jacket keeps the chill at bay without weighing down their stride.

Give the guy an excuse to take a load off. Lookout & Wonderland’s Magic Bath combines mineral-rich Japanese sea salt, French green clay, and nourishing oils with 500 mg of CBD for a seriously luxurious soak.

DedCool’s cult-favorite Xtra Milk scent gets the body scrub treatment, which means he can exfoliate his way to softer skin while smelling like the best version of himself.

Bowflex’s SelectTech dumbbells are about as functional as fitness equipment gets. A quick turn of the dial adjusts the weight, replacing an entire rack of dumbbells without turning his place into an Equinox.

The new Nike x Hyperice Air Zoom HyperSlide delivers targeted heat and vibration massage across the top of the foot, giving his hardworking dogs some well-earned R&R, rest day or not.

#6551. Montana’s Newest Congressman Dresses Like a Ralph Lauren Model

You couldn’t ask for a better embodiment of the blue wave than Sam Forstag, the 32-year-old rookie Montana Democrat who defeated his Republican rival in yesterday’s midterms to flip an important House seat. While unaffordability was a key issue in the midterms across the country, it’s especially noticeable in Montana, where an influx of billionaires and tech companies has sent the cost of living soaring. Like many of his compatriots, Forstag campaigned on promises of affordable housing, better healthcare, and getting money out of politics. Unlike his fellow Democratic challengers, however, he did it in a wardrobe that looked straight off a rack at the RRL store.

Last night, in front of a crowd of supporters in Missoula, Forstag delivered his victory speech dressed in a hickory-striped work shirt, jeans, a big western belt buckle, and a pair of weathered brown forestry boots. Forstag’s fit stands in contrast to the standard election night business-casual look favored by political hopefuls on both sides of the aisle, but he’s far from the first candidate to lean into the dress codes of blue-collar Americans. From Tim Walz’s camo hats and Filson Mackinaw jackets to John Fetterman’s Carhartt hoodie, workwear remains a potent symbol of working-class solidarity. In this case, however, it’s not just blue-collar cosplay.

Forstag at a student get-out-the-vote gathering at Montana State University in October.

Straight out of college and looking to pay off his student loans, Forstag joined the US Forest Service as a wildland firefighter and spent the next eight years battling blazes around the country, including four years as a smokejumper parachuting into fire zones. As a result, he comes by his love of Levi’s and sturdy, Goodyear-welted footwear honestly, and—Montana being a place where jeans and work shirts are considered business-casual for many—didn’t need to change his look for the campaign.

Forstag addressing voters at a town hall in August.

As authentic as Forstag’s election-night outfit was, he also appears to be a guy who understands the power of styling. While his shirt likely didn’t have Ralph Lauren’s initials on the tag (wildland firefighters only make $20 an hour), the way he wore it—half unbuttoned, layered over a red thermal henley, and tucked into his jeans—owes a debt to the romantic take on Western style Lauren has been trafficking in since the ’70s. Or, equally likely, the Montana cowboys who the designer borrowed it from. Either way, Forstag’s look, like the progressive politics he and his Democratic colleagues campaigned on, will be a welcome disruption to business as usual on Capitol Hill.

#6548. Baltimore Yat Gaw Mein

Yat gaw mein, also known as Baltimore yak or dirty yak, is Baltimore carryout comfort food at its finest. A Chinese American remix of Beijing’s zha jiang mian, the dish dates back to the late 19th century, when Chinese immigrants started settling in the city. Setting up shop in traditionally Black and working-class neighborhoods, Chinese cooks adapted their recipes to locally available ingredients, swapping fermented pastes for a simpler, soy-based gravy. Over time, something new emerged: savory wheat noodles, stir-fried with shrimp, beef, and vegetables and punctuated with a hard-boiled egg. Today you can find the beloved dish at counter spots around town like Jimmy’s Carryout, Danny’s, and Pimlico Chinese Carry Out.

In this at-home version from Steve Chu, the chef behind Baltimore’s Ekiben, smoky wok-charred noodles are tossed with umami-packed soy sauce and oyster sauce, rich fat, and sweet caramelized onions. What makes the dish “dirty” is the flavor that develops when soy sauce, sugar, and aromatics scorch against the hot metal of the wok, creating deep savory notes balanced by subtle sweetness. Be sure to keep the wok on high heat and stir frequently, since this allows the wok hei—which translates to “the breath of a wok” and refers to that elusive smokiness—to work its magic. The finishing squeeze of ketchup might sound unusual, but it delivers the acidic punch that makes the dish complete.

#6547. Abdul El-Sayed Is Winning at Watches, Too

Michigan voters made history Tuesday night by electing Abdul El-Sayed to the US Senate, as part of a greater blue wave that swept the nation. The 44-year-old former epidemiologist is set to become America’s first Muslim senator—and, in a slightly more trivial milestone, might also be the first real-deal watch nerd to reach the Capitol.

During his victory speech, the senator-elect sported a Grand Seiko Spring Drive Chronograph (ref. SBGC249), a limited-edition piece from the Japanese watchmaker that pairs a GMT (which tracks the time across multiple time zones) and a chronograph (i.e. a stopwatch function). Politicians tend to go one of two ways with their timepieces: Either they’ll cosplay as a man of the people with an affordable Timex or Casio, or they’ll sport a luxury watch with historical pedigree—like the Rolex “President” Day-Date, worn by several commanders-in-chief—to make themselves appear ready for higher office. An obscure Grand Seiko fits neither of those categories.

Instead, El-Sayed’s Grand Seiko is a burly sport model made by one of the world’s most elegant watchmakers. At 43.5mm, it’s hefty, a good fit for the senator-elect’s beefed-up forearms. It’s quite complicated, with several different functions, and a dial as busy as Little Caesars Arena during a Detroit Pistons game. It costs $12,400, but you’d have to be very deep into the hobby to recognize its value.

What I find so compelling about El-Sayed’s choice is that it doesn’t appear to be masquerading as some political symbol. It’s simply the excellent wristwear of an honest-to-goodness collector. “Folks who know me know I’m a sucker for automatic watches and sort of classic watches,” El-Sayed said in an interview with LGBT Detroit in May.

Throughout his campaign, he’s worn a wide range of pieces. His recent go-to appears to be a Mühle-Glashütte S.A.R. Rescue-Timer, a great-looking and obscure German tool watch. He also owns the U-1 SE, a large dive watch from the German independent brand Sinn.

Not all of El-Sayed’s timepieces are devoid of political meaning, however. The senator-elect has worn an Omega Speedmaster, the watch worn by American astronauts Buzz Aldrin and Neil Armstrong when they first set foot on the moon. That’s about as patriotic as a watch can get without excerpting the Declaration of Independence on the dial. Naturally, he also owns a Shinola Runwell, from the brand based in El-Sayed’s native Detroit. El-Sayed is very aware of what a certain watch can signal about the wearer. In his 2020 book, Healing Politics: A Doctor’s Journey into the Heart of Our Political Epidemic, he wrote about how seeing Rolex billboards along a highway prompted him to reflect on income inequality: “How many people who drive this road can actually afford one of those?”

But when did I realize that El-Sayed was a true-blue watch guy? It wasn’t the dialed-in collection of interesting tool watches that did it, but his appearance at a rally on Sunday. He wasn’t wearing a timepiece at all, surprisingly. Which meant that there, on his wrist, was the mark of a true collector: a watch tan.

#6544. The Best Men’s Rain Boots Ranked From Drizzle to Downpour

The best rain boots for men aren’t a one-size-fits-all shoe. The chance of precipitation may be 100%, but you could be stepping out to a gentle mist, wading through cataclysmic flooding, or taking on sporadic showers—so your choice of shoes and outerwear should adapt to the weather. Popping up out for a quick coffee could require a pair of simple gardening shoes or tall wellingtons depending on whether you’re walking a few blocks or taking your car to a drive-thru.

There are many factors to consider but the most important is that your feet and socks remain dry, no matter the shoe or how heavy the rain is. So take into consideration how tall or short your rain boot ought to be, whether you need grippy soles to get you through some rough terrain or you might have to contend with slush and snow, which inevitably means you’ll need some boots with insulation. Your rain boots and shoes have to contend with a litany of different factors so we rounded up the best options for every kind of wet-weather situation from mild mists to ankle-deep downpours.

For the kind of mediocre rain that has you second-guessing whether you should open your umbrella or just stick it out, you’ll likely get by just fine with a pair of shoes built with a water-resistant or waterproof membrane. Think a slight spray or an annoying mist. Many outdoors-focused shoes and boots come with some type of Gore-tex lining or a coating that helps slough light water. In mild rain situations, your shoes don’t need to be very tall, either. Low-cut gardening shoes or rain-ready sneakers can manage the weather just so long as there’s a low risk of deep puddles along the way.

Norda's trail-ready slip-on runners are an iron-clad pick for any rocky terrain you might encounter, purposely or not. With their lightweight, tear- and water-resistant Dyneema uppers, and crag-grabbing Vibram lugged soles, these bad boys can handle more than a drizzle. Plus, the easy slip-on design is great for when you just need to jam out the door for a quick errand, not to mention for keeping water from sogging your socks.

If the precipitation falls anywhere between a drizzle to moderate showers, I can’t be arsed to bust out my heavier rain boots. I’m unlikely to wear my wellies in showers, too, if I know I won’t be outside for too long. For most situations, a good water-resistant boot will more than suffice. Save your brain cells and just get a pair of Blundstones—they’re tough as nails, reliably dry in the majority of rainy days, and look great with any pant. But if boots on the wellies-chelsea spectrum aren’t your speed, there are plenty of other shoes with rain-prepped details like Gore-tex linings and DWR coatings to keep water at bay.

It's hard to argue against a pair of Blundstones. The quality leather uppers age beautifully, the shape is ruggedly handsome and endlessly styleable, plus the hardy soles can handle most everyday situations regardless of the forecast. No wonder why you see everyone from your local barista to Hollywood's leading men rocking a pair. Their waterproof design is adept at traversing through medium rain, so long as you don't step into any deep puddles, and we like that they don't present as a full-on rubber boot which makes them easier to wear all year long.

What you’re probably imagining when you think of proper rain boots are the kind wrought entirely out of a block of rubber, reaching up to your calf, without any laces. Some people refer to these as Wellingtons—or wellies—whose origins go back to the Duke of Wellington, and though they were originally made of leather, are now mostly thought of as a rain-ready boot. Their seamless, monoblock design in conjunction with impermeable rubber makes them the right choice for heavy rain or if you plan on being outside for several hours in average rainfall conditions. Of course, they’re well-equipped for lighter precipitation as well.

Hunter's flagship boots are a textbook Wellington rain boot. Made entirely of waterproof (not water-resistant) rubber from sole to shaft, no amount of rain, sludge, or slush penetrates these boots. The carved rubber soles keep their grip amidst slippery sidewalks and slick metal grates, while the height is tall enough to stand nice and dry through calf-deep water, but isn't so tall to be a physical or stylistic burden. And at just under $200, they're a reasonable price for the quality.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)

#6540. 13 Best Deodorant for Men 2026, According to Dermatologists

Frankly, the best deodorant for men sells itself. It stinks to stink: Body odor is right up there with bad breath as a totally avoidable misfortune. But while deodorant might seem like an odd category to get overly psyched about, as a longtime grooming editor, I can confidently say we're living in a golden age of the underarm essential.

Unlike the synthetic and chalky ghosts of deodorant sticks past, today’s best deodorants are a a sea of sophisticated formulas, improved textures, better scents, and naturally derived ingredients that, yes, actually work. The only hard part is honing in on the best one for you. Price, feel, skin sensitivity, and your sweat and odor levels matter. So I tested a whole lot of deodorants to narrow the field to seven standouts, some new, some tried-and-true, plus a few more deeper cuts I also loved.

I also tapped trusted dermatologists, for some expert insights further down. Just one last quick note: We’re not talking antiperspirants here, which primarily rely on aluminum compounds to reduce sweat. If that’s what you’re after, I’ve got recommendations for those, too.

Now, are there more targeted, more sophisticated, and, well, chicer deodorants on this list? Absolutely. But at the end of the day, you want your deo to do three things: keep the stink at bay, smell good, and be easy to repurchase. Dove Men+Care hits every mark, which is why it's in every drugstore.

The price, of course, is great, but then there's the scent: a surprisingly sophisticated blend of bright bergamot and warm white amber that's fresh, subtly citrusy, and softly warm. It smells so much better than your typical underarm fare and is actually a little mood-boosting, as the packaging suggests. The aluminum-free, baking-soda-free formula features moisture-attracting ingredients, giving it a little skincare credibility, too. It also feels super smooth going on, and kept its scent for most of the day. No notes!

Hip grooming brand Church California's deodorant only launched two years ago, but for me, it's everything a niche, forward-thinking deodorant should be: Effective, made with almost entirely naturally derived ingredients, and housed in compostable, plastic-free packaging. The formula uses magnesium and organic tapioca starch for odor and moisture control, with coconut oil and shea butter adding a little skin-conditioning goodness. And that scent? Wonderfully herbaceous, thanks to an essential oil blend that smells like the actual outdoors.

Admittedly, the sturdy cardboard tube takes a little getting used to—you push the stick up from the bottom instead of twisting a dial. And the glide is certainly a little less smooth. But oh, does the formula hold up. Even hours later, I'd catch little whiffs of those lovely botanicals, and when the scent eventually faded, there was still no detectable B.O. For the environmentally conscious guy, it's a no-brainer, and for the aesthete, it looks retro cool sitting on your medicine cabinet shelf.

Ursa Major’s Hoppin’ Fresh stick is a modern legend in the deo space. Among us grooming geeks, this au naturel-meets-elevated formula changed the underarm game when it launched in 2015. It was one of those early artisanal step-ups from drugstore fare, and it earned the hype with thoughtfully curated, foraged-sounding ingredients, a subtle, natural-smelling citrus scent, and a skincare-centric stick that glides on smoothly.

It still holds up today, fighting odor, soothing skin, and absorbing excess moisture to keep you smelling fresh. For an aluminum-free deo, it also keeps me pretty dry, too. And even the packaging—a sleek, solid-blue tube—feels like a style upgrade. Scent wise, in winter Forest Fix is the way to go. But the original Hoppin' Fresh is always waiting in the wings once the warmer months come around.

I'm well aware that Old Spice has plenty of newer, more fragrance-centric variations, many with names like Swagger. But I've always preferred the tried-and-true stuff, and that blue stick, here in High Endurance Refresh, is about as iconic as deodorant gets. It's my old reliable, and one I always keep as a backup, whether I'm visiting family, staying overnight somewhere, or just need something I can count on in a pinch. This classic has aged remarkably well, because it simply works.

Then there's the nostalgia, which hits me every time I catch a whiff of that distinctively fresh-meets-medicinal aroma I've loved unironically for years. The formula isn't particularly fancy: science-y ingredients give the aluminum-free stick its smooth glide, while glycerin adds a little skin-conditioning benefit. Would I wear this for an Ironman competition? Probably not. But for everyday errands, workdays, and just about everything in between? Absolutely. At under $15 for a three-pack, it's also easily one of the best value options on this list.

When you think gel deodorant, you probably picture those old-school perforated dispensers with tons of holes. But Baxter of California's is a smooth, transparent gel stick that's still, in my book, among the best. Part of the appeal is the formula, which packs some impressive botanicals: witch hazel and tea tree oil help tackle odor-causing bacteria, while chamomile helps soothe skin. Add tried-and-true glycerin to the mix, and you've got a gel deodorant with some serious skincare cred, too.

Then there's the application itself. The clear gel glides on effortlessly, with a cool, crisp, almost medicinal feel that's especially refreshing during the warmer months. Better still, it won't leave white marks on your shirts, something I always appreciate with my go-to black T-shirts. Is it the most heavy-duty odor fighter on this list? Not quite. But for everyday wear, it keeps the funk at bay without feeling like you've applied much of anything.

I test a lot of deodorants, but few become permanent fixtures in my bathroom. So it's worth telling you that Freaks of Nature's Everyday Endurance has become my primary deodorant lately. The brand may be known best for sunscreen, but it knows its way around our underarms too. And much like its sunscreens, part of the appeal is the application. The lotion-like formula emerges through a clever F-shaped grate, and there's something oddly satisfying about watching it dispense before smoothing it onto your underarms.

Better still, it absorbs well without any gunky, sticky residue. Cocoa butter and sunflower oil give it a skin-conditioning quality that makes this one of the most soothing deodorants I've ever worn. It's premium-priced, sure, but between the standout application, exemplary performance, and skin-conditioning feel, it's worth every penny.

Gone are the days when deodorant had to smell like “Sports Ice Rush.” Today's best odor fighters offer sophisticated, subtle scents (so they don't compete with your cologne), and Salt & Stone remains one of the top examples, with deodorants that have actual scent pyramids—Saffron & Cedar, Black Rose & Oud, Bergamot & Hinoki, for example. All are nuanced, refined, and never too loud.

But it's not just about the scent. The formula neutralizes odor and absorbs excess moisture very well, while skincare-centric additions like niacinamide and shea butter help keep underarms comfortable. More importantly, in my experience, the fragrance doesn't devolve into some funky olfactory murk by day's end. You still smell like the scent you applied that morning, or at the very least, fresh. Are these the heaviest-duty or most affordable deodorants out there? Nope. But they deliver on fragrance and function, and that's a combination worth investing in.

Caldera Lab just launched its first deodorant, and given the brand's skincare chops, it's no surprise the formula reads like a serum: niacinamide, aloe vera, and hyaluronic acid soothe and hydrate; postbiotics and magnesium tackle odor; and arrowroot absorbs moisture. Stacked, indeed.

Can Aesop do any wrong? Not really, and its deodorant spray is another example of the botanical-meets-aromatic goodness the amber-hued brand does so well. It's the only spray on this list, and while its odor protection holds up well, a midday refresh is sometimes warranted—and honestly, part of the pleasure.

If your skin is extra sensitive, Nécessaire's entire fragrance-free lineup is exemplary, and its deodorant stick is no exception. It keeps odor impressively in check, is easy on sensitive underarms, and won't compete with your favorite cologne. It's also aluminum- and baking soda-free, dermatologist-tested, hypoallergenic, and even carries the National Eczema Association's Seal of Acceptance.

Cremo may be kings of shave cream, but it knows a thing or two about deodorant, too. The formulas are affordable, the odor protection holds up, and the scents smell so damn good. Take Palo Santo, with its warm, woody, slightly smoky aroma that feels far more sophisticated than its price suggests. Coconut oil and shea butter add the skin-conditioning touch you'd expect from a pricier stick. rant quietly in the background, look elsewhere—but if you like a lot of fragrance with your funk-fighting, Cremo delivers serious bang for the buck.

The Grey's culty, rightfully hyped deodorant is the best old-school-style roll-on I've ever tried. The unscented formula is so lightweight you almost have to check you're applying anything, but oh, you are, and there's a cooling tingle as it goes on. It's the most expensive deodorant on this list, but with its premium slim aluminum casing and impressively lightweight formula, it's an investment in barely-there odor-fighting care.

Malin+Goetz's Eucalyptus Deodorant is a stone-cold classic that helped elevate gel sticks and make them feel refreshingly natural. The scent is practically a trademark at this point, equal parts medicinal, herbal, and oh-so-subtle. But there's substance behind it: eucalyptus extract helps refresh skin, while odor-neutralizing citronellyl methylcrotonate keeps the funk at bay.

As a grooming editor, I've tried more sticks, sprays, gels, and roll-ons than I can count, from drugstore staples to niche newcomers. And while plenty have impressed me on first swipe, the real test is whether I keep reaching for them weeks, months, or even years later.

Here, I drew on firsthand testing, focusing on odor protection, scent, texture, application, skin comfort, and, of course, how well they hold up. I've worn these deodorants through warm and cool weather, long days, and everything in between. Some are favorites I've relied on for years, while others are newer that are now part of my medicine cabinet rotation. As I've said, the best deodorant sometimes depends on the occasion or even the season, so it's a good idea to have a few on hand if possible. At the end of the day, a deodorant needs to keep B.O. at bay, ideally absorb excess moisture, and feel comfortable. Some excel in certain areas more than others, but each one above warrants its spot among the best deodorants.

Here is some dermatologist advice on deodorants—the best ingredients to look for, plus some controversial ones, too—with insights from board-certified dermatologists Kiran Mian, Ryan B. Turner, and Heather Rogers.

The primary cause of body odor is “bad” bacteria pairing up with sweat. There are two ways to prevent the stink: Reduce perspiration in the area, and/or create an inhospitable environment for the bacteria.

These are the most common ingredients to seek out if you want to temper the proliferation of bacteria in your pits (and anywhere you’re particularly pungent).

This antimicrobial and anti-inflammatory mineral safely and effectively kills bacteria without irritating skin, says Mian.

Dr. Mian also endorses the non-irritating magnesium hydroxide, a naturally occurring compound that neutralizes bacteria without absorbing into the skin.

“The terpenes in tea tree oil make this ingredient naturally antibacterial and antifungal, helping to reduce body odor,” Turner says. “In its pure, concentrated form, it can be very irritating. It’s best to use a product that’s formulated with tea tree oil at low percentages. It’s still effective when diluted, and a little bit goes a very long way.”

This one is controversial to some. Most skin types will tolerate baking soda (sodium bicarbonate) just fine, but only trust reputable, well-reviewed brands who use it. “Baking soda has a very alkaline pH level, while that of the skin is more acidic,” explains Turner. “This disrupts the skin’s natural barrier, harming the good bacteria in our microbiome, and leaving our skin more susceptible to irritation and inflammation.” (We’ve included a few deodorants with baking soda below, plus many natural picks that explicitly exclude it.)

It’s easy to get these two confused, but they share a principle. “Prebiotics feed the good bacteria on our skin (bacteria that does not break down sweat releasing a foils odor), which then overpopulates those odor-producing bacteria,” explains Mian. “Probiotics are the good bacteria that will ideally populate the skin and overtake odor causing bacteria.” The names of these might be strange to you, like lactobacillus acidophilus or saccharomyces ferment. Chances are, a brand will proudly tout their pre- and probiotics, so look for that indication instead.

“These chemical exfoliating ingredients help resurface the skin, which also strips away bacteria as well as removes the dead skin that bacteria break down, which causes body odor,” says Turner. And Dr. Rogers echoes the love: “Using acids to exfoliate the skin in our armpits helps rebuild the acidic mantle [which is compromised with age], leading to better control of bacterial growth and odor. Many acids work great; glycolic, lactic acid and mandelic acid are my favorites. Salicylic acid, a beta hydroxy acid, has a higher chance of irritation.”

“Activated charcoal can help to absorb sweat and help to neutralize odors,” says Turner.

Arrowroot powder, tapioca starch, and cornstarch are popular ingredients in aluminum-free deodorants, helping absorb excess moisture and keep underarms feeling dry without blocking sweat production. Of tapioca starch, Mian says, “This non-irritating starch won’t clog sweat ducts like aluminum, which can cause breakouts.” (It's also terrific in anti-chafe products for similar moisture-absorbing benefits.) “Like tapioca starch, corn starch pulls moisture from the skin to leave a powdery feeling behind. Unless you have an allergy to these plants, they are safer to use,” says Turner. Both doctors particularly favor arrowroot powder as a naturally derived, generally well-tolerated ingredient for absorbing moisture.

Both help soak up surface moisture without blocking sweat production. Freaks of Nature's formula includes bentonite clay.

A naturally occurring, porous mineral powder that absorbs excess moisture and helps keep underarms dry. You'll find it in Ursa Major's deodorant.

Approach your deodorant purchase with the following considerations:

While it’s easy to celebrate a fully natural formula—and for good reason—keep in mind that not all natural ingredients are inherently good for your skin. Ingredients like baking soda and certain essential oils can cause irritation, particularly if your underarms are sensitive. Conversely, synthetic ingredients aren't automatically bad for your skin, either. “What’s more important is to consider the manufacturer of the products—are they reputable? Do they perform extensive testing? What are the ingredients used and from where are they sourced?” says Turner. That's sound advice whether you're shopping natural or synthetic. There's a reason dermatologists frequently recommend established brands like Dove: They have the resources to develop, test, and manufacture formulas at scale, often at a fraction of the price of more niche alternatives. Of course, that doesn't mean smaller brands aren't doing their homework, either. Finding the right deodorant still takes some trial and error, gents, but you can always start with our list.

If your underarms are easily irritated, start with the ingredient list. Baking soda, strong fragrances, essential oils, and certain acids can trigger irritation, though everyone's skin reacts differently. Look for skin-conditioning ingredients like shea butter, glycerin, and aloe, several of which you'll find in the deodorants featured above. Fragrance-free formulas are a smart starting point for sensitive skin, while unscented are worth considering, too—just know that some contain masking fragrances.

Deodorants come in all sorts of formats these days, from classic solid sticks and clear gels to sprays, roll-ons, lotions, and pastes. How they feel when you apply them matters as much as how well they work. Some glide on more smoothly, while others feel chalkier, thicker, or more moisturizing. Gels and roll-ons tend to feel lightweight and refreshing (great for warmer weather), while richer lotions and pastes feel more nourishing and suit the cooler months. Ultimately, the best deodorant feels comfortable, doesn't leave residue on your clothes, and fits your lifestyle.

Dr. Kiran Mian is a board-certified dermatologist at Hudson Dermatology and Laser Surgery in NYC, and volunteer faculty at the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai University.

Dr. Ryan B. Turner is a board-certified dermatologist and founder of Turner Dermatology in NYC and the TRNR Skin skin care line.

Dr. Heather Rogers is a board-certified dermatologist and co-founder of Modern Dermatology in Seattle and the founder of the Doctor Rogers skin care line.

#6538. Bob Dylan Dressed Like Timothée Chalamet to Meet the Pope

Today, millions of Americans awoke to social media feeds awash with images of and commentary about a landmark event that seemed nigh-impossible a mere 24 hours earlier. I speak, of course, of this image:

As the nation began to digest the results of a historic election that seemed to herald the arrival of a new America, two of our very best Americans met for a private audience on the other side of the Atlantic: Bob Dylan, the Great American artist, and Pope Leo XIV, the first American Pope. Dylan is in the midst of yet another lengthy European tour—his strongest in some number of years, judging by recent bootleg tapes. On November 2, he played a show at Milan’s Unipol Dome, then jetted over to Rome ahead of a November 5 performance at the ​​Palazzo dello Sport. What better way to kill an off-day than a quick chat with the Vicar of Christ?

It wasn’t the first time Bob met a Pope, as some pointed out. In 1997, he performed for John Paul II at the World Eucharistic Congress in Bologna, donning a dapper Western suit and a fine Stetson for the occasion. He and JP2 made for a bit of an odd couple, the mysterious American gunslinger and the seemingly-nonplussed Polish holy man.

Dylan has somewhat more in common with Pope Leo, a.k.a Robert Francis Prevost, a fellow Midwesterner. But even if the two men hail from similar geography, the sight of them together under the bright fluorescent lights of an anonymous Vatican office looked like the product of a well-crafted AI prompt, if not an outright fever dream. The images were so unexpected, so uncanny, I would’ve been convinced they were fake had they not been published by an official press organ of the Vatican.

Much of the whiplash stemmed from the two men’s appearances. Angel and devil. Spy versus spy. The man in the long black coat and the man in the short white mozzetta. The one liners wrote themselves.

On Wednesday, the Pope wore a clean white cassock and a tidy little zucchetto, with an iron cross hanging down around his neck. In short, he looked like the Pope. I suppose I would say the same about Bob: that he looked like himself. As any good Dylan fan knows, however, the meaning of that phrase has changed radically over the years. Bob’s shifting appearance is almost as notorious as his shifting artistic sensibility. Dust bowl refugee, sharp-suited mod, bohemian poltergeist, whatever this is—the man has worn many hats.

A hoodied Timothée Chalamet, seen with Kylie Jenner, in New York City.

Sometime in the early 1990s, Dylan took a liking to the humble hoodie, a stylistic evolution that intersected neatly with his booze-fueled artistic nadir. The hoodie allowed him to hide in plain sight, even as he continued to take the stage around the world on his Never Ending Tour. It was practical, yes, but it was also a defense mechanism, a way to synthesize his compulsion to perform and his aversion to being seen. That it often looked hard as hell did not hurt. (It should come as little surprise that Timothée Chalamet, who may as well have been born wearing a hoodie, seemed to riff on this era when he was channeling Bob all those months ago.)

In the 21st century, Bob came unstuck from time. On landmark records like “Love And Theft” and Modern Times, Dylan burrowed into an imaginary musical past situated somewhere between the Civil War and the Great Depression. His style shifted to match. In the early aughts, he adopted a full country-western guise, complete with a Lee Van Cleef mustache. In the years that followed, his sartorial inspirations shifted east: a little less cowboy, a little more riverboat gambler, often performing in wide-brimmed hats, long frock coats, and string ties. “Rock and roll Mark Twain” wouldn’t have been an inaccurate descriptor.

Which brings us back to today’s meeting with Pope Leo. On first glance, Dylan’s ensemble is bonkers: western black blouson with contrast stitching along the chest and collar; beneath that, a heavyweight black hoodie zipped to the chest; beneath that, a white collared shirt with several buttons undone, collar loosely drawn together with a polka-dot cravat. The hood is decidedly up, stationed atop a black flat brim cap. In some shots, Bob can even be seen sporting leather gloves and an old-fashioned umbrella, which seems to double as a cane.

Part “Man in Black” sobriety, part rock star debauchery, with a dash of the hypebeast flavor from Timmy’s A Complete Unknown press tour—the man got a damn fit off in Vatican City. There’s no other way to describe it. (It’s a shame Pope Leo left his Rhude jacket back at the Papal Palace.)

Still, it’s a look with roots in Bob’s past, or pasts: the hoodies of the ’90s, the Westernwear of the 2000s, even the polka dots of 1965. No one but Bob would ever dare to pair all these disparate elements, particularly for a private audience with the Bishop of Rome. No one but Bob could.

Someone should come up with a word for that.
