#6536. Matt Damon on the Oscars, Tarantino, and the Roles that Got Away

In the desert, as the song goes, you can remember your name. So it’s fitting that Matt Damon has come here—to a sweltering stretch of inland California near Lancaster—to reflect on his 30-odd-year career as one of the most familiar and bankable faces in Hollywood, and specifically on a year that will go down as one of that career’s most challenging and rewarding passages.

Damon set 2026 off with The Rip, a throwback pulp thriller that reteamed him with his lifetime BFF Ben Affleck. He then went on to dominate the summer to a degree most actors crossing the other side of 55 wouldn’t dream of, with Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey—a noisy, thrilling, engrossing feat of big-budget moviemaking that wouldn’t have worked without an Odysseus compelling enough to command audiences to take the 20-year journey with him. The role demanded everything in Damon’s toolbox, and then some, but the result was a cultural and commercial run that only Spider-Man himself could top.

Jacket (sold as suit) by Norton & Sons. Shirt by Dolce & Gabbana. Pants by Celine. Cummerbund by Charvet. Pocket square by Budd Shirtmakers. Boots by Tom Ford.

This is rare air, and Damon knows it. Lightning struck once for him, with Good Will Hunting in 1997—he never thought he’d see that happen again, much less 29 years later. If exhaustion is the price of beating those odds, then so be it. Still readjusting to life on dry land, Damon sat down to unpack the most unlikely filmmaking experience of his career—and begin working out where he might want to go next.

This interview has been condensed and edited.

Matt Damon: That is amazing coming from him. Yes, I did hear about it.

Yeah, I think if you talk to any actor my age, any of the usual suspects, they’d probably say the same thing. I think the more you do it, if you’re working with really good people, you’re always improving, you’re getting better. And then you’re living more life and you have your family dynamics, all of your primary relationships kind of fill up your reservoir. I’ve talked to other actors my age about it, Ben included—I think there’s a tendency to push when you’re younger, a little bit, to try to work yourself up, and the older you get, the more available a lot of this stuff is. These very, very, very big emotions are kind of just sitting [near the surface]. And so you can really relax into the work, and it can be very full without being forced.

I think so, yeah. I mean, I’ve never been a part of a movie like The Odyssey that was…. Well, I guess Oppenheimer, to an extent, was somewhat of a phenomenon as well.

My workload was a lot heavier on this one, and everybody’s was, and that was kind of the joy of it. We knew we were the first ones to ever try to do a full film in IMAX [70 mm], and that comes with a whole host of challenges for every single department. We were all, I think, very patient with each other because we knew what was being asked of the other people. So in terms of the acting department—the IMAX load is two and a half minutes or three minutes. And so these big, heavy emotional scenes—you couldn’t play them all the way through. You’d have to cut them up. And the crew was just awesome, very patient with us. It felt like we were explorers, in a way. We got to be the first ones to do it, and that really bonded us.

I never would have expected it. And also, to play a role that’s so physical and, at my age, to even have a role that’s available on a scale like this. I’d always wanted to make a David Lean movie. To have it come at this point in my life, I felt incredibly lucky, not only to be able to do it, but also to be able to contextualize it. I don’t think I would’ve appreciated it as much 20 years ago.

No, I just don’t think I would’ve been able to understand and to hold as much gratitude as I am and as I did throughout the process. It’s all about how you look at things. And in retrospect, yeah, we were probably cold and wet. There was a lot of physical discomfort that was temporary. But because I felt so grateful to be there, I was just so excited. I was just so joyful the whole time that I didn’t really notice any of that.

[Laughs] There are no sure things, man. I think that’s where you get into trouble, being like, Well, this is a surefire hit. I’ve heard that before.

I felt it when I saw it. There is that thing that can happen where you make a movie, and you feel like it’s really good, and it just doesn’t connect, and there’s just nothing you can do about that. But as the audience reactions started to come in and the critical reactions were great as well, it was like, Okay….

Chris [Nolan], the most nervous he is in the entire process, from the very beginning to the very end, is the eve of the film coming out. He’s genuinely nervous. He talks openly about it, because that is the moment that you put it out into the world and you have to live with the reaction. Ultimately, the audience decides. Once it opened and the responses were what they were, I think I felt like, We’re going to be okay. It was a big bet in the sense that it was a big budget. I’m not used to playing in that arena. The numbers that movies have to get to just to break even at that level are just so beyond the movies that I’m known for.

A few of them. But The Martian, which I think is the biggest movie that I’ve ever headlined—I think The Odyssey made three times what that made. And that was a real hit for me. But there’s this other league of these kinds of movies that are in the billions that I’ve just never….

Well, the thing about a role like that is every single day I was paired with an unbelievable scene partner. One day I’d be working with Annie [Hathaway], and then the next day I’d be working with Himesh [Patel]. It’s following this character through all these different stages of his life and people who are close to him. So every day was a different, exciting little challenge, a little puzzle to figure out. I’ve always said that when you’re working with a great actor, they’re so good that they’re good enough for both of you. And it’s almost like this wave comes and you just stand up on your board and ride it. Then you have Samantha Morton, who literally just picks you up and shoulder-carries you.

It’s very hard to come in and do really intense things in a very short time frame. [Morton’s scenes]—that was under a week of shooting. Each day being incredibly intense. And that was what was so incredible about these actors who would come in. They’re under a lot of pressure. Nobody says that, but we all know the deal. These are critical scenes, and everybody just came in and killed it. It just got more and more exciting as we were going.

I agree. She’s incredible. She’s always been incredible. We had a near-miss working together in 2003—and I got to know her then—in a movie with Heath Ledger. I just think the world of her as an actress, and I was excited when we did the scene. I was like, This one’s going to really work. And I hope—it doesn’t always happen right away, but hopefully it’s lodged her back in the minds of people who are making those decisions, because she’s just extraordinary.

That’s the emotional spine of the movie. A lot of that monologue that I have at the end is in voiceover, but I wrote it all out and I had it on my phone and I would just carry it with me off set. I would just revisit it and run through it a few times, and I was doing that for months and months. Firstly, I thought it was just so beautifully written, but also because I knew that scene had to land. If that lands, then the movie works.

And that also means setting them up as a young couple before he leaves. There’s a lot of engineering that goes into getting to a scene like that and then you have to execute it. I spent an inordinate amount of time thinking about that one.

Well, at that point, the audience has been on the journey with you, and it’s been a hell of a journey, so you don’t have to tell them that. We were tired. That scene I shot with Samantha, oddly, we had come from Iceland, but we were shooting nights, and we came to Scotland, and I had it happen two or three times on this movie where I just didn’t sleep. I was tired and I went to bed, and I’m sure everyone’s had that feeling where you’ve got to get up the next day and you have something that you have to do and you can’t fall asleep and you’re kicking the blanket around and you’re staring at the ceiling. By the time I got to work to do the scene with Sam, I hadn’t slept in 24 hours, and by the end of the day it was 36 or whatever. I remember thinking the great thing about this character is he probably didn’t sleep last night. Don’t worry about it. So the weariness was very real for a lot of the movie.

Coat and pants by Dries Van Noten. Shirt by Dior. Cummerbund (throughout) by Charvet.

It’s all in there because it’s what I’m actually feeling. As I was saying before, it’s very easy to relax into what’s real and available and to have access to it.

This was the biggest one that we’ve ever done, and we’ve always had a two-week rule. I think the first stretch [on The Odyssey], I was away for three and a half weeks. The first half of the movie was the hardest because the kids were still in school. And then once the summer hit, they could come to set and we could be together. But that first stretch—luckily they’re huge Chris Nolan fans and they knew that it was this incredible role, so they were really supportive, and it was a sacrifice for them, and they were awesome about it.

He’s turned down a lot of work. And in fact, he really wanted to take on the CEO role at [Damon and Affleck’s production company] Artists Equity because it would keep him home. So when he directs—for instance, Animals, that was all shot in LA. He said to me when we started the company—he’s got joint custody, which means he gets to be with the kids half the time. And he said, “If I go to Estonia and do a movie, it’s not like I get that time back.” So that for him means he’s got to stay in LA.

I felt that way when I did Air with Ben. It was such a joyful experience, and we had so much fun, and we really loved the movie we made. We had this discussion about how I don’t want to do [a project] unless it’s joyful and unless it’s really right for my life. So much of being a young actor is you’re afraid it’s going to go away. You’re very much still in touch with that feeling of being unemployed. And so when Ben and I got famous, we both really worked. Buried ourselves in work. Partly because people are suddenly offering you things and you can’t imagine turning anything down, right? It just seems counter to everything you’ve been trying so hard to do for your entire life.

The first time was before The Bourne Identity came out. I had done The Legend of Bagger Vance with Will [Smith] and Charlize [Theron], and that movie didn’t do well. And Will was, as he is now, the biggest star. And so it’s like, it’s not Will’s fault. [Laughs] And then I did All the Pretty Horses with Billy Bob [Thornton], and that was a whole fraught experience. There were different ideas between the studio and between Billy Bob about what the movie should be, and so it ended up getting cut in a way that wasn’t to Billy’s liking and it didn’t do well. And so I had had these misses. And so there was this stretch where the phone just didn’t ring. The silence was…. It was a real rose-colored lenses coming off.

Well, the other thing was that The Bourne Identity was delayed something like a year. And so the word was, That’s going to be a stinker. But in that time, I went right from [filming] Bourne to Ocean’s Eleven. Then there was this whole period of time where nobody was calling, except for Steven Soderbergh, who called and offered me the movie The Informant! And I was so confused. I asked him, “Are you asking me to write it?” Because I couldn’t imagine he was asking me to be in it, because nobody else was calling. He goes, “What? No, Scott Burns is writing it. I want you to be the lead.” And I’ve never forgotten that. He just paid no attention to what everyone else was saying. But anyway, leaving that aside, nobody else was calling. And then The Bourne Identity came out, and it was a big hit.

It’s not personal. It’s business. And people can’t really go out of their way to help you. It’s really a pretty cold calculus that determines whether or not you’re on the list or off the list. And you never know who’s on the list. And the list can be different for different studios, who they’ll greenlight a movie with.

No, no. There’s very, very frank conversations between agents and studio people going like, “I can’t greenlight the movie with X, but I can with Y.” And that list is always changing, and I know that sometimes I’m on it and sometimes I’m not. I just have to remember it’s not personal, but you can feel it if you’re on the list or not.

Yeah. And [the] quality of projects [you’re offered]. I’m sure I’ve worked my way off that list a number of times. I’ve had some movies that didn’t work, and then you got to pay attention. I remember 2012 to 2014-ish, maybe, Patrick [Whitesell], my agent for 35 years, said I was taking a lot of supporting roles—because again, I’m not strategizing at all. I’m just like, Oh, True Grit. I want to work with the Coen brothers. Great.

Yup, George [Clooney directing], great. So there was this string of movies like that. Patrick called me and he said, “You’ll become a supporting actor. We got to think about this next one.” And then what happened to come along was The Martian. But that’s about as much strategizing as we’ve ever done.

Interstellar was in there too. I’m so happy that I’m in that movie. I love the role, even though it’s a tiny part. When Chris offered me [the role], we hadn’t worked together, and [when] he offered me the part in Interstellar, he said, “Do you know that saying ‘There are no small parts, there are only small actors’ ? This is a small part.” But I think he undersold it, because it’s a really kind of pivotal role, and I had a ball doing it.

Yeah, I think I was on a Bourne movie or about to go do Green Zone with Paul Greengrass. That and Milk. I was supposed to play the Dan White part. And I was really excited about it. I’d spoken to Sean [Penn], and I’d started the research. I’d been watching the documentaries about him. Then Sean’s schedule changed and the movie had to push three months, which meant that it was going to conflict with the movie I was going to do with Paul. So I had to bow out, and I just loved that script so much. And I love Gus [Van Sant], obviously. And so that was a bummer. But having said that, they got Josh Brolin, and Josh is absolutely brilliant in the movie. That helped a lot, I think, with this feeling that I have, that I do believe that the right actor gets the part.

I never like to say that, because I don’t want to live with the regret, but I can tell you that I passed on Ted, and I’ve never had this experience where I laughed so hard reading a script. I literally took it to dinner and was reading parts of it out to my friends at dinner. And then I just got too clever by half. I don’t know what I was thinking. I was like, Well, maybe the teddy bear’s actually the best part.…

So I overthought it and I talked myself out of it. And because it shot in Boston, it would’ve been a chance for me to be home—my father’s passed away now, and I look back and I go, Man, that would’ve been six months back [home].

But, again, the right actor got the part. Mark [Wahlberg’s] great, and the movie’s great, and I wouldn’t have made it any better.

They’re really hard to do. And I don’t read a lot [of them]. I don’t think people think of me for comedies a lot. But I love them, and I love watching them, and I love doing them if I can get my hands on one. But they’re few and far between. I think because they don’t travel well. They’re harder to get made because you really have to get all your money back in America. Comedy is so cultural. So the margin for error, I think, is smaller, which is what’s so great when you see a great one.

Anthony [Minghella], I think, was the first, with Talented Mr. Ripley. And he talked openly about it. A lot of these directors do. They want to use whatever it is that you have to offer in a new way and in a fun way and in an unexpected way. Some of those roles are the best roles that I’ve ever had a chance to play. Really interesting, complex people, and I love that.

I shot a scene in a movie theater with [Jack Nicholson], and Jack was working with an earwig. It was just the three of us before the crew came in, and we were just rehearsing. Marty [Scorsese] goes, “Let’s run this and see how we think it’s going to go.” And Jack took the earpiece out and we rehearsed the scene a few times and Jack was letter-perfect with his dialogue. And so Marty went to get the crew, and as people were coming in I said, “Hey, man, what’s the deal with the earwig? You obviously know all your lines—you’re writing stuff.” And it was really interesting because, he goes, “The older you get, the harder it is to remember your lines.” And that feeling of going up on a line, that panic, that surge of adrenaline that you get—he goes, “It just makes me so uncomfortable.” It’s a fail-safe. And if he knows [the earpiece is] there, his body stays in that relaxed state. People might think it was laziness. It was the opposite. He was a grinder. He worked really, really hard. It was more about just protecting himself.

No, no, no, no, no. I think everybody was really focused on Marty. And I remember I was shooting second unit for The Bourne Ultimatum during that Oscars, so I didn’t go. I was living in Miami at the time, and I had an infant. [My wife] Lucy and I were laying in bed watching it and so nervous for Marty. And then when he won, I was jumping up and down in the bedroom and I remember being really excited. And then the next category was best picture, and The Departed won. And I realized in that moment I hadn’t even considered that that could happen. And I turned to Lucy and I was like, “Shit, we should be there!” And then the next few weeks, I got all these calls from all the cast and the studio, like, “Hey, where were you, man?” I totally blew it. And then the next time that that happened was on Oppenheimer, and we had this whole scary thing happen where my wife had a brain aneurysm.

She’s totally fine, thank God, but [she] had this brain surgery the week before the Oscars. But it was the first night she felt good enough to get up and had her energy back. And she surprised me and got a dress and we went out to the after-party, because she remembered that the last time it happened, I didn’t do it. And she was like, “This time we’re going out.” And it was pretty fucking heroic of her to do that and really meaningful.

It’s not something you can control. You got to just do the work, and that’s got to be the reward. Because it’s like, then if you don’t win, do you feel bad about something that was really positive? Don’t turn this into something bad. It’s a tricky one. I always think about old movies when I look at them and it says, “So-and-so won an Oscar, so-and-so didn’t.” You’re judging it in a totally different way. It’s not attached to that anymore. I think the real way they should do them, really, is 20 or 25 years later. You’ll have a really good idea of which movies really endured and really still matter to you. There can be hype in the moment, or it can be a zeitgeist thing, but it doesn’t hold up. The goal for me is to make as many of those movies that are around 20 years later. That’s a cool thing. People come up [to me] about Good Will Hunting and I realize they weren’t born when the movie came out. It’s just very cool for Ben and me to have a new generation of people actually see it.

I don’t know what that means. I live in Brooklyn. [Laughs] If the trail is in that area, yeah. I don’t know what that entails. I will say, the technical side of this movie—I think of someone like Willie Burton, our sound guy, who’s in his 80s and he is a legend in the business.

And I did not loop a line in this movie, which is incredible. And I did not loop a line in Oppenheimer, which is incredible. Willie and Doug [Shamburger], his boom operator, are so good. Every scene on the ship, Willie is down below in the hold mixing in storms. The fact that he salvages all of that dialogue is nothing short of amazing. The technical achievement of our heads of department and their teams were really special. And I just hope that people realize that, realize what it took to do what they did, because from a technical standpoint, I hope they all get their flowers.

I didn’t think I was going to work. And then the Daniels called me and they had this project that was so out there, and it was such a Daniels movie and it’s so different from anything I’ve ever been able to do that I couldn’t pass it up. They’re really good people, and this movie’s like a cosmic love bomb for the world. So it felt like the right place to put my energy, but we’re almost done shooting it, and yeah, I’m going to take a long time off.

I haven’t thought about it. There are a lot of great performances. Paul Newman had some great ones at the end. We have the studio now with Artists Equity, and I feel like we’re just going to do stuff we really want to do, because it’s a very time-consuming job, and if you’re not 100 percent in, it’s just not worth doing. You have to really let it take over your life. I think that was one of the joys of The Odyssey—it dominated our lives.

There was a funny thing that happened on The Odyssey. We were in Scotland. I was with my kids. They’d just come over and gotten out of school, and we were eating dinner in this tiny hotel in this tiny town. And so there was one little dining room, which had 10 little tables in it, and the crew had watched dailies and so they were shuffling in, and I was already eating with my kids—I had jeans and a flannel shirt and a baseball hat on. And Chris came in and he walked by the table and I said, “Hey, Chris.” And he turned and he goes, “Oh, hey, Matt. I didn’t recognize you with the beard.” Chris doesn’t say stupid things—it was definitely the stupidest thing he’s ever said, because we’d been next to each other for six months.

He had just walked out of dailies watching close-ups of me, I’m sure with this giant beard. And I looked at him and he immediately realized what he’d said and he just looked at his shoes and then he looked up, like, Can we just agree that this never happened? And he shuffled off, and my kids go, “Dad, does Chris Nolan not know who you are?” I was like, “No, guys, he’s just exhausted.” He was that tired. He hadn’t seen me out of wardrobe in a while, you know what I mean? We were so tired by the end of that movie.

Well, he should write us a movie. We would be very happy if he did that.

Who knows? I have no right to expect it, let’s put it that way. I don’t want to sound like a broken record, but I have such gratitude for the fact that this came along my path at this point. I have no right to expect anything like that to happen again. But in the meantime, I just want to make good stuff and definitely keep working with Ben, because we’re at that point in our life where we’re like, “Fuck it, why not?” This was our dream as kids, as boys. We got to honor those boys and keep working together if we can. ✦

#6534. Andy Cohen Reveals The Chaotic Meal He Enjoys With His Kids Every Sunday: 'This Is What Makes Us A Family'

Plus, the childhood snack combo he still swears by.

After enough New Year’s Eves throwing back shots with Anderson Cooper, Andy Cohen seems like someone you can trust on one very important topic: curing the dreaded next-day hangover.

“Gosh, just a cheeseburger,” Cohen tells me when I ask what food he swears by the morning after drinking. More specifically, he heads to Corner Bistro in New York City’s West Village for one.

Booze just so happens to be part of Cohen’s latest gig, as he’s teamed up with Svedka ahead of the 2027 Super Bowl.

After the brand’s AI Super Bowl ad drew heavy backlash last year, the vodka brand is challenging the public to create a commercial that’s even better. At stake is a $500,000 cash prize and the chance for the winning ad to air during the big game, with Cohen serving as one of the judges.

“I just think this contest is amazing, and I can’t wait to judge it,” he tells me. “This is really giving people a huge stage and permission to be creative surrounding a drink that I love.”

His taste in drinks may be his own, but burgers apparently run in the family—his son orders them during their weekly dinner ritual.

“We go out every Sunday night to the same restaurant in New York City, and [my daughter] has chicken noodle soup; he has a cheeseburger,” Cohen shares. “My daughter eats everything and has the best taste. She has pickles and broccoli with her soup—it’s the most disjointed meal. I have a turkey burger, but this is our process, and this is what makes us a family.”

Talking about what his kids eat also sent Cohen back to a few favorites from his own childhood. “Spaghettios and Doritos make a great combination,” he tells me, a pairing he grew up eating and still stands behind.

Then there’s Provel cheese, another longtime favorite. “[It’s a] big St. Louis thing; it’s kind of gross,” Cohen says.

Not exactly a ringing endorsement, but he still loves it anyway.

#6533. Darian Mensah Might Be the Richest Player in College Football. But He's Still Going to Class.

A decade ago, an interview with the quarterback of the unbeaten, top-ranked team in college football might have involved the player, the reporter, and the team’s sports information director. Maybe the player’s dad stood nearby, keeping guard. But when I talked to Darian Mensah on the Sunday before Halloween, there were five other people on the call, plus a few more sitting in the room with the Miami Hurricanes’ smooth but press-shy signal-caller.

Mensah is the most modern sort of college star, a two-time transfer now at his third school who has navigated a bevy of NIL deals. “I’ve got a great group of people who I think truly want to see me win,” he tells me.

The most notable among them is Malachi Toney, the sophomore receiver who caught 16 balls from Mensah the day before we spoke. By throwing tons of on-target passes, Mensah is comfortably pacing to set college football’s single-season record for completion percentage. As the Canes get ready to travel to South Bend to face No. 4 Notre Dame this weekend, the 21-year-old Heisman Trophy frontrunner is at 80.7 percent, several points clear of second place all-time.

At first glance, Mensah has a simple job: just throw the ball to Toney, who has unparalleled open-field maneuverability. In a 52-point outing against Pittsburgh last month, the two accounted for 194 yards and three way-too-easy scores. “I mean on the first touchdown, I gave a little signal to Mali,” Mensah recalls. “You can see there's only one guy over the pod of three guys.” The film confirms it: Pitt didn’t put enough defenders near Toney, and Mensah identified it. “You got to keep going to it,” he says.

Mensah loves talking football, though he’s just as happy to get in-depth about how chaotic his life is right now. Mensah is trying to deliver Miami back to its glory days as he manages a variety of commercial arrangements, prepares for his professional future, and, yes, even going to class, contrary to popular ideas about the NCAA football player experience. “I have to go to class,” he confesses. “Transferring [two] times kind of messed with my credits.” He has three classes per day, on Tuesdays and Thursdays, and has had to keep himself eligible at Tulane (which “set the standard for me”), Duke (“probably even a little bit harder”) and now Miami: “Balancing that, school and football, was tough. But I think it set me up for where I'm at now, where maybe it's not as rigorous as far as academics go.”

Mensah gets particularly excited to discuss fashion, which I bring up because most athletes who sit for GQ have at least a moderate enthusiasm for clothes. Mensah, for what it’s worth, is wearing a cropped Adidas T-shirt—the brand is one of his many partners, alongside Dr Pepper and Sling TV. Miami has a roughly $50 million roster, one of the five biggest budgets in the sport. And Mensah, as the team’s star quarterback, is probably its top earner. But I wondered, more specifically, how much money Mensah is set to earn this year between football and his various NIL agreements. “I don’t know,” he demures. “As much as God allows me to.”

Market rate for a Power 4 starting QB is in the low to mid-seven figures, and Mensah is almost certainly on the high end of that spectrum. I’m not interested in pocket-watching, but just a few weeks ago, the United States did pass a bill that would cap how much someone like Mensah can make. The “Protect College Sports Act,” a bill developed by Sen. Ted Cruz and his Democratic counterpart, Maria Cantwell, may come up for a House vote in the upcoming lame-duck session of Congress. (It passed the Senate with a bipartisan array of 77 votes.) The bill would cap school pay to athletes at about $50 million per university, essentially freezing the market right where Miami’s football team currently stands.

The bill’s backers, including seven-time National Champion Nick Saban, have argued that college football player pay is out of control and endangers the integrity of college sports writ large. Essentially, the bill is coming for Mensah’s money, more than practically anyone else among the hundreds of thousands of NCAA athletes. So how does that sit with him?

“I grew up in a single-parent household, and my mom providing for all my siblings was not easy,” he explains. “So to be able to take care of my family in a way that I wasn't able to before has only been a blessing. I don't really know what to say about that other than I'm blessed to be in the position I'm in.”

Mensah doesn’t preoccupy himself with the weight of Miami football's illustrious tradition. He knows the program was “like a dynasty and a powerhouse back then,” and he’s gotten to meet plenty of legends of The U. “Me and the guys always strive to follow in their footsteps,” he says. “But yeah, I was born in 2005...”

In his relatively short life, Mensah has packed in a lot. After playing high school ball in Santa Maria, California, he started his college career as a three-star true freshman at Tulane in 2024. His only other college offer, he told me, was from FCS Idaho State. He entered the transfer portal to play at Duke in 2025, then reentered after winning the ACC Championship last year. In addition to climbing the economic ladder, Mensah has lived in four cities over the past four years. He’s only 21, but he’s got the palette of a washed-up 32-year-old like me. Maple Street Café in New Orleans, which has a chicken lemon dish that is “some of the most amazing pasta I’ve ever had in my life,” is a standout.

Come 2027, there’s a good chance Mensah will make it five cities in five years. He’s eligible for the NFL draft, and should he declare, he’s expected to be taken early in the first round by one of the league’s many QB-needy teams. But I’m a college football guy at heart, so near the end of our call I ask Mensah what he thinks of the impending 24-team playoff format that’s likely to be instituted next season. "If everything goes to plan," he says, "it won't really concern me." Why not, I ask?

#6529. These Prosciutto di Parma and Grana Padano Recipes Deserve a Spot on Your Holiday Table

Preparing a memorable holiday meal doesn’t mean that things need to be complicated; simplicity can often be the best route to success. It’s easy to get hung up on overcomplicated techniques, expensive and hard-to-source ingredients, and combinations of flavors that sometimes require a leap of faith.When we cook for our friends and families, the dishes we return to time and again tend to have a few things in common: They make the most of what you have on hand, and they rely on the best ingredients possible. We’d much rather have a small amount of something exceptional than a large quantity of a substitute that just doesn’t measure up. Grana Padano PDO and Prosciutto di Parma PDO are two such ingredients, made according to generations-old traditions and held to strict production standards.Both are protected by the European Union’s Protected Designation of Origin (PDO), which guarantees that each is produced in its designated region of Italy using time-tested methods. Grana Padano is aged for at least nine months to develop its mellow, nutty flavor and granular texture, while Prosciutto di Parma is cured for a minimum of 400 days and made with just pork and sea salt. Their quality and versatility make them just as useful for everyday cooking as for a more celebratory meal.

For your next big gathering, these six recipes from our archive show just how much you can do with a few good ingredients.

Celery root adds an earthy sweetness to these mashed potatoes, which are whipped with butter, half-and-half, and Grana Padano until smooth and creamy. A generous amount of the aged Italian cheese gives the mash a salted, savory depth that makes it the perfect cozy side dish on an autumn table or as part of a holiday feast. Get the recipe >

Inspired by panisse, the famous Provençal chickpea fritters, these crisp polenta fries are enriched with butter and Grana Padano, then fried until golden and crunchy. The creamy roasted garlic dipping sauce gets a touch of heat from chipotle peppers and hot sauce, providing the perfect pairing for the rich, crispy polenta. Get the recipe >

Thinly sliced raw kale gets a bright, savory treatment in this simple salad. Grana Padano lends creamy depth, while fresh lemon juice and dried currants bring acidity and sweetness to balance the slightly bitter greens. Allowing the salad to rest briefly softens the kale and encourages the flavors to meld. Get the recipe >

Cloaked in a creamy buttermilk sauce brightened with lemon, this pasta gets a salty, savory note from thinly sliced Prosciutto di Parma folded in just before serving. Grana Padano lends a nutty finish, while poppy seeds cooked in butter bring an aromatic, toasty quality. Get the recipe >

Peas are gently simmered in a broth seasoned with olive oil, garlic, parsley, and small pieces of Prosciutto di Parma, allowing the ham to season the liquid as it cooks. A touch of sugar brings out the peas’ natural sweetness, while the prosciutto’s salty character balances the dish, making for a simple, deeply flavorful side. Get the recipe >

Garlicky sautéed escarole is layered with two kinds of Prosciutto di Parma: chopped pieces that cook with the greens, and thin strips that are fried until crisp, adding texture and crunch. Chile flakes and lemon juice round everything out, balancing the ham’s saltiness with heat and acidity. Get the recipe >

Head to DistinctlyDeliciouslyEuropean.eu to learn more about Grana Padano PDO and Prosciutto di Parma PDO.

#6521. Stuffed Pepper Soup

Stuffed peppers are one of the most versatile comfort foods. Literally—put anything in them, and they’ll taste great. The same goes for our stuffed pepper soup recipe. We take the classic stuffed pepper combination of ground beef, rice, tomatoes, and lots of cheese and turn those hearty flavors into an even more comforting soup that you can cozy up to all year long.

Grab a large pot and add in your favorite extra-virgin olive oil. Heat over medium heat, then add in your bell peppers and onion. Cook those down until soft while mixing occasionally—this should take around 7 minutes. Then, you can add the garlic and cook until fragrant, which should only take around 1 minute. Once everything is cooked down and fragrant, add the ground beef and season with salt and pepper. Season generously, but remember that you can always add more later; you can’t take it away. Once the beef is no longer pink, drain the fat and return the pot to the heat.

Once back on the heat, add the broth, crushed tomatoes, diced tomatoes, and your dried oregano. Bring the mixture to a boil, then reduce the heat to a simmer. Now it’s time to cook the rice. Add the rice and simmer while covered until the rice is tender. This should take around 40 minutes, but make sure to check on the rice every now and then. If you need a bit more liquid, add a little bit more water. Give it a taste, then season with salt and pepper as needed.

Once your soup is seasoned to your liking, garnish with shredded white cheddar (my personal favorite, but you do you) and some freshly chopped parsley for freshness.

The full list of ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

Want to switch up this soup? Start by thinking about your favorite type of stuffed pepper. Do you like yours with ground turkey, ground pork, or sausage? What about using tomato paste for a richer flavor? Tomatillo sauce could replace tomato sauce. White beans, black beans, or navy beans might be the starchy addition if you prefer that over rice. If you’re vegan or vegetarian, fill your soup with zucchini, mushrooms, kale, or other favorite vegetables.

Stuffed pepper soup will last for no more than 3 to 4 days in the fridge. Make sure to seal tightly in a container if there are leftovers or you're meal prepping. The soup will last up to a month in the freezer. Let the soup thaw in the fridge before slowly reheating.

#6515. The History of This Must-Try Cheese Goes Back Nearly 1,000 Years

An authentic wheel of Grana Padano carries a signature brand burned into its rind—so you know that if you see a diamond pattern and the cheese’s name, it must have been produced in the Valle Padana (Po River Valley) of Northern Italy according to strict rules with roots in a 12th-century Cistercian monastery. At the time, the region surrounding the city of Milan was primarily soggy marshland. The monks who built the monastery—Chiaravalle Abbey—were followers of the Rule of Saint Benedict, which, along with devotion to prayer, mandated that they practice agriculture with a focus on responsible, sustainable use of land. They first figured out how to turn the marshy landscape into arable fields so they could grow high-quality feed for dairy cattle. Since milk (and fresh cheese) didn’t last long, the next challenge for the monks was how to preserve this nutritious, valuable food source to nourish themselves and their community.

The method they developed—heating the milk in copper cauldrons, adding rennet (a coagulating enzyme), cutting the curds into tiny pieces, and molding, salting, and aging the cheese—remains largely unchanged today. By producing the first iteration of what is now known around the world as Grana Padano, the monks of Chiaravalle Abbey earned the distinction of operating the world’s first cheese dairy.

Easy to like, portable, and a good source of protein, the cheese became an emblematic food, enjoyed by Italians at all levels of society through the Renaissance and into the modern era. Its iconic name, meanwhile, developed over time. The monks called it “caseus vetus”—Latin for “old cheese”—but since the peasants didn’t know Latin, they referred to it as “grana” for its distinctive grainy texture. As production spread well beyond the monastery, each province had its own grana: Cheese made in Milan was “grana Milanese,” and “grana Mantovano” was from Mantua.

It wasn’t until 1954 that the Italian government codified the name Grana Padano to unite the different varieties of grana cheese under a single wider geographical indication and that the Grana Padano Protection Consortium was established. In 1996, Grana Padano was awarded the European Union’s Protected Designation of Origin (PDO) designation. Today the consortium incorporates 134 member dairies, who together produced over 6 million wheels of cheese in 2025. In addition to safeguarding the name, the organization is also responsible for the global promotion of Grana Padano PDO and governs every detail of production to ensure that each wheel meets the strict standards established under the PDO.

The specifications start with the cows’ diet: All fresh forage and at least 75 percent of dry fodder (such as hay) must be grown within the cheese production area. Raw milk must be collected within 24 hours of milking and be partially skimmed, allowing for natural separation of the cream. After a natural whey starter is introduced to the copper (or copper-lined) milk vat, calves’ rennet is added for coagulation. The cheese is then broken into rice-size curds using a spino, which resembles a large whisk, and packed into round molds, where it sits for at least 36 hours. The wheels are immersed in brine for anywhere from 14 days to a month and aged for at least nine months and up to 24 months or longer. Wheels aged over 20 months can be labeled Riserva.

Bite into a chunk of Grana Padano PDO, and you taste the sweetness of fresh milk coupled with a savory hint of toasted nuts. Riservas may be flakier and more crystalline in texture than younger versions of the cheese but never dry or crumbly, and they must still have a delicate flavor. Grana Padano PDO is an exceptionally versatile cheese, equally delicious on a cheese board, wrapped with a slice of Prosciutto di Parma PDO and drizzled with aged balsamic for a sophisticated appetizer, as a filling for ravioli, or grated over soup or pasta.

It’s good for you, too. Lactose- and gluten-free, Grana Padano PDO is low in fat and an excellent source of protein, calcium, and vitamin B12. A 30-gram piece (just over one ounce) has as much nutrition as a pint of milk, making it a beneficial as well as tasty solution to the “afternoon slump” for both kids and adults. Whoever you share Grana Padano PDO with, be sure to tell them what they have in common with Italians from more than 1,000 years ago—that they’re savoring a taste of European culinary history.

Head to DistinctlyDeliciouslyEuropean.eu to learn more about Prosciutto di Parma PDO and Grana Padano PDO.

Funded by the European Union. Views and opinions expressed are those of the author(s) only and do not necessarily reflect those of the European Union or of the granting authority. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

Bite into a chunk of Grana Padano PDO, and you taste the sweetness of fresh milk coupled with a savory hint of toasted nuts. Riservas may be flakier and more crystalline in texture than younger versions of the cheese but never dry or crumbly, and they must still have a delicate flavor. Grana Padano PDO is an exceptionally versatile cheese, equally delicious on a cheese board, wrapped with a slice of Prosciutto di Parma PDO and drizzled with aged balsamic for a sophisticated appetizer, as a filling for ravioli, or grated over soup or pasta.

It’s good for you, too. Lactose- and gluten-free, Grana Padano PDO is low in fat and an excellent source of protein, calcium, and vitamin B12. A 30-gram piece (just over one ounce) has as much nutrition as a pint of milk, making it a beneficial as well as tasty solution to the “afternoon slump” for both kids and adults. Whoever you share Grana Padano PDO with, be sure to tell them what they have in common with Italians from more than 1,000 years ago—that they’re savoring a taste of European culinary history.

Head to DistinctlyDeliciouslyEuropean.eu to learn more about Prosciutto di Parma PDO and Grana Padano PDO.

#6514. Azalea Cocktail

Recipe by Kate McKiernan, Chicago, Illinois

The Azalea cocktail is the signature bright pink, lemonade-and-vodka drink of the Masters Tournament, delivering spring and summer party refreshment.

The Azalea is the signature cocktail of the Masters Tournament, and as a hostess with the mostest, I love leaning into a theme. This drink is a hole-in-one crowd pleaser, whether you’re a golf fan or not. No one can deny the lure of a pretty-in-pink, citrusy vodka cocktail like this one, a perfect sipper on a warm, breezy day on or off the green.

Named for the stunning azalea blooms that famously line the grounds of the 13th hole at the Augusta National Golf Club, the Azalea cocktail is refreshing and perfectly sweet. It’s simple on the page with only a few ingredients, but the bright acid of the lemonade and the sweet kick of the grenadine go perfectly together and work well with a neutral spirit like vodka. It’s an easy cocktail to scale up for a crowd, making it perfect for Masters watch parties, spring brunches or any gatherings that could use a little extra pink charm.

Fill a chilled rocks glass with fresh ice.

Add ice, vodka and grenadine to an empty cocktail shaker. Fill the shaker with ice, and shake the cocktail until it’s well chilled, 15 to 20 seconds.

Editor’s Tip: For a more layered look, add the grenadine directly to the glass and only shake the vodka and lemonade, allowing the pink color to settle to the bottom.

Strain the cocktail into the prepared glass.

Garnish the cocktail with an orange slice and a cocktail cherry.

Editor’s Tip: You can also use the syrup from the cherries to stand in for grenadine in a pinch.

To make Azalea cocktails for a crowd, multiply the portions by the number of people you’re serving. Combine the vodka, lemonade and grenadine in a large pitcher or beverage dispenser ahead of time, then refrigerate the mixture until you’re ready to serve. If you don’t plan to shake each drink individually, you’ll want to add water to the mixture to compensate for the dilution you get from shaking a cocktail over ice. To achieve the proper dilution, add water equal to 20% to 25% the total volume of the base mixture.

Another way to gauge: Make a single serving of your cocktail. Measure the total volume of liquid, stir the mixture with ice until it’s chilled, and measure it again. The difference is the exact amount of water you need to add per serving in your batch.

Stir the mixture well before pouring it over ice, and wait to add garnishes until serving so everything feels fresh.

A rocks glass, aka a double old-fashioned, is the traditional glassware choice for an Azalea cocktail, giving it a classic, country-club feel while keeping things simple and elegant. If you want to dress things up for brunch or spring entertaining, a highball glass also works beautifully, offering extra room for ice and garnishes.

#6513. Brownie Batter Bark

Recipe by Molly Allen, Hood River, Oregon

This egg-free brownie batter bark is quick and easy, and tastes just like brownie batter straight from the bowl.

When I’m making brownies, I always want to swipe my finger against the edge of the bowl and taste the brownie batter. The problem? While it’s delicious, brownie batter contains raw eggs and raw flour, which can make you sick. When you want to lick the spatula but don’t want to risk food poisoning, this brownie batter bark recipe is the perfect solution.

Line a 15x10x1-inch baking sheet with parchment paper.

In a large bowl, combine almond flour, brown sugar, cocoa powder and salt. Stir in the melted coconut oil and vanilla extract until the mixture is smooth.

Spread the mixture on the prepared baking sheet into an even layer. Freeze it for 20 minutes.

Meanwhile, in a small microwave-safe bowl, microwave 1 cup of chocolate chips in 30-second increments until they’re melted, stirring between the intervals. Spread them over the frozen base. Sprinkle the bark with the remaining 1/2 cup of chocolate chips.

Freeze the bark for one hour or until it’s firm. Break it into pieces.

Brownie batter bark is best stored in a zip-top bag or airtight container in the freezer. The cold freezer temperature will keep the bark hard, and provides the best texture when you pull out a few pieces to enjoy. Keep the bark stored in the freezer for up to two months.

This is a great make-ahead potluck dessert. Make it any time, and then store it in the freezer. Grab a few pieces at a time, or serve a whole platter to a crowd.

No almond flour on hand? You can swap in all-purpose flour, but keep in mind the texture may not be the same. If you use all-purpose flour, it should be heat treated before consuming raw.

Spread the flour on a baking sheet and heat it in a 400ºF oven for about five minutes or until it reaches an internal temperature of 160º. Then add it to the dry ingredient mixture.

This recipe calls for unsweetened cocoa powder to deliver deep chocolate flavor. Semisweet chocolate chips are used for the melted chocolate topping for a balance of sweetness. It’s best to stick with semisweet chocolate for a more robust flavor (as opposed to milk chocolate).

You can melt chocolate either in the microwave or over a double-boiler on the stove top. Heat the chocolate slowly on low heat for the smoothest consistency.

In a large bowl, cream the butter and confectioners’ sugar until they’re blended. Beat in the vanilla and almond extracts until combined.

In another bowl, whisk together the all-purpose flour, baking powder and salt. Gradually beat the flour mixture into the creamed mixture just until no flour streaks remain. Fold in the dried cranberries and chopped pistachios.

Editor’s Tip: Use a hand mixer or stand mixer to make creaming the butter and sugar easier.

#6509. Condensed Milk Bread Rolls

Recipe by Kallee Krong-McCreery, Escondido, California

These milk bread rolls are enriched with condensed milk, which makes them fluffy and tender with a touch of sweetness.

Baking bread is one of my biggest culinary joys. It feels magical that a handful of pantry ingredients can create a supple dough, which then transforms into tender, browned bread just begging for a slathering of butter. Whether I’m making sourdough French bread or homemade dinner rolls, the process is mostly the same, but a few small ingredient changes can give a recipe a distinct identity. In these condensed milk bread rolls, that small change is a can of sweetened condensed milk.

This recipe was inspired by Brazilian milk buns (pão de leite condensado) and Portuguese sweet bread, which have sweetened condensed milk added to the dough for flavor and a sugary edge. Most of these recipes use a starter technique called tangzhong, where a cooked flour paste is kneaded into the dough to create a fluffy, cloud-like texture similar to that of Japanese milk bread. We simplified the process for our buns, skipping the starter so they come together without advanced planning.

In a small bowl, dissolve the yeast in warm water. In a large bowl, combine the milk, eggs, butter, sugar, vanilla, salt, yeast mixture and 1-1/2 cups of flour. Beat the mixture on medium speed for three minutes until it’s smooth. Stir in enough of the remaining flour to form a soft dough.

Turn the dough onto a floured surface and knead it until it’s smooth and elastic, six to eight minutes. Place the dough in a greased bowl, turning once to grease the top. Cover the bowl and let the dough rise in a warm place until it has doubled in size, about one hour.

Editor’s Tip: When kneading, start with a light dusting of flour, adding more only as needed. It’s normal for dough enriched with eggs and butter to be tacky initially (it eases as you knead and develop gluten), but adding too much flour will make the bread dry.

Punch down the dough. Turn it out onto a lightly floured surface, then divide and shape it into 12 balls. Place the dough balls in a greased 13×9-inch baking pan. Preheat the oven to 350°. Meanwhile, cover the pan with a kitchen towel and let the rolls rise in a warm place until they have almost doubled in size, 45 to 60 minutes longer.

Editor’s Tip: For evenly-sized rolls, divide the dough by weight using a kitchen scale. The best kitchen scales offer measurements in grams and ounces, so you can divvy up the dough easily no matter which system you prefer.

Bake the rolls until they’re lightly browned, 15 to 20 minutes. Cover the pan loosely with foil if the tops brown too quickly.

Since these rolls are yeast-based and require two rises, you have to make them at least a few hours ahead of time, but you can stretch that to the day before. Make the dough as directed, but cover and refrigerate them after shaping and placing them in the baking dish. The next day, let the rolls warm and rise at room temperature before baking them as directed.

You can freeze baked condensed milk bread rolls for up to three months in an airtight, freezer-proof food storage container. Thaw them in the refrigerator, then warm them in a low oven, around 300°, until they’re soft.

Dense or dry rolls are usually the result of underproofing, overbaking or adding too much flour. Proofing gives the yeast time to lift the heavy dough, weaving air into it for a light texture. If the dough doesn’t rise enough, the lack of air produces rolls with a tight, firm crumb. Use the time cues in this recipe as a guide—proofing may take longer in a chilly kitchen, or go faster on a hot day.

Overbaked rolls can be dry or hard because the extended oven time pulls out their moisture. Since ovens vary, set a timer and check them a few minutes early—even three to four extra minutes can make a big difference. They could also be dry if you knead in too much flour, or if you didn’t measure your flour correctly from the start. Extra flour will hydrate as it proofs, sucking up the liquid that would otherwise contribute to tender rolls.

You can tell yeast is active when a creamy, foamy layer forms on the water’s surface a few minutes after mixing it with warm water. It looks like frothy, milky coffee! Although you don’t always have to proof yeast depending on the type and recipe, proofing active dry yeast gives you visual confirmation that it’s viable. Sometimes the yeast can die off even before the expiration date; proofing checks for that. Once the dough is mixed, you’ll see active yeast at work as it starts to expand, rise and relax the dough.

These lightly sweetened rolls are versatile and can be served for breakfast or brunch with your favorite frittata, crispy bacon and a bowl of fruit salad, or with afternoon tea or dinner. The flavor of the condensed milk—especially as it caramelizes on the roll’s tops—would pair especially well with pork chop dinners, which often feature a sweet ingredient. I also want to try them as a twist on croissant ice cream sandwiches in place of the flaky French pastry.

This recipe doesn’t use a whole can of sweetened condensed milk, but don’t toss the rest. Put it to work in other sweetened condensed milk recipes, or try something new and shake up a Vietnamese coffee martini. Better yet, serve condensed milk rolls with the martini for a happy hour to remember.

Our editors and experts handpick every product we feature. We may earn a commission from your purchases.Learn more.

As a child of the ’90s and lover of all things Christmas, I never really warmed to the minimalist, beige Christmas aesthetic. The latest My Christmas House collection, for instance, is swoon-worthy. But my heart belongs to the colorful, often mismatched decor of Christmases past.

Choose from fun designs like this I Love The 90s cassette tape ornament or VHS tape. If you prefer a more classic look, opt for a red peppermint reflection ornament and  retro tree top.

Most of the larger buildings light up to really bring your village to life.

#6502. Ditch Your Fleet of Coffeemakers—The Chefman Coffee Station Pro Handles Everything

Can't decide between a drip coffee maker, Nespresso, Keurig or an espresso machine? The Chefman Coffee Station Pro does all four—and takes up very little room.

Our editors and experts handpick every product we feature. We may earn a commission from your purchases.Learn more.

As Kevin Malone famously said in Season 8, Episode 2 of The Office, “Why waste time say lot word when few word do trick?” In my opinion, the same concept should apply to countertop appliances. After ditching my microwave, countertop oven, air fryer and toaster for the all-encompassing Panasonic HomeChef, I began to wonder what else I could condense into a singular appliance.

After musing over my fleet of five(!!!) coffeemakers consisting of Keurig and Nespresso machines, an automatic espresso maker, a manual espresso maker and a classic drip coffeemaker, I realized that it was time to address the barista elephant in the room. I had to reclaim my coffee bar counter space—but how? The answer, it turns out, is the Chefman Coffee Station Pro.

Here’s how I tested it over several months and why it’s become my go-to coffee machine.

Can't decide on a brew method? The Chefman Coffee Station Pro tackles them all. Yes, really.

In the age of the all-in-one appliance, maximizing countertop space just makes sense. In small kitchens especially, cramming half a dozen different-yet-similar appliances into limited space feels like such a waste. And sure, you don’t need both a blender and a food processor, but it’s nice to have the choice between the two. That’s why combo machines like the Ninja CrushBoss came out and started changing the game. That’s also the idea behind the Chefman Coffee Station Pro.

Unlike other x-in-one machines that incorporate a couple of different brewing methods (looking at you, Keurig K-Duo), the Chefman Coffee Station Pro combines 10 different functionalities into one machine. Craving a quick single cup? You can use it with both K-cups and Nespresso pods. Need to fill up a full carafe for a party? It does that, too. But what if you want to leisurely enjoy an iced latte or a shot of espresso on the weekend? Well, that’s also an option with the built-in manual espresso machine and automatic milk frother.

In terms of functionality, the Chefman Coffee Station Pro offers espresso, cappuccino, latte, single-serve coffee, 12-cup carafe, cold brew, cold-pressed espresso, coffee over ice, hot milk and cold foam. To try it out, I packed up all my other coffee makers and made it my primary coffee machine for two months.

You’d probably expect a machine with so many capabilities to be enormous, heavy, complicated, unwieldily. Not so with the Coffee Station Pro. It’s so simple to set up and use, I didn’t even need to read through the provided booklet before I was whipping up drinks. It also takes up barely more space than a dedicated espresso machine.

Right off the bat, I brewed a pot of drip coffee. First, I’m super impressed with the way the machine can read what I’m brewing based on the basket insert I choose. Pop in the drip basket, and it’s prompting me to choose the number of cups. Swap it out with the K-Cup pod holder, and it’s now asking how many ounces I’d like. It’s truly like magic!

Then, there’s the customization. First, pick between a single cup or a whole carafe. Then, choose to enjoy your coffee either hot or over ice. You also get the option of brewing a cup or a whole pot of cold brew for summertime get-togethers. There’s even a delay brew function, which makes it easy to prep your poison of choice the night before.

As I mentioned, the machine changes its touchscreen prompts based on which brew basket you choose. When you pop in a K-cup, you’re given the option of having it hot or over ice. There’s also a choice of 4-, 6-, 8-, 10- and 12-ounce cups. Then, you can adjust the strength and temperature, with three different settings for each. Finally, there’s a small flip-down platform to rest your cup on so that it doesn’t splash everywhere as it brews.

Want to use a Nespresso capsule instead? A pod insert pops into the K-cup adapter. Again, the machine will intuit this automatically and adjust accordingly. Overall, this aspect of the machine is incredibly intuitive. Plus, you can save specific settings as one of three “favorites,” if you want to speed things up with one-touch operation.

As for espressos, this thing does work. It boasts a 20-bar pressure system that brews espressos topped in a beautiful, silky crema. Pick from a single or double shot, or opt for a cappuccino or latte, both with a choice of single or double. If you choose a milk-based drink, ensure the milk reservoir is filled and angle the metal wand into your cup—the machine does the rest.

If you’re familiar with a standard manual espresso machine, you already know how to operate the portafilter. If not, no worries—there are helpful videos from Chefman to guide you through the process. You can also opt for hot milk, or warm or cold foam on its own by choosing the “froth” setting if you want to add any of those to your cup of regular coffee.

On the top of the machine next to the brew basket is an area with accessory storage for the portafilter baskets, Nespresso adapter and combination scoop/tamper. On the side of the coffee station is a place to stow the portafilter. While there’s no built-in storage for the brew basket, K-cup basket and carafe when they’re not in use, I do love that the smaller accessories have a dedicated space so they won’t be misplaced.

In terms of cleaning, this might be one of the easier machines I’ve ever used. All of the inserts simply rinse clean. But the part I really love is the milk frother. Instead of nooks and crannies that collect smells and residue, the entire thing comes apart into several pieces, so you can thoroughly rinse and wash every single part that touches milk. Win!

Chefman isn’t the only brand to claim that they’ve created an “all-in-one” solution for flexible baristas. In our research, we also found three other similar machines: The De’Longhi All-in-One, the Ninja Prestige Dual Brew Coffee Maker and, of course, the classic  Keurig K-Duo.

The only issue? We couldn’t find a single other appliance that offered K-Cup compatibility and Nespresso pod capability and a carafe and a manual espresso machine and automated milk frothing/latte settings. All of the options above offer at least two functions. Maybe four. But not all five. That crown belongs to the Chefman Coffee Station Pro alone.

Hopefully, we see more options pop up in the near future. But for now, the Chefman Coffee Station Pro is in its own category of coffee maker.

In my home, I used to have five different coffeemakers, including a Keurig, a Nespresso, an automatic espresso machine, a manual espresso maker and a classic drip coffeemaker. In fact, there were so many appliances that they began spilling into my dining room after I ran out of space on my dedicated coffee bar. After converting to the Chefman Coffee Station Pro, I’ve pared that number down to just one. One!

The only changes I’d make after using this machine since August 2026 are minor gripes. I do wish that there was a built-in coffee grinder, since having a separate bean grinder means having yet another appliance. And while I appreciate that there is a small storage cavity for tinier accessories, I don’t love having to find a place in my cupboard for the carafe and basket inserts when I’m not using them.

But honestly? Even with these small annoyances, I wouldn’t even knock off a full star on my final rating. The size, ease of use and cleanliness of the machine make it more than worth it—especially for flexible home baristas like me.

Condense five coffeemakers into just one with this ingenious appliance.

Want to clear up your counter space and join me in the future? You can snag this new appliance on the Chefman website or on Amazon. At $400, the Coffee Station Pro is absolutely an investment. And for some folks who won’t use all the different settings, it may not be worth the cost. But for coffee lovers who want to brew based on vibes (or who already have a long lineup of machines like I used to), the price point feels totally fair. After all, you’re getting five different machines in one!

The easier question is what can’t it make? It’s capable of espresso, single-serve coffee, a 12-cup carafe, cold brew and coffee over ice. While it’s not programmable to make specific drinks such as a cafe crema or Americano like some automatic espresso machines, the manual espresso component and automatic milk frother still offers extreme flexibility for budding baristas.

#6500. I Tried French Fries From 11 Fast Food Chains—And One Was Clearly The Best

I firmly believe that French fries can make or break a fast food meal. You could have the best burger or chicken sandwich in the world, but if the fries disappoint, the whole experience takes a hit. And with so many fast food chains making fries a menu staple, you'd think they'd all have perfected them by now.

But not all French fries are created equal. Some are perfectly crispy and salty, others rely on bold seasonings to stand out, and a few are better off being covered in dipping sauce. Personally, I'm looking for fries that can stand on their own without ketchup or ranch to make them interesting. A good fry should have a crispy exterior, a fluffy potato interior, and enough seasoning to keep me reaching for another.

So I tried fries from 11 popular fast food chains, including McDonald's, Wendy's, Popeyes, and Burger King, to see which ones actually deliver. I judged each based on flavor, texture, seasoning, and overall enjoyment.

Here's how they ranked, from my least favorite to the one I'll never get tired of ordering.

I love a good crinkle-cut fry, but Raising Cane's left me seriously disappointed. The biggest issue was the texture. Rather than having a satisfying crunch on the outside, these fries were soft and limp.

The saltiness didn't help matters, either. They were overly salty, but without the crispy exterior or distinctive potato flavor I was hoping for, the seasoning didn't do much to save them.

Raising Cane's may be known for its chicken fingers and signature sauce, but based on this tasting, the fries aren't the reason I'd go back.

As much as I love Shake Shack, I was surprised by how little I enjoyed its fries.

They had a great texture, but the biggest problem was the salt. These were seasoned so heavily that they bordered on unpleasant. Instead of bringing out the potatoes' natural flavor, the salt overwhelmed everything else.

I can appreciate a generously salted fry, but there's a difference between enhancing the flavor and completely taking over. Unfortunately, Shake Shack fell into the latter category.

Chick-fil-A's waffle fries have one major advantage over the competition: their shape. The thick, waffle-cut potatoes deliver a substantial potato flavor, which I appreciated.

But as much as I enjoyed that aspect, the fries themselves weren't particularly exciting. They felt more like a way to scoop up Chick-fil-A's signature sauces than something I'd be especially eager to eat on their own.

There's nothing wrong with a fry that's great for dipping, but I wanted more flavor when eating these without sauce.

Sonic's Groovy Fries are pretty much the definition of a standard fast food French fry.

I enjoyed the crinkle-cut shape, which makes them a little more interesting than a traditional straight-cut fry. Beyond that, the flavor wasn't particularly memorable.

They're perfectly serviceable, and I certainly wouldn't complain about having them alongside a burger. But compared with some of the more flavorful fries in this ranking, Sonic didn't do quite enough to stand out.

Five Guys has a pretty distinctive flavor profile when it comes to its fries.

Because the chain cooks its fries in peanut oil, they have a noticeable peanut-oil character. That sets them apart from the competition, though I wanted a little more emphasis on the potato itself.

I did appreciate that the fries are served with the skin still on. It adds texture and a more pronounced earthy potato flavor, making them feel a little different from the more uniform fries offered by other chains.

There's plenty to like here, but the peanut-oil flavor stuck with me most.

When it comes to seasoning, Popeyes might actually have the most flavorful fries of the entire bunch.

The Cajun seasoning delivers a bold, peppery, savory flavor that immediately sets these fries apart from their competitors. There's so much more going on here than just salt, and I appreciated how much personality the seasoning brought to each bite.

Unfortunately, the texture held them back.

These fries were noticeably greasy, which made them feel heavier than I'd like. And while the seasoning was delicious, the excess oil started to take away from the experience.

Wendy's earns some serious points for letting the potato actually taste like a potato.

What I particularly loved was the skin-on preparation, which gave the fries a more pronounced, earthy potato flavor, similar to Five Guys. The skin also added texture, making each bite feel more substantial than some of the simpler fries on this list.

Sometimes you don't need a complicated seasoning blend to make a good French fry. Wendy's proves that a strong potato flavor can go a long way.

Taco Bell's Nacho Fries have always stood out from traditional fast food fries, and after trying them alongside the competition, I was reminded just how good that seasoning is.

The flavor is distinctly cheesy and savory, with a seasoning blend that makes every bite interesting. Compared with standard salted fries, these bring something completely different to the table.

My only real complaint was the texture. I wanted a little more crunch to balance out all that seasoning, especially because the flavor itself was so satisfying.

The first thing I noticed was how much larger the fries were than I expected. But what really impressed me was their combination of crispiness and potato flavor.

The exterior delivered a satisfying crunch, while the inside retained a strong, recognizable potato taste. It was exactly the kind of balance I was looking for throughout this taste test.

I went into this ranking with low expectations for Burger King's fries, but they ended up being one of the most enjoyable surprises.

KFC might not be the first chain that comes to mind when you're craving French fries, but after this tasting, I think it deserves a little more credit.

These fries had a deep, almost kettle-cooked flavor that immediately set them apart. I really enjoyed the rich potato flavor, which gave them more character than many of the other options.

The fries were wonderfully crispy, with a satisfying crunch that made them incredibly easy to keep eating. KFC delivered one of the best balances of flavor and texture in the entire lineup, and I wouldn't hesitate to order these again.

McDonald's fries have been the standard I've compared every other fast food fry to for years, and after tasting 11 different chains, they're still my favorite.

What makes McDonald's so good is how consistently they deliver the experience I want from a French fry. The flavor isn't overly complicated, but the balance of potato, salt, and that familiar fried taste just works.

While some chains lean heavily on seasoning and others struggle with texture, McDonald's gets the fundamentals right. The fries are satisfying on their own, and I can eat an entire order without getting bored.

I know what I'm getting when I order them, and that's exactly what I want.

After trying all 11 chains, I still haven't found a fast food fry that makes me want to abandon the Golden Arches.

Sometimes the obvious choice really is the best one.

#6498. I Tried the New AirDoctor 1500 Tower—And It’s the Perfect Size for Eliminating Kitchen Odors

Our editors and experts handpick every product we feature. We may earn a commission from your purchases.Learn more.

It happens to the best of us. You’re minding your own business as dessert bubbles in the oven or dinner sears on the stove, then—bam! The smell of burning reaches your nose, and before you know it, you’re dragging out a stepladder to silence the blaring smoke alarm.

I hate to say that this is a fairly frequent scenario in my kitchen. While I do my best to prevent pans of lasagna from overflowing and exercise caution when searing steak, accidents still occur. The only difference is that I now have a solution to clear the lingering smell from my space.

I’ve been testing the AirDoctor 1500 air purifier for two months, and it’s a portable yet powerful option for eliminating pesky kitchen odors. Here’s everything you need to know.

Banish bad odors from your kitchen with this mini-yet-mighty air purifier.

The AirDoctor 1500 is a high-performance air purifier packed into a sleek, compact package. Unlike other air purifiers that can take up as much space as a dehumidifier, the 1500 measures less than 10 inches wide and 21 inches high. It features three filters: a pre-filter for trapping larger particles like pet hair, a carbon and VOC filter for filtering odors and an UltraHEPA for capturing everything else. It’s best suited for small-to-medium spaces up to 320 square feet and comes in black or white.

I’ve been testing the AirDoctor 1500 air purifier since its launch in September 2026. I’ve used it in my kitchen, entryway, office and family room.

The AirDoctor arrived on my doorstep in a sturdy brown box. It was well-packaged to prevent damage during shipping, and setup couldn’t be easier. All you have to do is lift the unit out of the box, remove the plastic bag, peel off a few stickers and, most importantly, set up the filter.

The filter housing snaps off easily, and installation is as simple as removing the filter from its packaging and popping it back in. In all the air purifiers I’ve tested, AirDoctor has the most seamless filter access, thanks to built-in magnets in the housing. This is important not only for changing the filter but also for cleaning it.

After completing setup, I plugged in the AirDoctor and turned it on. It immediately whirred to life, starting at high speed with a red light and working its way down to a low-speed setting. The control panel is reactive to a simple touch, with options for speed (auto, one, two, three or four), ion, dim and timer. The interface also includes a light that signals when the filter needs to be checked or replaced.

As the air purifier got to work, I was impressed with how little sound it produced. On level one, you can barely hear it. It’s a slightly audible white noise that blends easily into the background. Level four is obviously the loudest, similar to a fan on high speed. If you’re very sensitive to noise, you might find any air purifier, including the AirDoctor, too loud to sleep with. But in a kitchen, the whir blends right into the din of cooking, a running dishwasher and other ambient sounds.

Within a week of the AirDoctor being in my space, it faced its first real challenge. I had a potpie galette in a 425-degree oven, and some of the filling started to leak onto the pizza steel it was baking on and burn. Within minutes, my oven filled with smoke. I managed to get the galette out without setting off the alarms, but my space was filled with an unpleasant, burning-vegetable odor.

I turned on the AirDoctor, set it to auto, and it immediately kicked into high gear. The light turned red—a sign that it senses poor air quality—and the fan worked overtime to cleanse the air. After about 15 minutes, the light turned yellow—moderate air quality—and I could already sense a difference. Within an hour, it was back to a blue light, which signifies good air quality, and it’s like the accident never happened.

Over the next few weeks, the AirDoctor helped clear the air after I burnt some toast in the toaster and cooked cruciferous vegetables. It also helps combat day-to-day odors, like a full trash can or a garbage disposal ready for a scrubdown.

What I like best is that the AirDoctor isn’t just masking these scents; it’s actually removing them from the air. As an allergy sufferer, I’ve also noticed fewer symptoms (especially sneezing) when the air purifier is running. Thanks to the 1500’s portable size, it’s easy to tote it around my downstairs living area, too.

The AirDoctor’s filters are designed to last—but the exact lifespan depends on many factors. How long you run the air purifier each day, for starters. Along with what kind of baseline air quality is in your home. Pets, for example, are an obvious culprit for added dander and hair. Luckily, the 1500 includes a built-in sensor light that tells you when it’s time for a refresh.

You can also pop off the housing and give the filter a good vacuum. I like to do this once a month or so, but again, buildup may be more or less severe depending on your home’s air quality conditions.

I’ve had the opportunity to also test the AirDoctor 1500’s larger cousin, the AirDoctor 3500. But how do these models compare?

Size is the most obvious difference. The 1500 is six inches slimmer than the 3500 and weighs roughly half as much. I find that the 1500 blends more seamlessly into my space than the 3500, taking up less footprint. The round tower design is also very easy to slip into a corner.

That said, the 1500 covers rooms up to 320 sq. ft., while the 3500 covers up to 630 sq. ft. This makes the 1500 a great option for kitchens, offices, laundry rooms and small bedrooms, while the 3500 is a better choice for large living rooms and open-concept homes.

Beyond that, performance is very similar. Both models are quiet and easy to operate. They use a 3-stage filtration system, including an UltraHEPA. They’re both high-quality machines that are built to last. And that quality does come at a premium. The 1500 retails for $399, while the 3500 costs $659.

My spouse likes to joke that I’m like a basset hound given how sensitive I am to smells. That’s why the AirDoctor 1500 is an obvious choice in our kitchen. It efficiently rids our space of odors from cooking, pungent foods and even the occasional smoke-induced accident. Not only is this machine reducing odors that would otherwise linger for days, but it’s also cleansing our air of other contaminants.

This way to cleaner air! I love this air purifier's slim footprint, low noise level and triple filter design.

The AirDoctor 1500 air purifier is available from the brand’s website or Amazon. It retails for $399.
