#6496. A Mineral Fingerprint Taken From Stonehenge Just Deepened the Mystery of Its Ancient Origins

Ice couldn’t have carried the six-tonne slab hundreds of miles. Ancient people had to—and we don't know why.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

Before anyone could set Stonehenge’s Altar Stone in place, someone had to get it to southern England. According to geological research, the six-tonne sandstone slab came from northeast Scotland—a journey of hundreds of miles with a cargo that couldn’t be replaced if it were lost.

“My view is that the evidence strongly favours transport by Neolithic people, most plausibly involving a maritime journey,” Chris Kirkland, a professor of geology at Curtin University in Australia, tells Popular Mechanics. Kirkland helped trace the stone to Scotland in a 2024 study that overturned the long-held belief that it came from Wales. At the time, we looked at how a six-tonne slab might have traveled that far, possibly by sea. Since then, two studies—one of them Kirkland’s—have tested whether glaciers could have spared people some of the hauling. Kirkland’s reading is that they probably didn’t, which leaves archaeologists with a harder question than how the stone was moved. “Transporting a six-tonne stone across Britain would have demanded considerable organisation, cooperation and communication between distant communities,” he says. Were people coming together just to move this stone, or could they already count on help from communities far from home?

The Altar Stone is made of sand eroded from older rocks, and the mineral grains in that sand record where it came from. Researchers date some of the grains by measuring how much of their uranium has decayed into lead, then look for sandstone elsewhere with the same pattern of ages. “The mineral fingerprint points to northeast Scotland, specifically the Old Red Sandstone of the Orcadian Basin,” Kirkland says—roughly 700 kilometers (435 miles) from Stonehenge in a straight line, and farther by any real route. Later work narrowed the closest match to Caithness, around Sarclet, though the exact outcrop the slab was cut from hasn’t yet been found.

The ice would still leave people with a job

Two studies published this year tested whether glaciers could have done part of the work. In Communications Earth & Environment, Kirkland and Anthony Clarke examined mineral grains in modern stream sediments around Stonehenge and found no trace of the distant material that glacial delivery would have left behind. A separate study in the Journal of Quaternary Science modeled ice flow from Scotland and found that ice could at most have carried the stone toward Dogger Bank, now beneath the North Sea—not to Salisbury Plain.

Kirkland cautions that a possible glacial path “is not evidence that the stone actually followed it.” Around the Caithness rocks that best match the Altar Stone, glaciers mostly carried material north and east, away from southern Britain. And even if ice had delivered the slab to Dogger Bank, it would have had to arrive in one piece, and people would then have had to retrieve it before rising seas submerged the area—with hundreds of kilometers still to go. Too much would have to go right, Kirkland says. He can’t rule out some help from ice, but he thinks people brought the stone from Scotland themselves.

The cargo needed people who could work together

Kirkland favors a sea route because of what an overland one would involve: hauling six tonnes of stone along rivers and bogs over nearly the entire length of Britain. A sea route would bypass many of those barriers, though such a voyage carried risks of its own, and a combination—using boats for part of the trip, hauling overland for the rest—is possible. No archaeological evidence yet identifies the route or the vessels.

Whatever the route, the harder problem is the people. Did a single group stay with the Altar Stone all the way to Stonehenge, or did it pass from one community to the next, each knowing where it was headed and why it mattered? If the journey stretched over generations, then its destination and its purpose had to be handed down along with the stone, Kirkland says. Archaeologists already have evidence of long-distance connections across Neolithic Britain; the Altar Stone would show what those connections could accomplish. Pinpointing the outcrop it came from would give them a starting point for reconstructing how.

Some of the Altar Stone’s grains were already billions of years old when they settled into the sandstone, Kirkland says. That history is written in the rock. The far more recent story of the people who moved it is harder to recover. If they brought it from Scotland, they took on an enormous amount of work for something they could have left where it was. Finding out how they did it would be an achievement. Finding out why they thought it worth doing would tell us even more.

#6495. 12 Dishes and Drinks Everyone Should Try in Mexico City

Mexico City is a colossal, beautifully overwhelming metropolis and an absolute paradise for those who live to eat. Across thousands of restaurants, markets, street stalls, bakeries and fondas, food from every corner of the country converges. So deciding where (and what) to eat can feel daunting.

I’ve spent over a decade writing about Mexico’s food scene, along with popping up on TV shows to talk about it occasionally. My favorite way to navigate it all is still right here on the ground with my food tours. Sidestepping the obvious spots and encouraging travelers to go deeper into the food culture here constantly reminds me of the fascinating nature of how we chilangos eat.

To me, that means having the flexibility to move between completely different culinary worlds. We can spend lunchtime standing on a crowded sidewalk, leaning over a taco drenched in salsa and lime juice, then dress up that same evening for a multicourse dinner at a dimly lit restaurant. We’re fiercely loyal to the neighborhood places that have fed us for years and know our names, but we remain insatiably curious about the next opening or dish worth crossing town for.

That same flexibility shapes the city’s food. CDMX knows exactly how to pull tradition into modernity without losing its spirit. These 12 spots offer a taste of the city’s versatility: dishes and bites deeply rooted in history but shaped by the wonderfully chaotic, ever-evolving way we eat today.

Adolfo Prieto 1364 B, Tlacoquemecatl del Valle, Benito Juárez +52 55 5559 6358

I remember being dragged out of bed early on a weekend as a teenager to have breakfast here. Although I was still drowsy, the smell of the wood-fired stoves, the café de olla (traditional “pot coffee”) bubbling away, and sounds of the older musicians playing classic tunes on their guitars were enough to bring me around. I was fully awake by the time a plate of refried beans with scrambled eggs and a stack of warm tortillas made it to the table. It’s the kind of humble combination you’d find in almost any Mexican home. At Fonda Margarita, these eggs, which are cooked over a wood fire and enriched with lard, take on a different life. Getting here early is a requirement, as they open around 6:30 a.m. and by 10 a.m., all the food is gone.

C. Chilpancingo 35, Colonia Condesa, +52 55 5264 4976

Few breakfast menus are complete without chilaquiles. What started as a way to use up day-old tortillas is now one of the country’s quintessential breakfasts: triangles of tortillas, deep-fried until crisp, then bathed in salsa. At Chilpa in leafy Condesa, several salsas are available, from a red, tomato-based version to the bright, tangy tomatillo number, all with different spice levels. Pile on your choice of cecina, shredded chicken, or fried eggs. Crema, grated cheese, and chopped onion are standard, making it easy to order a different version every visit.

If you asked my Mexican grandma what breakfast looks like, she’d say coffee and a pan dulce—more often than not that would mean a concha. The quintessential Mexican pastry makes an appearance in virtually every bakery in town, from the most traditional versions made with a combination of lard and shortening to the tender butter-forward renditions found in most modern bakeries. At Panadería Gala, a speakeasy-style bakery, the extra-bouncy conchas get an extra layer of indulgence when topped with a white or dark chocolate ganache quenelle.

The Margarita may be known worldwide as the quintessential Mexican cocktail. Legend has it that the iconic drink was first conceived in Ensenada, Baja California, and its ties to coast and borderlands are still strong. But chilangos tend to save it for specific occasions and places such as a beach vacation, a favorite cantina, or a particular restaurant where we know they’ll make a killer one. At Despacho Margarita, the classic shaken version gets a new life, alongside spicy and frozen variations. In the slushy machine there are usually two flavors: a classic tequila and lime version and a seasonal mezcal-based one that changes monthly, with unique flavors such as nopal and shiso or raspberry and cayenne pepper.

Cantinas have long been places to gather over good food and several rounds of drinks. In these laid-back establishments, both are given equal importance. At Covadonga, a cantina without background music, generations mix at the tables, from the young and cool to sixty-somethings who come to play dominoes (available on request).

Banderas have been a popular order here from the beginning: three shot glasses lined up, one with tequila, another with lime juice, and a third with sangrita, a red-hued blend of tomato and fruit juices. Together they mimic the three colors of the Mexican flag (hence the name). They’re not meant to be knocked back but rather sipped slowly, alternating between glasses.

There’s a certain type of stand you’ll see repeatedly throughout the city centered around a large, round comal, usually fired with charcoal, with a bucket of corn masa and, more often than not, two women working side by side. Gorditas, quesadillas, and sopes are all on the menu, but the tlacoyo may be the most emblematic of central Mexico and Mexico City.

Football-shaped and stuffed with your choice of fava bean purée, requesón (a soft cheese similar to ricotta), or refried beans, tlacoyos are typically topped with cactus salad for a tangy contrast, grated queso fresco, and red or green salsa, depending on your preference. They're the kind of morning or afternoon snack you might grab during errands or before wandering into the adjacent market.

Petén 248 y, Avenida Universidad, Narvarte Poniente, Benito Juárez+52 55 5536 3060

The idea of stacking meat on a rotating spit was brought to Puebla by Lebanese immigrants. Once it reached Mexico City, it evolved into one of the city’s great late-night bites: the taco al pastor. At El Vilsito, a garage by day that moonlights as one of the city’s best taquerías, they take the pastor one step further, turning it into the most Chilango version possible by making it into a torta. A warm, lightly toasted telera gets a smear of refried beans, avocado, onion, tomato, and freshly shaved pastor, all finished with melted cheese.

Calle Versalles 113, Casa Versalles, Juárez+52 55 8568 7397

You can’t talk about Mexico City without talking about its obsession with seafood. It may seem strange for a city far from the coast, but seafood from both of Mexico’s coastlines makes its way here through the second-largest seafood market in the world (surpassed only by Tokyo’s Toyosu) before being distributed across the rest of the country.

At Taller de Ostiones, clams, barnacles, and tuna arrive three times a week by air from Baja California Sur, alongside sustainably farmed oysters. Those bivalves pair perfectly with a house-made sauce that’s rich, briny, deeply umami, and slightly spicy.

Mexico City runs on fondas and street food. These casual eateries serve homestyle food that’s quick, affordable, and feels like a warm hug. Stepping inside a fonda means being a part of a daily ritual known as comida corrida, where a meal typically unfolds in four acts: a steaming bowl of soup or broth to start, followed by your choice of rice or pasta. Next comes the main course, which at some places, like Nuevo Tehuacán, is what dictates the price of the meal; meatballs in chipotle, pork with tomatillo and purslane, and chicken in peanut sauce are classics. Everything is washed down with a pitcher of freshly made agua fresca and finished with a sweet treat like a cup of gelatin. Look around, and you’ll see schoolchildren sitting elbow to elbow with office workers all savoring the comfort of one of the city’s most democratic meals.

Mostly homemade, flan is a staple of casual Mexican cooking, but it’s one of those desserts that look deceptively simple. Getting the texture just right (silky, firm, not overly sweet) is way harder than it seems, which may be why the best versions are often found in homes. Siembra may be a temple dedicated to heirloom corn masa, tacos, and antojitos, but its flan has all the right qualifications to receive just as much attention.

Fernando Montes de Oca 89, Cuauhtémoc, Colonia Condesa+52 55 5286 1258

Ice cream parlors used to be an almost daily afternoon ritual for families and couples alike. Nevería Roxy was one of the early ones, and its 1950s-diner palate of teal and red has barely changed, even in the newer locations. Alongside the classic milk- and cream-based flavors, sundaes, floats, and banana splits, you’ll find nieves.

The word literally translates to “snow,” and the closest comparison might be a sorbet, though the texture tends to be a little coarser and the flavors typically center around seasonal fruits. Mamey, an endemic fruit, may have a short season, but Roxy keeps its nieve de mamey year-round. With a faintly nutty flavor reminiscent of baked sweet potato, it’s become a local favorite. And thanks to mamey’s lush, natural creaminess, it’s easy to forget there’s no dairy in the cup.

Avenida 5 de Mayo 39, Centro Histórico de la Ciudad+52 55 5521 1787

Stepping into Dulcería de Celaya feels more like entering a 19th-century jewelry store than a candy shop. Founded in 1874, this Centro Histórico institution still retains its original art nouveau interior, complete with French mirrors, tiled floors, and hand-carved oak cabinets, all filled with traditional sweets made in-house. Among the colorful sweet treats are candied lime skins stuffed with shredded sweetened coconut. The peel is candied and tender and, unlike fresh lime rind, is not bitter, so you can go ahead and bite into the whole thing at once.

Recommendations on where to dine, shop, and stay, wherever you’re headed next.

Seek out tlacoyos, chilaquiles, and nieves in this culinary capital.

Mexico City is a colossal, beautifully overwhelming metropolis and an absolute paradise for those who live to eat. Across thousands of restaurants, markets, street stalls, bakeries and fondas, food from every corner of the country converges. So deciding where (and what) to eat can feel daunting.

I’ve spent over a decade writing about Mexico’s food scene, along with popping up on TV shows to talk about it occasionally. My favorite way to navigate it all is still right here on the ground with my food tours. Sidestepping the obvious spots and encouraging travelers to go deeper into the food culture here constantly reminds me of the fascinating nature of how we chilangos eat.

To me, that means having the flexibility to move between completely different culinary worlds. We can spend lunchtime standing on a crowded sidewalk, leaning over a taco drenched in salsa and lime juice, then dress up that same evening for a multicourse dinner at a dimly lit restaurant. We’re fiercely loyal to the neighborhood places that have fed us for years and know our names, but we remain insatiably curious about the next opening or dish worth crossing town for.

That same flexibility shapes the city’s food. CDMX knows exactly how to pull tradition into modernity without losing its spirit. These 12 spots offer a taste of the city’s versatility: dishes and bites deeply rooted in history but shaped by the wonderfully chaotic, ever-evolving way we eat today.

Adolfo Prieto 1364 B, Tlacoquemecatl del Valle, Benito Juárez +52 55 5559 6358

I remember being dragged out of bed early on a weekend as a teenager to have breakfast here. Although I was still drowsy, the smell of the wood-fired stoves, the café de olla (traditional “pot coffee”) bubbling away, and sounds of the older musicians playing classic tunes on their guitars were enough to bring me around. I was fully awake by the time a plate of refried beans with scrambled eggs and a stack of warm tortillas made it to the table. It’s the kind of humble combination you’d find in almost any Mexican home. At Fonda Margarita, these eggs, which are cooked over a wood fire and enriched with lard, take on a different life. Getting here early is a requirement, as they open around 6:30 a.m. and by 10 a.m., all the food is gone.

C. Chilpancingo 35, Colonia Condesa, +52 55 5264 4976

Few breakfast menus are complete without chilaquiles. What started as a way to use up day-old tortillas is now one of the country’s quintessential breakfasts: triangles of tortillas, deep-fried until crisp, then bathed in salsa. At Chilpa in leafy Condesa, several salsas are available, from a red, tomato-based version to the bright, tangy tomatillo number, all with different spice levels. Pile on your choice of cecina, shredded chicken, or fried eggs. Crema, grated cheese, and chopped onion are standard, making it easy to order a different version every visit.

If you asked my Mexican grandma what breakfast looks like, she’d say coffee and a pan dulce—more often than not that would mean a concha. The quintessential Mexican pastry makes an appearance in virtually every bakery in town, from the most traditional versions made with a combination of lard and shortening to the tender butter-forward renditions found in most modern bakeries. At Panadería Gala, a speakeasy-style bakery, the extra-bouncy conchas get an extra layer of indulgence when topped with a white or dark chocolate ganache quenelle.

The Margarita may be known worldwide as the quintessential Mexican cocktail. Legend has it that the iconic drink was first conceived in Ensenada, Baja California, and its ties to coast and borderlands are still strong. But chilangos tend to save it for specific occasions and places such as a beach vacation, a favorite cantina, or a particular restaurant where we know they’ll make a killer one. At Despacho Margarita, the classic shaken version gets a new life, alongside spicy and frozen variations. In the slushy machine there are usually two flavors: a classic tequila and lime version and a seasonal mezcal-based one that changes monthly, with unique flavors such as nopal and shiso or raspberry and cayenne pepper.

#6494. Copycat Coopers Hawk Red Wine–Braised Short Ribs

This copycat Cooper's Hawk red wine-braised short ribs recipe delivers fall-apart-tender short ribs bathed in a luxurious sauce. It's like dining out in your kitchen.

One taste of the red wine–braised short ribs at Cooper’s Hawk is enough to understand why they’ve remained a bestseller. A generous portion of melt-in-your-mouth braised short ribs is stacked over a mound of buttery whipped potatoes, then drizzled with umami-rich braising liquid. When served with roasted vegetables and garnished with crispy fried onion strings, it’s a plate of comfort food that’s just as much a feast for the eyes as it is for the taste buds.

Whether you’ve been to Cooper’s Hawk and want to recreate the magic at home, or you don’t live near a Cooper’s Hawk location and want to know what all the fuss is about, this copycat recipe belongs on your must-try list. It’s the perfect recipe for chilly days when you want a meal that feels like a warm hug. It’s also impressive-looking and great for feeding a crowd, so keep it in mind as the holiday season approaches.

Cooper’s Hawk Winery and Restaurant is something like a more refined Cheesecake Factory that’s known for its wine rather than its cheesecake. Let me break it down. First, each chain greets diners by showcasing its specialties: Where the Cheesecake Factory has cases of cheesecake, Cooper’s Hawk has a well-appointed wine shop. Second, both are known for extensive menus that span a wide range of global influences and cuisines. Finally, both have a long list of signature dishes (we’ve replicated many Cheesecake Factory copycat recipes over the years) that keep customers coming back. These copycat Cooper’s Hawk red wine–braised short ribs, for example, are an ode to the dish that’s been on the menu since the restaurant opened its very first location in 2005.

Preheat the oven to 425°F. Sprinkle the short ribs with salt and pepper. In an ovenproof Dutch oven over medium-high heat, brown the ribs in oil in batches. Remove the meat and set it aside.

Add the celery, carrot and onion to the drippings, and cook them until they’re tender. Add the garlic, and cook for one minute longer. Add the wine and broth, stirring to loosen the browned bits from the pan.

Return the ribs to the pot. Add the parsley and thyme. Cover the pot and transfer it to the preheated oven. Cook the ribs for 2 hours to 2 hours and 30 minutes or until the meat is tender.

While the ribs are braising, in a small bowl, whisk all the butter ingredients until they’re blended. Transfer the mixture to a sheet of storage wrap, and roll it into a log. Refrigerate the butter until it’s chilled. Unwrap and slice it.

Place the hot mashed potatoes on a serving plate. Top them with the short ribs. Immediately top the ribs with a slice of Dijon butter and french-fried onions. Garnish the dish with additional fresh parsley, if desired.

Store any leftover short ribs in an airtight container in the refrigerator. Enjoy them within four days. Leftovers can be microwaved in a microwave-safe container, or they can be transferred to a Dutch oven and warmed in a 350° oven until they’re heated through.

Braised short ribs freeze like a dream. Store them in a freezer-safe airtight container and freeze them for three to four months. Defrost them completely in the refrigerator before warming them in the microwave or in the oven.

Short ribs are a heavily marbled cut of beef from the lower portion of the rib cage (the same area as the chuck and brisket). They have a robust, beefy flavor but contain a lot of connective tissue, so you need to cook them slowly with low heat for the meat to become tender.

Braising is one of the best (and most popular) ways to prepare short ribs. That said, you can make bone-in short ribs in a slow cooker or on a wood, pellet or charcoal smoker. To speed up the process, you can also make tender bone-in short ribs in a pressure cooker.

Cooper’s Hawk serves their short ribs with mashed potatoes and roasted vegetables, but that is far from the only pairing option. Serve them over a bowl of creamy polenta or on a bed of buttered egg noodles (similar to beef Stroganoff). For freshness, add a garden salad, grilled asparagus or creamed spinach for a more indulgent spin.

Sure! If you want to recreate Cooper’s Hawk braised short rib risotto, prepare this recipe as directed, minus the potatoes, veggies, and onion strings. Instead, saute a blend of mushrooms in a skillet and prepare this parmesan risotto while the short ribs braise in the oven. When everything is ready, shred the short ribs into small pieces and combine them with the mushrooms and risotto. Serve the risotto in shallow bowls with a few spoonfuls of braising liquid and a drizzle of white truffle oil.

#6493. Cranberry and Brie Stuffed Chicken

With its savory spinach filling, fruity sauce and gooey cheese topping, this cranberry Brie chicken makes any day a celebration.

Who doesn’t love a recipe that looks like it took hours in the kitchen, but is actually surprisingly easy to pull off? Our Test Kitchen’s cranberry Brie chicken is one of those impressive dinners. Tender chicken breasts are stuffed with a spinach filling, coated in bread crumbs and baked until golden. Then comes the best part: the cranberry sauce topping and slices of creamy Brie that melt right over the top.

With its festive colors and a blend of sweet and savory flavors, this dish is a great choice for Friendsgiving, Christmas Eve dinner or any occasion you want to impress guests. But don’t save it just for company! It’s also a fun way to dress up chicken breasts during the week.

Preheat the oven to 375°F. In a large skillet, cook the onion in oil until it’s almost tender. Add the spinach and garlic, and cook them until the spinach is wilted. Drain any liquid, if necessary. Season the filling with salt and pepper.

Pound the chicken breasts with a meat mallet to 1/4-inch thickness. Divide the spinach mixture among the pieces. Fold the chicken breasts in half, enclosing the filling, and secure them with toothpicks.

Place the bread crumbs and egg in separate shallow bowls. Dip the stuffed chicken breasts into the egg, then roll them in the crumbs to coat them. Place them seam side down in an 8-inch square baking dish coated with cooking spray. Bake the chicken, uncovered, for 25 minutes.

Spoon the cranberry sauce over the stuffed chicken breasts. Top them with slices of Brie (if desired, remove the rind from the Brie). Bake the chicken, uncovered, for 10 to 15 minutes longer or until a thermometer inserted into the thickest part reads 165°. Discard the toothpicks before serving the chicken. If desired, garnish it with rosemary sprigs.

Let leftover cranberry Brie chicken cool slightly, then transfer it to an airtight container and refrigerate for up to four days. The breading will soften as it sits, especially under the cranberry sauce and melted Brie, but it’ll still be tasty once reheated.

Cranberry Brie chicken tastes best fresh out of the oven, but you can absolutely get a head start on prepping the recipe. Make the spinach filling, and stuff the chicken breasts, securing them with toothpicks. Cover and refrigerate both for up to 24 hours.

When you’re ready to make dinner, dip the chicken in the egg, coat it with bread crumbs and bake it as directed. Save the cranberry sauce and Brie for the last 10 to 15 minutes of baking. Waiting to coat the chicken until just before baking helps keep the bread crumbs from getting soggy.

Reheat cranberry Brie chicken, loosely covered with foil, in a 350° oven until it’s warmed through to 165°. The microwave also works for single servings, but the breading will be a little soggier.

We love Brie here because its mild flavor and creamy texture pair so well with the tart cranberry sauce, but it’s not your only option. Camembert has a similar creamy texture and earthy flavor, or you could use goat cheese if you want something bolder and tangier. For a milder, kid-friendly option, try mozzarella or fontina. Both melt beautifully into a gooey, cheesy topping.

Boneless skinless chicken thighs can work in this recipe, but their smaller size makes them a little trickier to pound thin and fold around the spinach filling. If possible, choose larger thighs, and use toothpicks to keep the filling tucked inside. Since thighs vary in size and thickness, you may need to adjust baking time. Use a meat thermometer to make sure the chicken reaches an internal temperature of 165° before serving it.

Cranberry Brie chicken fits right in with holiday favorites like creamy mashed potatoes, roasted Brussels sprouts, stuffing or green bean casserole. For a more casual weeknight dinner, serve it over hot, fluffy rice to soak up the extra cranberry sauce, or pair it with roasted sweet potatoes or a simple green salad. And you can’t go wrong with a side of crusty bread, warm buttermilk biscuits or cornbread!

#6492. This Genius Trick Makes Perfectly Portioned Cinnamon Rolls—No Ruler or Guessing Required

This clever little move turns your knife into a measuring guide for cinnamon rolls.

I have no objection to a shortcut in the kitchen. I’m happy to let boxed cake mix handle dessert or a great jar of pasta sauce get dinner moving. But I just can’t make peace with cinnamon rolls from a can. I want to love them. I appreciate the convenience, and I understand the appeal of having warm cinnamon rolls before the coffee is finished brewing. They just never quite satisfy that craving for a warm, deliciously gooey cinnamon roll.

Some viral upgrades make a compelling case—particularly the heavy cream bath and generous additions of butter and brown sugar. But my long-standing love for cinnamon rolls has much more to do with the soft, pillowy dough, so I’m willing to wait through a rise or two.

Having committed to that, though, I’m still interested in anything that makes the process easier. So when I saw a video of someone flipping a knife over a log of cinnamon roll dough to mark perfectly even portions, I wanted to try it. Cutting equal slices by eye can involve a surprising amount of second-guessing when you’d really just like to get them in the pan. I made our classic cinnamon roll recipe and put the trick to the test.

The clever part of this trick is that the knife becomes its own measurement. Rather than judging the thickness of each slice, you use the width of the blade to mark equal portions along the dough roll before slicing it into pieces.

Once your dough is filled and rolled into a log, gently lay the blade flat across the top at one end, with the knife running crosswise over the log. Then flip it onto its opposite flat side, moving along the dough one blade-width at a time. It looks a little like the knife is doing small somersaults, with its edge leaving a light indentation at each turn.

Continue down the log, using just enough pressure to leave visible marks. Then go back and slice through the dough at those marks.

This worked beautifully with our classic cinnamon rolls, but the same technique would work with just about any cinnamon roll recipe. The turning motion was simple, and the knife left an evenly spaced cutting guide along the dough. Once the marks were there, I could slice the rolls without measuring each one or estimating where the next cut should go.

I particularly liked being able to see all the portions before I started cutting. It made the job feel settled: The spacing was already worked out, and all I had to do was follow it. For something that takes so little effort, it’s a useful bit of precision.

This is the kind of cinnamon roll shortcut I can get behind. I still get the homemade rolls I wanted, but there’s one less thing to fuss over before the pan goes into the oven.

#6491. The 10 Best Aldi Finds of November Include the Cutest Stocking Stuffer for Little Shoppers

From flavor mash-up snacks and holiday decor to a very special Barbie doll, these November Aldi Finds deserve a spot in your shopping cart.

November is one of my favorite months to go grocery shopping. First, no one can deny that it is peak soup season, so I love stocking up on ingredients for creamy butternut squash soup or a classic lentil stew. I never seem to mind that the sun sets earlier when I have a truly cozy lineup of fall dinner recipes.

And of course, Thanksgiving preparations take up a bulk of the month as well. In my family, I am tasked with two things: bread and Thanksgiving desserts. This year I am going to try my hand at making a sourdough pecan pie! Aldi is definitely the first place I go for my Thanksgiving shopping, because it’s guaranteed to have all my ingredients in stock. And, of course, there’s no shortage of seasonal goodies to look forward to.

Every month, Aldi drops a slew of new items and brings back some customer favorites. Since the grocery store is famous for its motto of “Here today, gone tomorrow,” I’ve turned my passion into an inside scoop so you can stock up while things last. Here are the most exciting November Aldi Finds that will make the holiday season even better.

Nothing says “welcome to the holidays” quite like a big wreath on the front door. This wreath has tiny pine cones and lights, which makes it even more magical. I love that it’s available with either a red or green velvet ribbon, so you can choose which suits your personal aesthetic best.

Of course, if you prefer savory treats over sweet ones, then you might really enjoy these turkey stuffing-flavored potato chips. All the classic Thanksgiving flavors you love, but in chip form. The wavy chip makes it perfect for dipping—say, in some homemade cranberry sauce?

This board is such a great idea! The snowman outline gives you plenty of opportunities to arrange your favorite meats and cheeses in a cute way. The snap-top lid ensures everything stays in place during transit from your house to wherever the party is. It’s so festive, I might just be giving these as gifts this year.

I’m a sucker for any kind of fancy bread, and this loaf in particular caught my eye. Imagine the pillowy sweet softness of a classic brioche, but with added maple nut flavor. It sounds like a breakfast dream come true! I’ll be using this for Thanksgiving leftover sandwiches and French toast.

If there’s one thing Aldi does well, it’s providing exceptionally budget-friendly dupes of much fancier brands. Like this adorable cherry red braiser pan, which has a leaf design on the lid. It might cost upward of $300 from some brands, but Aldi gives it to you at a mere fraction of the price.

OK, this is so cool! A special Aldi-branded Barbie doll is a perfect stocking stuffer for any of the grocery-obsessed kids (or adults!) in your life. This Barbie comes with her own shopping cart and reusable tote, so she is ready to stock up on all her favorite Aldi finds.

I have several holiday runners from Aldi, and they are some of my favorite pieces of decor. This beautiful floral version has big poinsettia flowers and holly leaves, so it’s sure to make a statement on your table for the holidays. It’s machine washable, too, which is very convenient—you don’t need to stress if someone spills red wine on it (speaking from experience!)

This has been my absolute favorite seasonal Aldi find over the last few years. Adding a few of these fancy flavored marshmallows to a simple cup of hot cocoa transforms it into an irresistible treat. I am partial to the peppermint flavor, but the salted caramel marshmallows are also incredible. And again, they are perfect for gifting!
